Catalogue report

Every entry, filed by its app group and then by species group — entries sharing a genus or a lookalike card, the ones one research session fills together. It opens with the groups that still have a draft, most drafts first. A habitat tag is followed by the words in that entry's own habitat prose that support it. Generated by Tools/catalogue_report.py; do not edit by hand.

Bermuda buttercup
Oxalis pes-caprae
bermuda-buttercup		shipped
careRequired		4Jun, Jul, Aug, Sep, Octcomplete
3 owed to a person
  • OWED, harvestEthics: a person's sentence. Facts to write from: Wikipedia says pulling up the plant, even with the roots, can leave some of the bulbs behind; Weeds Australia says hand removal after bulbil formation dislodges the bulbils and spreads them; Gardenista says it spreads faster when dug up. Weeds Australia lists New Zealand among the countries where it is a troublesome weed; NZPCN gives it as naturalised in 1883.
  • Habitat, filled 16/10/2026 (wood sorrel and clover audit; resolves the OWED habitat line of the 13/10/2026 page sweep): Plants of South Eastern New South Wales (Lucid), 'Oxalis pes-caprae', Where found, author Betty Wood, CC BY 4.0, https://apps.lucidcentral.org/plants_se_nsw/text/entities/oxalis_pes-caprae.htm, retrieved 16/10/2026 — first sentence verbatim; its Australian distribution ('Widespread. Not in Kosciuszko NP.') omitted. This is an Australian habitat statement, not a New Zealand one; no openly licensed New Zealand habitat text was found (Flora of New Zealand's Weed tab redirects to the Taxon profile, which has none). Tags from Tools/classify_habitats.py: forest (woodland), grassland (grassy areas), urban (gardens), disturbed (arable land, roadsides); 'along waterways' is not read as wetland by the classifier. The previous native-range sentence (Pacific Bulb Society) is range, not habitat, and was removed. Not used: Useful Temperate Plants' 'Roadsides and grassy places in S. Africa' (native range).
  • Edible parts, preparation and warning, 16/10/2026 (wood sorrel and clover audit; resolves the OWED warnings line of the 13/10/2026 page sweep): Useful Temperate Plants (Ken Fern), 'Oxalis pes-caprae', https://temperate.theferns.info/plant/Oxalis+pes-caprae, CC BY-NC-SA 3.0, retrieved 16/10/2026 — Edible Uses 'Leaves - raw or cooked' (cited there to Hedrick, Sturtevant's Edible Plants of the World; Crowe, Native Edible Plants of New Zealand; Kermath et al.), 'A pleasant acid flavour, the make a pleasant addition to mixed salads' and 'Use in moderation', 'Flowers - raw', 'A pleasant acid flavour, they make an attractive addition to the salad bowl' (the tasting notes are Ken Fern's own), 'Root - cooked' and 'The small bulb is sometimes eaten'; General Information 'The flowers, leaves and bulbs are all edible, being gathered from the wild and consumed locally.' Known Hazards (cited to Bown, Encyclopaedia of Herbs and their Uses) is word for word the text on Useful Tropical Plants 'Oxalis corniculata' and Useful Temperate Plants 'Oxalis magellanica', so the warning is the same sentence as on creeping-wood-sorrel, shamrock-wood-sorrel and snowdrop-woodsorrel: oxalic acid, all right in small quantities but not in large amounts since it can bind the body's calcium, less after cooking, especial caution for rheumatism, arthritis, gout, kidney stones or hyperacidity. It replaces the Wikipedia warning ('The leaves or roots are used in some parts of the world as a food, although the high levels of calcium oxalate in the plant may bear some risks. Such risks can be reduced if the plant parts are boiled first.'), from a Hazards section tagged as possibly original research and cited to a general vegetable-oxalate study; the two agree. Weeds Australia's 'mildly toxic to humans especially if eaten in large quantities' (cited above) agrees with it too. Wikipedia's runner sentence and the waterblommetjiebredie sentence are kept after the Useful Temperate Plants text. Not used: its cultivation, propagation and weed notes.

  • Least yellow sorrel · edible
  • forest ← Woodland
  • grassland ← grassy
  • wetland (not in its habitat prose)
  • urban ← gardens
  • disturbed ← roadsides
Creeping wood sorrel
Oxalis corniculata
creeping-wood-sorrel		shipped
careRequired		4year-round / unknownmonths
1 owed to a person
  • Edible parts, preparation and warning: Wikipedia, 'Oxalis corniculata', Uses section (revision 1368886676, 11/08/2026; CC BY-SA 4.0), cited there to Lee Allen Peterson, Edible Wild Plants (Houghton Mifflin, 1977), p. 104 — leaves edible with a tangy lemon taste; drink of leaves infused in hot water about 10 minutes, sweetened and chilled; 'Any woodsorrel is safe in low dosages, but if eaten in large quantities over a length of time can inhibit calcium absorption'. Useful Tropical Plants (Ken Fern), 'Oxalis corniculata', https://tropical.theferns.info/viewtropical.php?id=Oxalis+corniculata, CC BY-NC-SA 3.0, retrieved 15/10/2026 — leaves raw or cooked, flowers raw; added to salads, cooked as a potherb with milder greens or used to sour other foods; 'very small and fiddly to harvest'; Known Hazards (cited to Bown, Encyclopaedia of Herbs and their Uses): not to be eaten in large amounts since oxalic acid can bind the body's calcium, less after cooking, especial caution for rheumatism, arthritis, gout, kidney stones or hyperacidity. This replaces the OWED warnings line written when the recipes were added; those recipe pages' warnings agree with it. Not used from Useful Tropical Plants: medicinal uses, nutrient and oxalate percentages, cultivation (RHS/Flora Europaea dry habitats are not New Zealand).

Pale-pink wood sorrel
Oxalis incarnata
pale-pink-wood-sorrel		shipped
careRequired		4Mar, Apr, May, Sep, Oct, Novpreparation
5 owed to a person
  • OWED (16/10/2026), preparation and warnings (edible parts since written by the owner, 'Leafs, flowers and stems.', spelling regularised 16/10/2026) — no openly licensed source found that records this species as edible (Useful Temperate Plants has no record; Plants For A Future returned no entry; Wikipedia and Flora of New Zealand say nothing of use). The only species-specific source is Johanna Knox's Wild Picnic post above (all rights reserved, so not quoted), which records: raw leaves and stalks have a bright, sour taste and suit sweet and savoury dishes; leaves and flowers in a green salad, flowers as a garnish; it goes with root vegetables, fish, chicken and cream; used in place of lemon. Its caution: high oxalic acid levels, so not too much, and avoid it if you have gout, kidney trouble or rheumatoid arthritis and are avoiding high-oxalic-acid foods. Knox's A Forager's Treasury and Langlands' Foraging New Zealand (both owned, cited above) are the book routes. Not copied from creeping-wood-sorrel, whose text is about Oxalis corniculata. Web search 16/10/2026 (owner: "do a web search and find what you can") found no openly licensed text either; details in the next line.

That they look alike: Wild Food UK, 'Pink-Sorrel' (Oxalis articulata page; all rights reserved, facts only), https://www.wildfooduk.com/wild-plants/pink-sorrel/, retrieved 16/10/2026 — names Pale Pink-sorrel (O. incarnata) among the pink-flowered Oxalis it calls 'all too similar' to pink-sorrel. Petal lengths for O. incarnata outside New Zealand: Flora of Tasmania Online, Oxalidaceae (12–20 mm), https://flora.tmag.tas.gov.au/vascular-families/oxalidaceae/, and Plants of South Eastern New South Wales (10–22 mm, CC BY 4.0, cited above), retrieved 16/10/2026; the Tasmanian key also separates O. incarnata ('Stems well developed and leafy') from species with 'Stems absent or very short, never leafy'. Every test: Flora of New Zealand Online (Webb et al. 1988; CC BY 3.0 NZ), retrieved 16/10/2026: 'Oxalis incarnata', https://www.nzflora.info/factsheet/Taxon/Oxalis-incarnata.html; 'Oxalis articulata', https://www.nzflora.info/factsheet/Taxon/Oxalis-articulata.html. 'Almost hairless' is Flora's 'almost glabrous'. Ranges overlap: both are Exotic in New Zealand (Flora) and shamrock wood sorrel's habitat text (Plants of South Eastern NSW) names gardens, roadsides and other disturbed areas; Flora of Tasmania (cited above) gives O. articulata 'along roadsides, in waste places and some cultivated areas', and pale-pink wood sorrel's habitat text names roadsides and disturbed sites (the owner's own text since 16/10/2026; it was Plants of South Eastern NSW's when the card was written). Risk edible on cards opening pale-pink wood sorrel: its edibility is recorded only in all-rights-reserved, species-specific sources (Knox, Wild Picnic; Heartland Folk — see the OWED and web-search lines on that entry); Calflora's 'Do not eat' is a genus-wide flag. Owner to confirm. The owner has since written that page's edible parts in their own words ('Leaves, flowers and stems.'), which the risk matches; its preparation and warnings are still owed. Risk edible on the card opening shamrock wood sorrel: when the card was written its own page had no edible parts and the basis was Wild Food UK's O. articulata page (cited above), 'The leaves, stem and flowers are edible' — all rights reserved, species-specific. Since 16/10/2026 its page lists edible parts from Useful Tropical Plants 'Oxalis articulata' (CC BY-NC-SA 3.0, species-specific), which supports the risk.

Spinaches

Tetragonia
2 entries 1 owe text lookalike pairs 2/2
Entry State Photos Months Missing Lookalikes Habitats
Beach spinach
Tetragonia trigyna
beach-spinach		shipped
straightforward		4all yearpreparation
1 owed to a person
  • OWED, a person's sentence — warnings: Diego Bonetto, 'Native spinach' (all rights reserved; https://www.diegobonetto.com/blog/native-spinach), covering both T. tetragonioides and T. implexicoma, says the leaves contain oxalic acid, are not to be eaten raw in large quantities, and are blanched one to two minutes with the water discarded; people with a history of kidney stones are to eat them in moderation. No openly licensed source says this of T. trigyna, so the warning is not on the page. SEARCH, 10/10/2026: the open text on oxalates is all about T. tetragonioides — Wikipedia, 'Tetragonia tetragonioides' (CC BY-SA 4.0) says its medium to low oxalate levels are removed by blanching one minute and rinsing in cold water. Wikipedia's 'Tetragonia' genus article and 'Tetragonia implexicoma' say nothing on oxalates. The all-rights-reserved sources also describe T. tetragonioides, and disagree on the blanch: Warndu 10–15 seconds (https://warndu.com/blogs/first-nations-food-guide/get-your-warrigal-greens), ABC Organic Gardener about one minute, ANPSA three to four minutes with the water discarded, calling the oxalic acid high, and ASNSW about three minutes (https://anpsa.org.au/plant_profiles/tetragonia-tetragonioides/). Quoting a sentence about one species on another's page would be the inference this guide leaves to a person, so nothing was carried across. The owner searched as well on 10/10/2026 and found no warning for this species.
  • New Zealand spinach · edible
  • coastal ← beaches, coastal
  • forest ← woodland, forest
  • shrubland ← shrubland, hedges
  • grassland ← grassland, farmland

Black cherry
Prunus serotina
black-cherry		shipped
careRequired		4Feb, Marcomplete
3 owed to a person
  • OWED, harvestEthics: a person's sentence. Facts to write from: it is a National Pest Plant Accord species, so it must not be bred, distributed, released or sold anywhere in New Zealand; its seeds are spread by animals; Auckland Council's control advice treats the seeds as the part needing disposal, to green waste or landfill.
  • OWED (page sweep 13/10/2026), months: [2, 3] rest on Grow It Build It's 'late summer', read as the New Zealand season by the owner's ruling; no New Zealand record found shows ripe black fruit.
  • Prunus check 14/10/2026 (owner: "check every prune for correctness"): every field checked against curl'd sources by one pass and each fix re-verified by a second; deletions and weakenings only. 14/10/2026, owner's Prunus check (owner: "check every prune for correctness"): the OWED months line was corrected to say [2, 3] rest on Grow It Build It's season word 'late summer', not its northern calendar months 'mid to late August in zone 6', matching the months line above. 14/10/2026, owner's Prunus check (owner: "check every prune for correctness"): the Wikipedia line now names revision 1369959405, the revision live on 12/10/2026, because the 13/10/2026 edit (revision 1380007771) reworded two of the quoted summary sentences.

Japanese cherry
Prunus serrulata
japanese-cherry		draft
careRequired		4Aug, Sep, Oct, Deccomplete
2 owed to a person
  • OWED, harvestEthics: a person's sentence. Facts to write from: Auckland RPMP Sustained Control (no breeding, distribution, release, sale or planting in Auckland, three cultivars exempt); seeds are spread by birds and are the part Auckland Council asks to be disposed of.
  • OWED (recipes added 14/10/2026), warnings: a person's sentence on eating the blossoms. Facts to write from, both all rights reserved: Difford's (Sarah Watson) — 'It's advisable just to eat cherry blossoms in small amounts, particularly raw', cherry flowers, leaves, twigs and bark contain cyanogenic compounds that release cyanide when chewed or crushed, and 'the concentration reduces as flowers are processed by cooking, fully drying, etc.'; the flowers of cherry laurel (Prunus laurocerasus) 'are not treated as edible'. LearningHerbs (Vanessa Chakour) — avoid harvesting leaves with the flowers, because cyanogenic compounds 'can be concentrated in the leaves'. The page's only warning is Useful Temperate Plants' general cyanogenic-glycoside sentence.
  • Taiwan cherry · edible
  • coastal ← coast
  • forest (not in its habitat prose)
  • shrubland ← scrubland, shrubland
  • urban (not in its habitat prose)
  • disturbed ← roadside, wasteland

1 owed to a person
  • Months: December–February, from Environment Canterbury's New Zealand account (next line): 'Flowers appear in spring (September to November) and are followed by berries in summer.' The flowering agrees with the Flora's 'FL Sep-Nov.' Set 23/10/2026 (owner: 'also no months mentioned'); replaces the OWED note of 22/10/2026. The one wild iNaturalist New Zealand record with berries, obs 153604738 (Queenstown, 06/04/2023, photo -8), is later than the window: it shows the berries are still on the plant in April, not that they ripen then, so April is not added. Northern-hemisphere months (Missouri Botanical Garden's 'late summer' and 'ripen to blue-black by early fall', Oregon State University's 'mid-summer') are not converted.

Recipes474 recipes on 115 entries

Alexanders Smyrnium olusatrum 6 recipes

Alexanders soup

Ingredients
  • Four large flowering heads
  • an onion
  • a parsnip
  • a handful of wild garlic
  • a little dulse
Method
  1. Sweat the onion in oil, add the chopped alexanders and the rest, cover with stock and simmer about 10 minutes.
  2. Blend smooth and reheat to serve.

Steamed alexanders stalks

Method
  1. Take the leafy side stalks and the smaller branching ones.
  2. Steam for about 7 minutes, until tender, then toss with butter and season.

Candied alexanders stems

Makes roughly 120 g

Ingredients
  • 200 g young peeled stems
  • 100 g golden or brown granulated sugar
Method
  1. Wash, dry and finely chop the stems.
  2. Cook in a heavy-based pan over medium-high heat for about 8 minutes, until the liquid they give off has gone.
  3. Stir in the sugar and cook a further 10 minutes, until it crystallises around the pieces.
  4. Cool in the pan, then store in a dry jar — they keep about 6 months.

Alexander seeded bread

Ingredients
  • 1 heaped tbsp alexander seeds
  • 500 g white or wholemeal flour (or half of each)
  • a pinch of sea salt
  • 1 tsp quick yeast
  • 1 tsp sugar or honey
  • 400 ml warm water
  • 15 ml olive oil (optional)
Method
  1. Grind or chop the seeds roughly — you want texture, not powder; dry-roasting them at 140 °C for 10–20 minutes first makes them easier to grind, so long as they do not burn.
  2. Mix them through the flour and salt.
  3. Dissolve the yeast and honey in the warm water, stir into the dry mix and combine well.
  4. Knead about 10 minutes, until the dough springs back when pressed.
  5. Leave covered somewhere warm until doubled, knock it back, drop it into an oiled tin and prove again until doubled.
  6. Bake at 200 °C for 40–45 minutes, until the loaf sounds hollow tapped underneath.
  7. Rest 10 minutes in the tin, then cool on a rack before slicing.

Alexander and orange shortbread

Makes about 25

Ingredients
  • 100 g salted butter
  • 40 g demerara sugar
  • a heaped tsp of orange zest
  • 1 tsp roughly ground alexander seeds
  • 125 g plain flour
  • 1/4 tsp baking powder
Method
  1. Heat the oven to 180 °C (160 °C fan) and grease a large tray.
  2. Cream the butter and sugar, then beat in the zest and seeds.
  3. Mix the flour and baking powder separately and work them in, stopping as soon as it comes together — overworking makes the shortbread tough.
  4. Flatten the dough to roughly 20 x 15 cm and cut about 25 rectangles.
  5. Lift them onto the tray with a palette knife, at least 1 cm apart, and prick each with a fork.
  6. Bake 8–10 minutes, until light brown.
  7. Cool 5 minutes before moving them to a rack.
  8. They keep a week in an airtight tin.

Alexander seed and chocolate truffles

Built from leftover alexander seed cake.

Ingredients
  • 150 g of the cake (undrizzled)
  • 125 g mascarpone or another soft cream cheese
  • 120 g dark cooking chocolate
  • 1/2–1 tsp roughly ground alexander seeds if you want more of them
Method
  1. Crumble the cake into small pieces rather than crumbs and work in the cheese and seeds.
  2. Roll into balls about 2.5 cm across on greaseproof paper and chill for an hour.
  3. Melt the chocolate in a bowl set over a little simmering water, taking it off the heat when about half has melted and letting the rest come on its own.
  4. Dip each ball with a teaspoon until fully coated, return it to the paper, and leave to set hard.
  5. They keep several days.
Apple mint Mentha suaveolens 2 recipes

Apple mint jelly

Method
  1. A kilo of green cooking apples, quartered but not peeled or cored, simmered with a small bunch of apple mint under water until tender, about 40 minutes.
  2. Strain overnight through a jelly bag, stir a cup of cider vinegar into the juice and measure it, then add 500 g sugar for every 500 ml.
  3. Dissolve the sugar gently, boil hard to setting point, about 10 minutes, and stir a quarter cup of finely chopped leaves through off the heat before potting into warm sterilised jars.

Apple mint mojito

Ingredients
  • Five apple mint leaves
  • a tablespoon of muscovado sugar
  • two shots of rum
  • 100 ml soda water
  • a lime and ice
Method
  1. Squeeze two lime wedges over the sugar in the glass, add the leaves and muddle them into it, pour the rum over, fill with ice, top with soda and stir.
Arrow bamboo Pseudosasa japonica 1 recipe

Preparing fresh shoots

Method
  1. The husk is many overlapping brown layers covered in fine, sharp hairs, so handle it with that in mind.
  2. Run a knife up the side of the shoot to slit it, then unwrap and throw away the layers until you reach the pale core; cut off the pointed tip and the fibrous base.
  3. These are the bitter end of the five: cover the core with water, boil it hard, change the water, and keep going in five-minute rounds in fresh water until the flavour comes back mild.
  4. Taste between rounds rather than trusting a clock.
  5. Cut the shoots when they stand about 8–10 cm above the ground, taking them 5 cm or more below the soil.
Autumn hawkbit Scorzoneroides autumnalis 3 recipes

Wild greens with garlic and chilli

Serves 2–4

Written for any wild greens, dandelion among them.

Method
  1. Blanch the greens in boiling salted water until they taste good to you — for wild greens this can take 10–15 minutes — and chop long ones coarsely; you want about 225 g cooked.
  2. Slice a medium clove of garlic (about 5 g) as thinly as you can and cook it in 2 tbsp olive oil in a 20 cm pan over medium-low heat for 4–5 minutes, until lightly browned and fragrant but not burnt.
  3. Add a pinch of chilli flakes, then the greens; season, stir to coat and heat through.
  4. Serve with lemon wedges and more olive oil.
  5. With bitter greens, a finely chopped anchovy fillet or a dash of fish sauce can stand in for the salt.

Sautéed greens with pine nuts

Written for dandelion greens.

Method
  1. Gently cook garlic in 2 tbsp olive oil over medium-low heat for 3–4 minutes without letting it burn, then lift it out onto a paper towel.
  2. Toast 4 tbsp pine nuts in the same oil over low heat for 3–4 minutes until browned and lift them out too.
  3. Add about 1.25 litres (loosely packed) of greens cut into 5 cm pieces and cook on low for 2–3 minutes until wilted.
  4. Stir in 1 tbsp balsamic vinegar, the pine nuts and garlic, then about 45 g grated Parmesan; season with salt and pepper.

Roast pumpkin and sautéed greens salad

Written for dandelion leaves.

Method
  1. Heat the oven to 250 °C.
  2. Cut 1.75 kg butternut pumpkin into 2 cm-thick pieces, toss with olive oil, salt and pepper, and roast in one layer on two lined trays for about 20 minutes, turning once, until golden and tender; let it cool.
  3. Toast about 60 ml pumpkin seeds, tossed in olive oil and garlic salt, in a moderate oven for about 5 minutes.
  4. Tear 200 g washed leaves into bite-sized pieces and sauté in a hot oiled pan until cooked.
  5. Shake 60 ml honey, 2 tbsp balsamic vinegar and 1 tbsp olive oil in a jar (warm the honey first if it is stiff).
  6. Combine the greens and pumpkin, scatter with the seeds, dress, and season.
Banana passionfruit Passiflora tarminiana 4 recipes

Banana passionfruit jelly

Makes about six half-pint jars

Halve the fruit and scrape the pulp and seeds into a pot, then drop the empty skins in with them — in the hope they release pectin into the juice; the author notes you might not want to do that if the fruit is not organic.

Ingredients
  • 20–30 banana passionfruit
  • the juice of 2 lemons (6–8 tbsp)
  • up to 1.1 kg sugar
Method
  1. Add just enough water to cover, around 1–1.5 litres, bring to the boil and simmer 30–45 minutes, stirring now and then.
  2. Strain, pressing the pulp to get all the juice out, and throw away the skins and spent seeds.
  3. Return the juice to the pot with the lemon juice and sugar, and boil until it reaches setting point — allow another half hour, longer if it started watery.
  4. Pour into clean jars and water-bath 15 minutes to seal.
  5. The sugar can come down from 1.1 kg if that is too sweet, but some is needed for the pectin to set.

Curuba milk drink (jugo de curuba en leche)

Ingredients
  • 12 ripe fruit
  • 240 ml water
  • about 300 g sugar
  • 2 litres milk
  • 60 ml cream
  • ice
Method
  1. Halve the fruit and scoop the pulp into a blender with the water, and blend to a cream.
  2. Add the milk, the sugar and a little crushed ice and blend at medium speed, then add the cream and blend one minute more.
  3. Strain out the seeds if you like, and serve cold.

Curuba sorbete with chocolate

Ingredients
  • 8 fruit
  • 480 ml milk
  • 4 tablespoons sugar
  • a 200 g pot of cream
  • a bar of chocolate
Method
  1. Blend the pulp with 240 ml water and strain out the seeds.
  2. Blend the juice again with the sugar, the milk and 6 tablespoons of the cream, with a little ice if you like.
  3. Melt the chocolate, coat the inside of each glass and chill to set it.
  4. Whip the rest of the cream until firm, pour the sorbete into the glasses and top with the cream.

Tumbo jam (mermelada de tumbo)

Ingredients
  • 10 fruit
  • 600 ml water
  • about 400 g sugar
  • 2 cloves
  • the juice of 1 lemon
Method
  1. Scoop out the pulp, blend it for only about 3 seconds with 120 ml of the water, and strain to keep the liquid.
  2. In a large pot put the remaining 480 ml water, the sugar, the cloves and the lemon juice, add the strained liquid and cook over medium-low heat for about 40 minutes, until it thickens.
  3. Take out the cloves before it comes off the heat, and let it cool.
  4. Good on bread.

Barberry Berberis glaucocarpa 1 recipe

Barberry and peach bliss balls

Written for barberry (Berberis glaucocarpa) by the New Zealand forager who picked it.

Method
  1. Soak ½ cup (125 ml) barberries if they have shrivelled, and soak ¾ cup (185 ml) dates.
  2. In a food processor, grind ¾ cup (185 ml) almonds and ¼ cup (60 ml) sunflower seeds to crumbs.
  3. Add the barberries, dates, ¼ cup (60 ml) dried peaches, a pinch of salt, a pinch of cinnamon, 1 tablespoon carob powder and ¼ cup (60 ml) desiccated coconut, and process until the mixture holds together.
  4. Roll into balls and coat them in more coconut.

The source leaves out the unit for the almonds, barberries and coconut; cups are assumed here, matching its other lines. Millilitres use the 250 ml metric cup.

Bay tree Laurus nobilis 1 recipe

Bay leaf and lemon shortbread

Method
  1. Grind 3 small dried bay leaves with 1–2 tbsp of 200 g sugar to a powder, sifting out any stubborn bits.
  2. Beat with 454 g soft butter, the rest of the sugar, 1 tsp lemon zest and ⅛ tsp salt, then work in 480 g flour a little at a time to a soft dough.
  3. Shape 25 g rounds and bake at 175 °C for 18–20 minutes, until lightly browned at the edges.
  4. For the icing, grind 5 small bay leaves with 67 g sugar, then beat with 120 g icing sugar, 1 tbsp meringue powder and 3 tbsp lemon juice at medium speed for 4–5 minutes, until thick but still pourable.
  5. Dip the cooled biscuits and leave on racks 12–24 hours to set.
Beaked hawksbeard Crepis vesicaria 1 recipe

Horta (boiled wild greens with olive oil and lemon)

For 4

Method
  1. Soak about 450 g of freshly picked young greens in cold water for 10 minutes.
  2. Bring about 3.8 L of water and 3 tbsp kosher salt to the boil in a large lidded pot, add the greens, cover and bring back to the boil.
  3. Uncover and cook for 60 seconds, stirring until all are wilted, then taste one — some greens take longer — and drain in a colander once tender.
  4. Let them drain for a minute, turning them over, then tip into a warmed serving bowl, drizzle with olive oil and serve with lemon wedges and about ½ tsp finishing salt to taste.
Bermuda buttercup Oxalis pes-caprae 2 recipes

Leafy green tart with Bermuda buttercup

Method
  1. Make the filling first; the pastry needs no resting.
  2. Bring 2.5 cm of water to the boil in a large pot, pack in about 680 g of leafy greens (spinach, Swiss chard leaves, nettles, lamb's quarters, amaranth or quickweed), cover and cook on high for 2 minutes, stir, then cook 2 minutes more until just tender but still bright green.
  3. If you use nettles, cover them in boiling water instead and cook about 5 minutes.
  4. Drain, refresh in cold water, squeeze as dry as you can, roll in a clean tea towel to press out the rest, and chop roughly.
  5. Beat 3 egg yolks and 1 egg with 1 cup crème fraîche or sour cream, ½ cup whipping cream, ¼ tsp salt and plenty of black pepper, then stir in the greens and 3 tbsp chopped Bermuda buttercup stems, adding more salt if needed.
  6. For the pastry, stir 2¼ cups plain flour, ¼ tsp salt, ½ cup extra virgin olive oil and ½ cup tepid water together and form a ball.
  7. Heat the oven to 200 °C.
  8. Roll the pastry into a disc about 30 cm across on an oiled or lined baking tray, turn up and crimp the edge into a lip, spread in the filling and bake 40–45 minutes, until the pastry is crisp and the centre is set.

Sour grass pasta

Method
  1. Cook pasta of your choice as the packet directs, drain and return it to the pot.
  2. Stir in olive oil or butter until melted, then add shredded parmesan (or any cheese), a couple of handfuls of Bermuda buttercup flowers and leaves, and salt and pepper to taste.
  3. Toss and serve.
Black bamboo Phyllostachys nigra 2 recipes

Preparing fresh shoots

Method
  1. The husk is many overlapping brown layers covered in fine, sharp hairs, so handle it with that in mind.
  2. Run a knife up the side of the shoot to slit it, then unwrap and throw away the layers until you reach the pale core; cut off the pointed tip and the fibrous base.
  3. Acrid raw, and they come round with less work than most: cover with water, boil, and change the water once after 8–10 minutes, then simmer until tender.
  4. The shoots are wide — about 5 cm across — so slice before boiling if you want the leaching to reach the middle.
  5. They keep a distinct taste and smell of their own.
  6. Cut them at about 8 cm above the ground, 5 cm below the soil.

Hachiku simmered with chicken

For two

Ingredients
  • 200 g of shoots already boiled
  • 1 chicken thigh (about 200 g)
  • 3 snow peas
  • 15 ml oil
Seasoning
  • 15 ml sake
  • 30 ml mirin
  • 5 ml sugar
  • 22.5 ml soy sauce
  • 2.5 ml granulated dashi
  • 200 ml water
Method
  1. Cut the tips into 4–5 cm lengths and halve them lengthways; slice the rest 7 mm thick.
  2. Cut the chicken into bite-sized pieces and fry it in the oil over a medium heat until it changes colour.
  3. Add the seasoning and the shoots, bring to the boil, cover and simmer on a low heat for 10–15 minutes.
  4. Top with the snow peas, blanched.

Written for hachiku, the green-caned var. henonis, which is naturalised here.

Black cherry Prunus serotina 1 recipe

Fermented wild black cherry cordial

Method
  1. Use fully ripe cherries, so dark purple they look black; red ones are under-ripe.
  2. If they need cleaning, float 5 cups of cherries in a large pot of cold water, pour off whatever rises, and drain.
  3. In a large glass jar (about 7.5 L) crush the raw cherries by hand to break every skin — hands are gentler on the stones than a masher.
  4. Add 15 cups cold water, 5 cups raw cane sugar and 2 tsp citric acid (or 8 tbsp lemon juice) and stir until the sugar dissolves; any left will dissolve over the next day.
  5. Cover with cheesecloth or a linen towel tied on with string, so it can breathe but fruit flies cannot get in, and keep it in a cool spot out of direct sun, such as a kitchen counter.
  6. Stir vigorously with a clean spoon about once every 12 hours (at least once a day) and taste a teaspoon after each stir.
  7. After 2–3 days it should be bubbly; fewer days give a sweeter, less fizzy drink, more days a drier, fizzier one — the recipe's authors bottle theirs after 5–7 days.
  8. Strain out the stones, skins and pulp, finishing through a fine-mesh strainer, pour into swing-top bottles or jars (not plastic or metal) and refrigerate at once.
  9. Never store the bottles at room temperature: the fermentation keeps building pressure and they can burst.
  10. It keeps in the fridge for months, and is lightly alcoholic, probably about 1–2.5%.
Black passionfruit Passiflora edulis 2 recipes

Passionfruit curd

Ingredients
  • The pulp of about 12 passionfruit (250 ml)
  • 125 g unsalted butter
  • 165 g caster sugar
  • 5 eggs
  • 2 extra yolks
Method
  1. Whisk the pulp to loosen it and push it through a sieve, then stir half the seeds back into the juice.
  2. Put the juice, butter and sugar in a heatproof bowl over a pan of simmering water, keeping the bowl clear of the water, and stir until the butter melts.
  3. Beat the eggs and yolks lightly, whisk them in gently, and keep stirring for 20–25 minutes until it thickens: it is ready when a finger drawn across the curd on the back of a spoon leaves a line that holds.
  4. Pour into warm sterilised jars, seal, cool, and keep in the fridge for up to 8 weeks.

Passionfruit cordial

Makes about 1.25 litres

Ingredients
  • 880 g caster sugar
  • 500 ml water
  • 250 ml passionfruit pulp
  • 1 teaspoon citric acid
Method
  1. Put everything in a saucepan over medium heat and stir until the sugar dissolves, bring to the boil, take off the heat and let it cool.
  2. Keep it in the fridge for up to 4 weeks.
  3. Serve over crushed ice, topped up with water or soda water.
Blackberry Rubus fruticosus agg. 9 recipes

Blackberry jam

Makes about 8 jars

Written for blackberries; the page names no species.

Ingredients
  • 1.8 kg blackberries
  • 1.5 kg jam sugar (sugar with added pectin)
  • the juice and pips of 1 lemon
  • a fingertip-sized knob of butter (optional)
Method
  1. The night before, layer the berries and sugar in a very large bowl, cover and leave at room temperature so the sugar starts to dissolve; stir once in the morning.
  2. Put a small saucer in the freezer.
  3. Tip everything into a preserving pan or a large, wide pan and stir in the lemon juice; tie the lemon pips in muslin or a tea strainer and add them, because they give up pectin and help the set.
  4. Warm gently until all the sugar has dissolved, then bring to the boil and simmer for 5 minutes.
  5. Take off the heat and put a little on the cold saucer: once it is cool, push it with a finger, and if it wrinkles it has set.
  6. If not, boil 2–3 minutes more and test again, until it wrinkles.
  7. Skim, stir in the butter if using, and leave 15 minutes so the fruit settles before ladling into sterilised jars (wash them in hot soapy water and dry them in an oven at 170 °C, 150 °C fan, for at least 10 minutes).
  8. Keeps at least 6 months somewhere cool and dark; refrigerate once open.

Blackberry syrup

Makes about 200 ml

Written for wild blackberries.

Ingredients
  • 350 g blackberries
  • 115 g caster sugar
  • 3 strips of pared lemon rind
  • 2 slices of ginger each about 0.5 cm thick
  • 125 ml water
Method
  1. Wash and drain the berries and put them in a non-reactive pan with the sugar, lemon rind and ginger, then pour over the water.
  2. Bring gently to the boil and simmer for 15–20 minutes, stirring now and then, until the fruit is completely soft and the juice is starting to thicken.
  3. Take out the rind and ginger and press the pulp through a sieve with a wooden spoon (or use a mouli) to remove the seeds.
  4. Pour into a small sterilised bottle or jar, close it and leave to cool.
  5. Keeps up to a month in the fridge.
  6. Use it as a cordial with sparkling water, over ice cream, or folded into yoghurt or whipped cream.

Apple and blackberry shortcake

Serves 10

Written for wild blackberries; the author notes many roadside patches are sprayed, so pick only where you know they aren't, or use frozen.

Ingredients
Dough
  • 200 g softened butter
  • 200 g caster sugar
  • 4 eggs
  • ½ teaspoon vanilla essence
  • 400 g plain flour
  • ¼ teaspoon baking powder
Filling
  • 4 medium cooking apples (Granny Smith will do), peeled and cored
  • 200 g blackberries
  • 80 g caster sugar
  • the zest of a lemon and 50 g butter
  • caster sugar for sprinkling
Method
  1. Butter a pie dish about 24 cm across and 4 cm deep and swirl caster sugar round to coat it.
  2. Cream the butter and sugar, beat in the eggs one at a time, then the vanilla; sift in the flour and baking powder and stir until just combined.
  3. Cover and chill for at least an hour.
  4. Heat the oven to 190 °C (170 °C fan).
  5. Roll two-thirds of the dough between sheets of baking paper and line the base and sides of the dish; roll the rest for the lid and keep it chilled.
  6. Cut the apples into thick slices, toss with the 80 g sugar, the blackberries and the lemon zest, and fill the case.
  7. Dot with the butter, lay on the lid, press the edges to seal, cut a slit in the top and dust with caster sugar.
  8. Bake in the middle of the oven for 40–50 minutes, until a skewer comes out clean.
  9. Cool before serving with whipped cream or vanilla ice cream.

Blackberry crumble

Written for wild blackberries; the page names no species.

Ingredients
  • 500 g blackberries
  • 250 g plain or wholemeal flour (or 200 g flour and 50 g rolled oats)
  • 150 g soft brown sugar
  • 180 g cold unsalted butter, cubed
Method
  1. Heat the oven to 200 °C (180 °C fan).
  2. Put the berries in a lightly buttered ovenproof dish, with a sprinkle of sugar if they are sharp.
  3. Rub the flour and butter together with your fingertips until it looks like breadcrumbs, then stir in the sugar (and the oats).
  4. Spread the topping evenly over the fruit and bake on the middle shelf for about 30 minutes, until golden.
  5. Serve with cream or custard.

Blackberry sauce

Written for wild blackberries; the page names no species.

Ingredients
  • 250 g blackberries
  • 50 g caster sugar
  • 100 ml water
  • ½ tablespoon vanilla essence
Method
  1. Bring the berries, sugar and water to the boil and simmer until the fruit is mushy.
  2. Take off the heat, stir in the vanilla and push through a sieve to remove the seeds.
  3. Pour over ice cream, desserts, waffles or French toast, or stir into yoghurt.

Blackberry and apple hand pies

Makes 12

Written for wild blackberries; the page names no species.

Ingredients
  • 150 g blackberries
  • 2 eating apples
  • 50 g caster sugar plus extra for sprinkling
  • 2 tablespoons water
  • 1 beaten egg
  • 500 g shortcrust pastry
Method
  1. Heat the oven to 200 °C (180 °C fan) and line a baking tray with baking paper.
  2. Peel and chop the apples small and cook them with the sugar and water over medium heat for 5 minutes; add the blackberries and cook 5 minutes more on low, then tip into a bowl to cool.
  3. Roll the pastry 5 mm thick on a floured surface and cut 12 equal rectangles.
  4. Lay 6 on the tray, brush the edges with egg, spoon the fruit into the middle, cover with the other 6 and press the edges firmly with a fork.
  5. Cut a few slits in each, brush with egg, sprinkle with sugar and bake for 20 minutes, until golden and bubbling.

Blackberry flapjacks

Makes 12

Written for wild blackberries; the page names no species.

Ingredients
  • 100 g blackberries
  • 250 g jumbo rolled oats
  • 125 g unsalted butter
  • 125 g light brown sugar
  • 2 tablespoons golden syrup
Method
  1. Heat the oven to 200 °C (180 °C fan) and line a 20 × 20 cm tin with baking paper.
  2. Melt the butter, sugar and syrup in a pan over low heat, stir in the oats until coated, and press the mixture into the tin.
  3. Scatter the blackberries on top and press down firmly so it is level.
  4. Bake for 20–25 minutes, until golden, then cool before cutting into 12.

Summer pudding

Written for wild blackberries; the page names no species.

Ingredients
  • Blackberries alone or mixed with other summer fruit (raspberries, blackcurrants, redcurrants; frozen is fine) — enough to fill your pudding basin one and a half times
  • about 3 tablespoons caster sugar
  • sliced day-old white bread
  • a little butter
Method
  1. Butter the basin.
  2. Cut the crusts off the bread and line the basin with it, pressed firmly with no gaps, keeping a couple of slices for the lid.
  3. Heat the fruit and sugar gently in a pan until it simmers and runs with syrupy juice; taste carefully (it is very hot) and add sugar if it is sharp.
  4. Fill the basin with the fruit, keeping back some syrup and fruit.
  5. Put on the bread lid, press a plate on top and weigh it down with a can.
  6. Once cool, refrigerate overnight so the juice soaks into the bread.
  7. Turn out onto a plate, pour over the rest of the syrup and decorate with the leftover fruit.

Broad-leaved dock Rumex obtusifolius 9 recipes

Green goddess dip

Makes about 500 ml; it thickens in the fridge

Written for dock generally — the page does not say which; the same author names curled and broad-leaved dock as New Zealand's commonest docks. The author's greens were nasturtium, chickweed, onion weed, dandelion, oxalis, dock, plantain and wild mustard.

Ingredients
  • 500 ml (2 cups) roughly chopped wild greens
  • 125 ml (½ cup) cashews, soaked for an hour in the hottest tap water and drained
  • the zest and juice of 1 lemon
  • 60 ml (¼ cup) apple cider vinegar
  • 60 ml (¼ cup) water, only if your blender needs it
  • 2 peeled garlic cloves
  • 60 ml (¼ cup) extra-virgin olive oil
  • 60 ml (¼ cup) hemp seed oil (or more olive oil)
  • 5 ml (1 tsp) salt
  • 1.25 ml (¼ tsp) pepper
  • 30 ml (2 tbsp) nutritional yeast (optional)
Method
  1. Blend in a high-speed blender until smooth.
  2. Use it as a dip or dressing — with chips, steamed potatoes or kūmara, roast vegetables, an omelette or quiche, or stirred through pasta.

Weedolata (wild-greens gremolata)

Makes about 750 ml (3 cups)

Written for dock generally — the page does not say which; the same author names curled and broad-leaved dock as New Zealand's commonest docks. The author's greens were dock, onion weed, nasturtium, plantain, baby wild mustard, dandelion, sow thistle and a violet leaf.

Ingredients
  • 2 crushed garlic cloves
  • the zest and juice of 1 lemon
  • 500 ml (2 cups) firmly packed wild greens, sliced very thinly
  • 2.5 ml (½ tsp) salt
  • 1.25 ml (¼ tsp) cracked black pepper
  • 125 ml (½ cup) roughly chopped pistachios, walnuts, almonds or brazil nuts
  • 60 ml (¼ cup) hemp hearts, pumpkin or sunflower seeds
  • 60 ml (¼ cup) extra-virgin olive oil, hemp seed oil or a mix
Method
  1. To slice the greens, sort them into similar shapes, bunch and twist a few leaves together and slice as thinly as you can — this suits the tougher, stringier ones such as plantain, mustard and dock.
  2. Toss everything together.
  3. Serve as a side or salad, in a wrap or sandwich, or with pasta, risotto, eggs, or sausages and mash.
  4. It keeps at least 4 days in a sealed jar in the fridge.

Dock dolmas

Written for docks generally (the author names curly and broad-leaved dock).

Method
  1. Blanch 12 large dock leaves in boiling water, one or two at a time, for 30 seconds, and drain them on paper towels.
  2. Sweat ½ diced onion and 2–3 diced garlic cloves in 2–3 tbsp olive oil over a medium heat.
  3. Stir in 500 ml (2 cups) cooked rice, lower the heat, and add the juice of 1 large lemon (about 60 ml), a large handful of chopped mint and parsley, and any chopped dried fruit, nuts or leftover meat; season, add more oil if needed, and take it off the heat.
  4. Put two spoonfuls of the rice on each leaf, fold in the sides and roll up like a burrito.
  5. Drizzle with olive oil and sprinkle with coarse sea salt.

Dock chips

Written for curled and broad-leaved dock.

Method
  1. Wash the leaves and dry them very well, toss them in oil and lay them in a single layer on a baking tray.
  2. Season with salt or other seasonings and bake at 150 °C for 10–15 minutes, until crisp.
  3. Don't let them brown, which makes them more bitter; if they don't crisp, they were not dry enough.

Dock chowder

Written for curled and broad-leaved dock (from Edible Garden Weeds of Canada, 1978).

Method
  1. Melt 2 tbsp butter and cook 1 chopped onion until lightly browned.
  2. Add 750 ml (3 cups) washed, shredded dock leaves and cook 1–2 minutes until wilted.
  3. Add 750 ml (3 cups) milk, 750 ml (3 cups) fresh clams or oysters (or cod), and 2 large potatoes, cooked and cubed but still slightly firm.
  4. Cover and simmer over a low heat for 10–15 minutes, until the seafood is cooked.
  5. Season with salt and pepper.

Dock casserole

Written for curled and broad-leaved dock (from Edible Garden Weeds of Canada, 1978).

Method
  1. Bring 60 ml (¼ cup) water to the boil, add 500 ml (2 cups) washed dock leaves and simmer 3–4 minutes, then drain.
  2. Melt 60 ml (¼ cup) butter, cook 2 tbsp grated onion (and 2 pressed garlic cloves, if you like) for 1 minute, stir in 2 tbsp flour, then slowly stir in 250 ml (1 cup) milk; season and cook until thick.
  3. In a greased small casserole dish, layer the dock with 3 sliced hard-boiled eggs, pour the sauce over, and top with 125 ml (½ cup) panko and 125 ml (½ cup) grated aged cheddar.
  4. Bake at 180 °C for 20–25 minutes.

Dock seed crackers

Makes about 30

Written for the seeds of broad-leaved and curled dock.

Method
  1. Spread the gathered seeds on a baking tray and dry them in a low oven until slightly toasted, without burning them (about 625 ml, 2½ cups, of dried seed makes 250 ml, 1 cup, of flour); soaking them overnight first takes out some bitterness.
  2. Heat the oven to 180 °C.
  3. Grind the seeds in a coffee grinder or food processor, and mix 250 ml (1 cup) of the dock flour with 250 ml (1 cup) oat flour, ½ tsp salt, 1 tsp onion powder and 1 tbsp yeast flakes.
  4. Add up to 125 ml (½ cup) water a little at a time until it holds together without sticking, knead, cover and rest 10 minutes.
  5. Roll half the dough out about 2 mm thick on a greased, floured tray, cut into crackers or leave whole, and bake for 15 minutes; repeat with the rest.
  6. Cool completely.
  7. They keep a week in an airtight container.

Dock, dandelion and nettle puddings

Makes about 35 small puddings or one large one

Written for broad-leaved dock, with dandelion; the author says any dock picked in spring, before flowering, will do, and so will other strong-tasting spring greens such as nettle, plantain or watercress.

Method
  1. Boil 400 g pearl barley until tender, drain and cool.
  2. Hard-boil 4 eggs and chop finely.
  3. Finely chop 2 large leeks and soften them in a little oil.
  4. Blanch about half a supermarket bag of washed dock and dandelion leaves in boiling water for 20 seconds, then plunge into iced water (skip this and the puddings are more bitter and less green); squeeze out every drop of water and chop finely.
  5. Mix the chopped eggs, leeks, barley and greens with 3 raw eggs, and season very well.
  6. Wrap the mixture tightly in a tray covered with cling film, set in a larger tray of hot water, or seal it in a pudding bowl with baking paper, foil and string and stand it in a pan of water reaching at least halfway up.
  7. Steam for 45–60 minutes, in a 160 °C oven or on the hob, until it holds together; if it won't roll into balls, mix in 1–2 more raw eggs and steam longer.
  8. Turn out and serve as one pudding, or roll into small balls while warm and chill them (they freeze individually at this stage).
  9. Roll the chilled balls in flour, then in 1 beaten egg, then in 4 tbsp fine dry breadcrumbs (mixed, if you like, with 4 tbsp dried, ground nettle), and deep-fry for 1–2 minutes until browned; or simply flour and fry them.
  10. Serve warm, with garlic mayonnaise.

Dock soup with rice, egg and lemon

Serves 4

Written for dock generally — the author thinks his leaves were most likely curled dock, not broad-leaved; the New Zealand version below names broad-leaved and curled dock.

Ingredients
  • 170 g young dock leaves, washed
  • salt
  • 30 ml (2 tbsp) extra-virgin olive oil, plus more to finish
  • 50 g (¼ cup) basmati or other long-grain rice
  • 1 small onion, chopped
  • 15 ml (1 tbsp) chopped garlic
  • 15 ml (1 tbsp) chopped fresh oregano
  • a pinch of ground cumin
  • 1 litre chicken or vegetable stock
  • 1 large egg, beaten
  • plenty of fresh lemon juice
Method
  1. Use young leaves.
  2. Drop the dock leaves into boiling salted water for a few seconds until just wilted, cool them in cold water and squeeze them dry (about 180 ml).
  3. Toast the rice on a tray in a 175 °C oven until just golden, then simmer it, covered, with a pinch of salt and 125 ml water until tender.
  4. Chop the dock both ways so no long strands are left.
  5. In a 3-litre pot, soften the onion and garlic in the oil over medium heat for about 10 minutes, until just browning at the edges.
  6. Add the stock, dock, oregano and cumin, bring to a simmer and season, then add the rice and simmer gently for about 10 minutes.
  7. Pour the beaten egg in a spiral over the surface without stirring and let it heat through.
  8. Serve with a drizzle of olive oil, black pepper and enough lemon juice that the soup tastes lemony.
  9. For a simpler version without egg (Wild Botanica, New Zealand): soften the onion and garlic, wilt the chopped leaves (central veins removed) in the pot, add the stock, cumin, oregano and salt, simmer for 10 minutes, then stir through about 50 g cooked rice and finish with lemon juice.
Brown oyster mushroom Pleurotus australis 1 recipe

BBQ oyster mushroom steaks

Makes 2 steaks

Written for oyster mushrooms generally: the author's were pink oyster mushrooms (Pleurotus djamor) from a grow bag, and the author says wild-foraged oysters would be even better.

Ingredients
  • 1 large cluster of oyster mushrooms
  • 80 ml (⅓ cup) extra-virgin olive or refined coconut oil
  • 50 g butter, dairy or plant-based
  • 5–10 ml (1–2 tsp) wild salt or smoky chipotle seasoning
  • 10 ml (2 tsp) smoked paprika
  • 5 ml (1 tsp) cracked black pepper
  • 5 ml (1 tsp) salt (less if your seasoning is salty)
  • the leaves of a small stick of rosemary, chopped
Sauce
  • 500 ml vegetable stock
  • 150 ml smoky barbecue sauce
  • 15 ml (1 tbsp) liquid smoke
  • 4 finely chopped garlic cloves
Method
  1. Simmer the stock for 10 minutes, add the barbecue sauce, liquid smoke and garlic and simmer 15 minutes more, until it thickens.
  2. Keep the cluster's stems together and tear it into two or three hand-sized pieces.
  3. On high heat, melt 60 ml (¼ cup) of the oil with 30 ml (2 tbsp) of the butter in a cast-iron pan, press each piece in and weigh it down with another cast-iron pan or a heavy pot; cook about 8 minutes, until golden.
  4. Flip, press again and cook until golden, then flip, season the top generously, add the rest of the oil and butter, and cook pressed for 5 minutes a side.
  5. Leave the pieces in the sauce for 5 minutes.
  6. Heat the oven to 180 °C, put the steaks on a lined tray, baste with half the remaining sauce and bake 15 minutes; flip, baste with the rest and bake 15 minutes more.
  7. Serve with mashed potato or kūmara and greens or a salad.

Bush lawyer Rubus australis 3 recipes

Lawyer berry jelly

Makes 6 jars of 250 ml

Written for Australian native raspberry (Rubus probus), a different bramble; the author does not mention New Zealand lawyers.

Ingredients
  • 1 litre berry juice (fresh or thawed frozen berries put through a cold-press juicer)
  • 300 ml cold-pressed apple juice
  • the juice of 3 lemons
  • 1.3 kg white sugar
  • 10 whole cloves
  • the zest of an orange
Method
  1. Somewhere between a jam and a jelly: the juice is pressed rather than dripped through a bag, so it sets cloudy.
  2. Wash the jars and dry them, open end up, in a 120 °C oven for about 15 minutes, leaving them in the warm oven until needed; boil the lids for 5 minutes and let them air-dry.
  3. Put the berry, apple and lemon juices and the sugar in a jam pan, dissolve the sugar over low heat, then bring to the boil.
  4. Add the cloves and orange zest, skim off foam as it rises, and boil to setting point — about 30 minutes for the author, giving a soft set.
  5. Ladle the hot jelly into the hot jars and seal.

Lawyer berry vinegar

Written for Australian native raspberry (Rubus probus), a different bramble; the author does not mention New Zealand lawyers.

Ingredients
  • 400 g berries
  • 500 ml white wine vinegar
  • 100 g white sugar
Method
  1. Warm the vinegar with the sugar until it has dissolved.
  2. Put the berries in a large clean jar or a non-reactive bowl, pour the warm vinegar over them and leave overnight.
  3. Strain gently through a fine sieve or muslin and pour into a sterilised bottle or jar.

The author uses it to quick-pickle wafer-thin slices of raw beetroot (3 tablespoons of vinegar and ¼ teaspoon of sea salt for 2 small beetroot, soaked at least 15 minutes) served with goat's cheese.

Lady in Red cocktail

Written for Australian native raspberry (Rubus probus), a different bramble; the author does not mention New Zealand lawyers.

Method
  1. For one drink.
  2. Muddle a big handful of berries (fresh or frozen) in a cocktail shaker, add 60 ml tequila blanco, 30 ml Cointreau, 60 ml fresh lime juice and 60 ml sugar syrup, shake hard over ice and pour.
  3. Strain it if you don't want the seeds.
Bush nettle Urtica sykesii 4 recipes

Classic stinging nettle soup

Serves 6

Written for common stinging nettle (Urtica dioica and similar nettles) in North America, not for this species.

Method
  1. Blanch about 225 g nettles in salted boiling water until just wilted, about 30 seconds, cool them in cold water, squeeze dry and finely chop a handful to finish the soup.
  2. Sweat 1 cup diced celery, 2 cups diced leeks, 2 diced shallots and 1 small diced onion in 3 tbsp butter over medium heat for 5 minutes.
  3. Add about 1 litre chicken stock and 2 cups diced potato and simmer until tender, about 15 minutes.
  4. Add the blanched nettles and blend, then stir in the chopped nettles and 120 ml cream and season with salt and white pepper.
  5. Serve with crème fraîche and chopped dill or mint.

Steamed nettles with ricotta

Serves 2

Written for common stinging nettle (Urtica dioica and similar nettles) in North America, not for this species.

Method
  1. Wash about 225 g young nettle tops and steam them, covered, over boiling water for 2–3 minutes until tender.
  2. Meanwhile warm 3 large crushed garlic cloves in 3 tbsp olive oil over medium heat for at least 10 minutes until browned, then discard the garlic.
  3. Add the steamed nettles to the oil, season with salt, pepper and a pinch of chilli flakes, gently fold in ½ cup ricotta and warm through.
  4. Serve warm or at room temperature with olive oil and lemon wedges.

Nettle spanakopita

Serves 6

Written for stinging nettle in Britain; the recipe does not name the species.

Method
  1. Brown 25 g butter in a large frying pan, add 150 g washed, still-damp nettle leaves and cook for about 6 minutes until wilted; cool, squeeze out the liquid and chop roughly.
  2. Mix with 200 g crumbled feta, 50 g grated parmesan, the zest and juice of a lemon, two-thirds of a beaten egg, grated nutmeg and seasoning.
  3. Heat the oven to 200 °C (180 °C fan).
  4. Lay 3 filo sheets end to end, overlapping by about 5 cm, brush with some of 75 g melted butter, add 3 more sheets and more butter, then a seventh sheet in the middle.
  5. Spoon the filling in a line about 2 cm wide along one long edge, tuck in the ends, roll into a long sausage and coil it into a spiral about 20 cm across in a shallow round pan or on a buttered baking tray.
  6. Brush with the rest of the egg, scatter with 1 tbsp sesame seeds and bake for 40–45 minutes until golden.

Nettle and blue cheese rarebit

Serves 4

Written for stinging nettle in Britain; the recipe does not name the species.

Method
  1. Wilt 200 g nettle leaves in 1 tbsp hot olive oil for 1 minute, cool briefly and chop roughly.
  2. Mix with a 200 ml tub of crème fraîche, 1 tsp wholegrain mustard, half of 140 g crumbled blue cheese, and seasoning.
  3. Toast 4 slices of sourdough on both sides under the grill, spread with the nettle mix, top with the rest of the cheese and grill until golden and bubbling.
Catsear Hypochaeris radicata 2 recipes

Catsear root coffee

Method
  1. Dig up the roots, scrub them, roast them lightly in the oven, then grind them and brew to taste.
  2. Diego Bonetto, whose method this is, gives no temperature, time or quantity; the dandelion root coffee on the dandelion page has them, for dandelion root.

Steamed and fried catsear buds

Method
  1. Pick flowering stems with their buds and flowers; a good patch gives enough for dinner in a couple of minutes.
  2. Wash them and put them in a pot with a shallow layer of water, cover, and steam over medium heat until soft.
  3. Then fry them in extra virgin olive oil with garlic salt, or whatever seasoning you like, over medium-high heat until lightly golden.
  4. Scatter with petals from a fresh flower to serve.
Cheeseweed mallow Malva parviflora 2 recipes

Moroccan mallow salad (khoubiza or bakkoula)

Serves 4

Written for mallow in general; the author says little mallow is the kind most eaten in Morocco.

Ingredients
  • About 225 g mallow leaves and stems (about 2 litres, packed)
  • 120 ml olive oil
  • about 120 ml chopped parsley and/or coriander leaves
  • 4 cloves garlic, pressed or finely chopped
  • 1½ tsp paprika
  • 1½ tsp ground cumin
  • ¼ tsp salt
  • ¼ tsp cayenne pepper (optional)
  • 2 tbsp lemon juice
  • the chopped pulp of 1 preserved lemon
  • preserved lemon rind
  • a handful of red olives to garnish
Method
  1. Wash the mallow thoroughly, drain, chop the leaves and stems and steam over simmering water for 15–20 minutes, until tender; it shrinks a lot and darkens.
  2. Press out the extra water against the side of the steamer basket.
  3. Meanwhile, fry the oil, garlic, herbs and spices over medium or medium-low heat for several minutes, until the herbs wilt and the garlic smells fragrant, without burning the garlic, then take off the heat.
  4. Add the mallow, preserved lemon pulp and lemon juice and cook over medium heat for 5–10 minutes.
  5. Taste and adjust the seasoning, and serve warm or at room temperature with the olives and preserved lemon rind, usually as a dip with bread.
  6. Without preserved lemon, use a little more lemon juice; the same method works with spinach, kale or purslane, with the steaming time adjusted.

Sautéed cheeseweed

Written for cheeseweed, which the author names as Malva parviflora, in the US Southwest.

Ingredients
  • Several handfuls of cheeseweed leaves (about 1 litre once chopped)
  • 1 large onion, diced
  • cooking oil (the author suggests bacon fat or butter)
  • salt and pepper, or lemon or vinegar
Method
  1. Strip the leaves from the main stem and wash them; the leaf stalks can stay but chop them small, as they can be tough (chopped stems add a celery-like crunch).
  2. Chop the leaves coarsely.
  3. Brown the onion in a large frying pan or wok as much as you like (spring onions or garlic chives also work), add the greens and cover with a lid so their own moisture steams them as they shrink.
  4. Season and serve as a side dish or on pasta.
Cherry-plum Prunus cerasifera 4 recipes

Cherry plum jam

Makes about 7 jars

Ingredients
  • 1.8 kg cherry plums
  • 1.8 kg brown cane sugar
  • about 285 ml water
Method
  1. Put everything in a large, flat-bottomed pan, stir, and bring to the boil.
  2. Boil until the skins start to come off and the fruit comes away from the stones.
  3. Pour the mixture into a large piece of muslin and squeeze the pulp through it back into the pan.
  4. Keep boiling until it reaches setting point, which can take as long as 30 minutes.
  5. To test, chill a couple of saucers in the fridge before you start; drip a little jam onto one, let it cool for a moment, then push it sideways with a finger — it is ready when it wrinkles and feels tacky.
  6. Pour into sterilised jars and screw on the lids.

Cherry plum jam with lemon and vanilla

Makes about 1.2 litres, and scales well

Ingredients
  • 1 kg cherry plums
  • 1 kg white granulated sugar
  • 2 tbsp freshly squeezed lemon juice
  • if you like, 1 tsp vanilla extract or the scraped seeds of half a vanilla bean
Method
  1. Sterilise the jars and let them cool, and count the plums.
  2. Put the plums in a large heavy-based pot, stoned or whole (the author prefers to stone them first), and warm over medium heat, stirring often so they do not catch, until they burst and release some juice.
  3. Stir in the sugar and lemon juice, bring to the boil, then lower to a hard simmer; if the plums went in whole, press them against the side of the pot with a wooden spoon until the stones come free and sink.
  4. Cook to 105 °C, or as high as about 107 °C for a firmer set; the last few degrees can take a long time.
  5. Without a thermometer, chill a small plate in the freezer, and once the jam has noticeably thickened put a spoonful on it: if it looks set, it is done; if it is runny, keep cooking and test again every few minutes.
  6. Take it off the heat and stir in the vanilla, if using.
  7. If the plums were not stoned, strain the jam through a loose colander as needed and count the stones to be sure they are all out.
  8. The skins can stay in, or be removed with a colander or food mill.
  9. Ladle into the jars leaving 1 cm at the top, stand them in a very large pot of hot water covering them by 4 cm, bring to the boil and boil for 15 minutes, then lift them out very carefully and leave them to cool completely on the bench.
  10. Skip the water bath only if the jam will be eaten within a couple of weeks, kept in the fridge.

Cherry plum wine

Ingredients
  • 2.8 kg cherry plums
  • 1.4 kg sugar
  • about 4 litres water
  • 1 sachet wine yeast (any red wine yeast will do)
  • 1 tsp yeast nutrient
  • 1 tsp pectolase
Method
  1. Boil 1 litre of the water, pour it over the fruit and crush the fruit with the end of a rolling pin until no lumps are left.
  2. Leave it a few hours, then add the rest of the water and the pectolase — plums are rich in the pectin that sets jam and also clouds wine.
  3. Leave a couple of days, then strain through a fine sieve.
  4. Bring the juice quickly to the boil in a saucepan and turn off the heat straight away; the author once skipped this and lost the whole batch to acetobacter.
  5. Pour the hot juice over the sugar and stir until it dissolves, cool to room temperature, then add the yeast and yeast nutrient.
  6. Top up to 4.5 litres if necessary, funnel into a demijohn and fit an airlock.
  7. Rack into a clean demijohn after four to six weeks, and again a few weeks later if you want.
  8. Bottle when clear — or, if it will not clear completely, bottle it anyway.

Spiced pickled plums

Makes a 1-litre jar

Written for plums in general rather than cherry plums.

Ingredients
  • 250 g granulated sugar
  • 420 ml apple cider vinegar
  • ½ tsp crushed coriander
  • 1 tsp ground cardamom
  • 750 g plums, stoned and halved
  • 1 small cinnamon stick
  • 1 red chilli cut into quarters
  • 1 star anise
Method
  1. In a large saucepan, bring the sugar, vinegar, coriander and cardamom to a gentle simmer and cook for 5 minutes to infuse.
  2. Add the plums and cook for about 2 minutes, until just softened but still holding their shape.
  3. Put the cinnamon, chilli and star anise in a sterilised 1-litre jar (or two or three smaller ones), scoop in the plums and pack them tightly, leaving about 1.25 cm at the top, then pour the vinegar over until they are completely covered.
  4. Seal while still warm, leave to cool at room temperature, then refrigerate for 2 weeks before serving.
  5. They should last a couple of weeks in the fridge; for longer storage they can be processed in a water bath.
  6. Good on toast with farmhouse cheese, on savoury porridge, in salads, or with pork chops, chicken or turkey.

Wild weed spread

Makes about 600 g

The author makes it with chickweed, oxalis and onion weed together.

Ingredients
  • 500 ml (2 cups) raw cashews
  • 500 ml hot water
  • 80 ml (⅓ cup) extra-virgin olive oil
  • 1 garlic clove
  • the zest and juice of 1 lemon
  • 30 ml (2 tbsp) apple cider vinegar
  • 60 ml (¼ cup) nutritional yeast (or finely grated parmesan, or cream cheese)
  • 625 ml (2½ cups) wild greens, roughly chopped and tightly packed
  • 15 ml (1 tbsp) chopped thyme
  • dill
  • coriander or parsley
  • 5 ml (1 tsp) salt
  • 2.5 ml (½ tsp) pepper
Method
  1. Soak the cashews in the hot water for an hour, then drain them and throw the water away.
  2. Blend everything together until very smooth.
  3. The mixture is stiff, like cream cheese: a stick blender in a jug works best, and a jug blender may need a little water.
  4. Spread it on toast or crackers, or use it in sandwiches and burgers.

Spoon it into a jar or dish with a lid, pour a thin layer of olive oil over the top so it does not discolour, and keep it covered in the fridge; the author says it keeps for a couple of weeks.

Green goddess dip

Makes about 500 ml; it thickens in the fridge

The author's greens were nasturtium, chickweed, onion weed, dandelion, oxalis, dock, plantain and wild mustard.

Ingredients

  • 125 ml (½ cup) cashews, soaked for an hour in the hottest tap water and drained
  • the zest and juice of 1 lemon
  • 60 ml (¼ cup) apple cider vinegar
  • 60 ml (¼ cup) water, only if your blender needs it
  • 2 peeled garlic cloves
  • 60 ml (¼ cup) extra-virgin olive oil
  • 60 ml (¼ cup) hemp seed oil (or more olive oil)
  • 5 ml (1 tsp) salt
  • 1.25 ml (¼ tsp) pepper
  • 30 ml (2 tbsp) nutritional yeast (optional)
Method
  1. Blend in a high-speed blender until smooth.
  2. Use it as a dip or dressing — with chips, steamed potatoes or kūmara, roast vegetables, an omelette or quiche, or stirred through pasta.

Wild summer salad

Written for the author's mixed wild leaves.

Method
  1. Mix pungent and mild leaves about 1 to 3, then scatter a few extras over the top.
  2. Dress simply with walnut oil, elderberry vinegar, salt, pepper and a little mustard; he finishes it with fermented greens and pickled buds.

Of his lists, this catalogue covers dandelion and hairy bittercress (pungent), chickweed and young hawthorn leaves (mild), and gorse flowers and flowering currant blossom (extras). He warns to be extra careful identifying leaves before the plants flower.

Choko Sicyos edulis 1 recipe

Choko and yacon slaw

Serves 4; it keeps well in the fridge

Method
  1. Grate into a large bowl 1 choko, peeled with the seed removed (peel it whether you eat it raw or cooked; it is firm), and 1 scrubbed yacon.
  2. Add a quarter of a red cabbage, sliced as finely as you can, the juice of 1 lime, 2.5 ml (½ tsp) salt and 2.5 ml (½ tsp) coconut sugar, and toss — the author adds the lime and salt straight away to take away the choko's slightly astringent feel.
  3. Then add 60 ml (¼ cup) coconut thread, 60 ml (¼ cup) raisins, 60 ml (¼ cup) mung bean sprouts, 4 sprigs of mint, 45 ml (3 tbsp) aioli (egg, aquafaba or tofu based) and some black pepper, and toss well.

The author's greens, picked from roadsides around Whangaroa Harbour, were nasturtium, kawakawa, fennel, samphire, fat hen, wild mustard, cleavers and clover. The author warns that New Zealand roadsides are still sprayed with glyphosate, so pick with care.

Climbing dock Rumex sagittatus 1 recipe

Climbing dock salsa verde

Makes about 8 servings

Written for climbing (rambling) dock in Australia, with farmer's friend and dandelion leaves.

Method
  1. Put 500 ml (2 cups) climbing dock leaves, 125 ml (½ cup) farmer's friend leaves, 125 ml (½ cup) dandelion leaves, 250 ml (1 cup) parsley leaves, 2 crushed garlic cloves and 2 tbsp drained capers in a food processor and chop finely.
  2. With the motor running, add 125 ml (½ cup) extra-virgin olive oil, a dash of red wine vinegar, 1 tsp Dijon mustard and the juice of 1 lemon, and process until combined.
