Clustered dock Rumex conglomeratus 1 recipe

Dock soup with rice, egg and lemon

Serves 4

Written for dock generally — the author thinks his leaves were most likely curled dock but is not certain; the New Zealand version below names broad-leaved and curled dock.

Ingredients
  • 170 g young dock leaves, washed
  • salt
  • 30 ml (2 tbsp) extra-virgin olive oil, plus more to finish
  • 50 g (¼ cup) basmati or other long-grain rice
  • 1 small onion, chopped
  • 15 ml (1 tbsp) chopped garlic
  • 15 ml (1 tbsp) chopped fresh oregano
  • a pinch of ground cumin
  • 1 litre chicken or vegetable stock
  • 1 large egg, beaten
  • plenty of fresh lemon juice
Method
  1. Clustered dock is very bitter, more so as it ages, so use only young leaves.
  2. Drop the dock leaves into boiling salted water for a few seconds until just wilted, cool them in cold water and squeeze them dry (about 180 ml).
  3. Toast the rice on a tray in a 175 °C oven until just golden, then simmer it, covered, with a pinch of salt and 125 ml water until tender.
  4. Chop the dock both ways so no long strands are left.
  5. In a 3-litre pot, soften the onion and garlic in the oil over medium heat for about 10 minutes, until just browning at the edges.
  6. Add the stock, dock, oregano and cumin, bring to a simmer and season, then add the rice and simmer gently for about 10 minutes.
  7. Pour the beaten egg in a spiral over the surface without stirring and let it heat through.
  8. Serve with a drizzle of olive oil, black pepper and enough lemon juice that the soup tastes lemony.
  9. For a simpler version without egg (Wild Botanica, New Zealand): soften the onion and garlic, wilt the chopped leaves (central veins removed) in the pot, add the stock, cumin, oregano and salt, simmer for 10 minutes, then stir through about 50 g cooked rice and finish with lemon juice.
Common lavender Lavandula angustifolia 3 recipes

Lavender biscuits

Written for English lavender (Lavandula angustifolia) — the author names it and bakes with 'Hidcote'.

Method
  1. Cream 115 g butter (or vegan margarine) with 50 g caster sugar until pale and fluffy.
  2. Stir in 1 teaspoon vanilla extract, 1 tablespoon dried lavender flowers (or 2 tablespoons fresh), 175 g plain flour and 2 tablespoons plant-based milk, and bring it together into a ball.
  3. Wrap and chill for 20 minutes.
  4. Heat the oven to 200 °C.
  5. Roll the dough out on a lightly floured board, cut out biscuits and bake on a tray for about 10 minutes, until golden.
  6. Cool for 5 minutes on the tray, then on a rack.
  7. Serve sprinkled with sugar.

Fresh lavender ice cream

Written for English lavender (Lavandula angustifolia) by a lavender farm that grows it; the author uses 'Hidcote' for the deepest colour.

Method
  1. Pick a handful of flowers just as they open.
  2. Heat about 600 ml (2½ cups) milk gently with the flowers; do not let it boil.
  3. Strain out the flowers and, while the milk is still hot, stir in about 200 g (1 cup) sugar and ¼ teaspoon salt.
  4. Stir in 1 teaspoon vanilla extract and about 950 ml (4 cups) light cream, and chill for at least 2 hours.
  5. Churn in an ice-cream maker, or freeze in a steel bowl and whisk every 15 minutes until thick.
  6. Scatter in a few fresh florets in the last minutes.
  7. With dried flowers instead, use 3 tablespoons, steep them in the hot milk for 5 minutes and strain at once; steeping longer makes it bitter and grey.

Metric amounts are converted from the source's cups.

Lavender jelly

From Lynda Dowling's Herbal Adventures, on the recipe page of a lavender farm that asks for English lavender (Lavandula angustifolia).

Method
  1. Sterilise five 1-cup (about 250 ml) jars.
  2. Bring about 780 ml (3¼ cups) water to the boil, add about 360 ml (1½ cups) fresh lavender flowers, cover, simmer for 5 minutes and take off the heat.
  3. Without lifting the lid, steep for at least 20 minutes, or overnight.
  4. Strain, and measure about 720 ml (3 cups) of the infusion into a deep pan.
  5. Whisk in one box of powdered pectin and 1 tablespoon white wine vinegar, bring to the boil and whisk in about 800 g (4 cups) sugar.
  6. Keep it at a rolling boil that cannot be stirred down for about 1 minute.
  7. Take it off the heat, let it stand for 5 minutes, skim, then pour into the jars and seal before it sets.

The author lets it age for a month. The source does not give the size of the pectin box. Metric amounts are converted from the source's cups.

Common mallow Malva sylvestris 5 recipes

Sautéed mallow with onions (khobeizeh)

Serves 2

Written for mallow in general; the page names no species.

Ingredients
  • 450 g mallow leaves
  • 2 tbsp (30 ml) olive oil
  • 1 medium onion, diced
  • 1 medium lemon
Method
  1. Pull the leaves off the stems and soak them in water with a small pinch of salt, then rinse well under running water to remove any dirt or grit and drain completely.
  2. Fry the onion in the oil until soft and translucent, then lift it out.
  3. In the same pan, cook the leaves with a splash of water over medium heat, stirring now and then, until they wilt and shrink.
  4. Once they are tender, stir the onion back in, tip onto a plate and finish with lemon juice, to taste, and a drizzle of olive oil.
  5. Young leaves are best; older ones are tougher and have less flavour.
  6. Good with vermicelli rice or fresh pita bread.

Egg muffins with mallow

Written for Malva sylvestris.

Ingredients
  • 6 large eggs (or more, to fill the tray)
  • 3 tbsp milk, grated Parmigiano Reggiano
  • salt and freshly ground nutmeg to taste
  • freshly picked mallow leaves (the page gives no amount)
Method
  1. Heat the oven to 180 °C.
  2. Cook the leaves in boiling water for a few minutes, rinse, and blend to a purée.
  3. Whisk the eggs, milk, cheese, salt and nutmeg in a small bowl, stir in the mallow purée, and pour into a prepared muffin tray.
  4. Bake until cooked through and golden, 30–35 minutes.
  5. Leave to cool, then keep in a container in the fridge until eaten.

Mallow with chickpeas (espinacas con garbanzos style)

Written for common mallow (Malva sylvestris) picked in Andalusia, as a mallow version of the Granada tapa of spinach with chickpeas.

Ingredients
  • A bag of mallow leaves
  • a large jar of chickpeas (drained)
  • 4 tsp (20 ml) cumin seeds
  • half a bulb of garlic (crushed)
  • the juice of 1 lemon
  • olive oil and salt
Method
  1. Rinse the leaves, roll them into a tight bunch and slice roughly at about 1 cm.
  2. Steam them gently, covered, over low heat with a small cup of water until wilted, then set aside; mallow goes dry if only fried, so steam it first.
  3. Dry-toast the cumin seeds in a large frying pan until lightly browned and grind them.
  4. Fry the cumin in olive oil over medium heat for a minute, add the garlic and stir for about two minutes, then stir in the chickpeas to coat them, and then the mallow.
  5. Cover and simmer gently for 10–15 minutes, adding a little water if it dries out.
  6. Season with salt and lemon juice.
  7. Serve on its own or with bread.

Mallow soup with hazelnut dukkah

Serves 4

Written for common mallow, used in place of jute mallow in an adaptation of the Egyptian green soup molokhia.

Ingredients
  • 200 g mallow leaves (plus a few flowers to decorate)
  • 2 tbsp (30 ml) olive oil
  • 1 small onion, finely chopped
  • salt
  • 4 garlic cloves, chopped
  • 2 tsp (10 ml) ground coriander
  • 500 ml well-flavoured stock
  • 4 tbsp (60 ml) natural yoghurt
dukkah
  • 2 tbsp (30 ml) hazelnuts
  • 1 tbsp (15 ml) sunflower seeds
  • 2 tsp (10 ml) sesame seeds
  • 1 tsp (5 ml) each of cumin
  • fennel
  • nigella and coriander seeds
  • ½ tsp (2.5 ml) sweet paprika
Method
  1. Strip and rinse the leaves and chop roughly.
  2. Soften the onion with a pinch of salt in the oil over low heat for 5 minutes, add the garlic until lightly golden, then the coriander until fragrant, then the stock, and bring to the boil.
  3. For the dukkah, roast the hazelnuts at 160 °C for 10 minutes, rub off their skins in a tea towel, toast the seeds for 5–10 minutes until lightly browned, then crush everything roughly in a mortar and mix in the paprika.
  4. Add the leaves to the boiling stock and cook about 5 minutes, until tender but still bright green, then purée and season.
  5. Serve with a spoonful of yoghurt, the dukkah and the flowers, with rice or flatbread.

Garlic mallow soup (molokhia style)

Written for common mallow (Malva sylvestris), cooked to a molokhia recipe.

Ingredients
  • 800 g common mallow leaves
  • 6–8 garlic cloves, crushed
  • about 1 litre of chicken broth
  • 1 tbsp (15 ml) vegetable or olive oil
  • ½ tsp (2.5 ml) salt
  • a pinch of black pepper
Method
  1. Bring the broth to the boil, add the washed leaves, return to a mild boil, then simmer gently without boiling, stirring now and then, until the leaves have melted (15–20 minutes); simmer about 5 minutes more and take off the heat.
  2. Fry the garlic in the oil over medium heat until golden, without burning it, add the pepper, then ladle soup into the pan a little at a time — it should sizzle — until it is all mixed in.
  3. Salt to taste and eat as it is, or poured over rice and toasted pita.
Common sorrel Rumex acetosa 4 recipes

Sorrel soup

Written for common sorrel (Rumex acetosa).

Method
  1. Rinse 2 large handfuls of sorrel leaves and dry them in a salad spinner.
  2. Coarsely chop 1 onion and dice 1 potato, and fry them gently in a pressure cooker for about 3 minutes.
  3. Add 500 ml homemade stock and the sorrel, bring up to pressure and cook for 3 minutes.
  4. When it has cooled enough to open, blend with a hand blender.
  5. Serve with bread.

Green borscht (sorrel soup with potato)

Written for common sorrel (Rumex acetosa).

Method
  1. Fry 1 chopped onion in a little oil until soft, add 1 chopped garlic clove and 1 small-diced potato and fry for 2 minutes more, then pour in 600 ml stock (chicken or vegetable).
  2. Bring to the boil and add the sorrel: about 50 g of chopped leaves for a gentle sourness, 100 g or more for a sharp one.
  3. The leaves darken at once.
  4. Simmer for about 10 minutes, until the potato is soft, then blend.
  5. Serve hot or cold.
  6. For a richer soup, stir in a beaten egg at the end and cook a few minutes more; serve with a halved hard-boiled egg or a spoonful of sour cream.

Sorrel butter

Written for sorrel bought at a market; the author doesn't name the species.

Method
  1. Put half of 225 g soft unsalted butter, in thin slices, in a food processor, add 1½ cups (about 375 ml) finely chopped sorrel leaves, 1 teaspoon fine salt, 1 teaspoon ground black pepper and the finely grated zest of 1 lemon, then the rest of the butter.
  2. Process until evenly mixed, scraping down once or twice.
  3. Roll into a log about 4 cm across in baking paper or plastic wrap, twist the ends and chill.
  4. It keeps 5 days in the fridge or 3 months frozen.
  5. Slice discs onto cooked salmon or other fish, chicken or steamed vegetables.

Spring quiche with leeks and sorrel

Written for sorrel bought at a market; the author doesn't name the species.

Method
  1. Pastry: pulse 1¼ cups (about 160 g) plain flour with ¼ teaspoon salt, add 115 g cold cubed butter and pulse to fine crumbs, then with the motor running add 1 tablespoon cider vinegar and 3–5 tablespoons iced water until it just comes together.
  2. Flatten, wrap and chill 1 hour.
  3. Roll out to line a 23 cm tart or pie tin, line with foil and baking beans and bake 20 minutes at 190 °C; remove the beans, brush with 1 egg white, prick the base and bake 5 minutes more.
  4. Filling: cook 1 large sliced leek (white and pale green parts) in 2 tablespoons olive oil, covered, over medium-low heat until golden and caramelised, 20–25 minutes.
  5. Whisk 3 eggs with ¼ cup grated parmesan, ½ cup milk, ½ cup half-and-half (or equal parts milk and cream), ¼ cup ricotta, 1 teaspoon salt, ½ teaspoon pepper, 2 tablespoons chopped dill and 1 tablespoon chopped rosemary.
  6. Spread the leeks in the case, pour the egg mixture over and bake about 30 minutes, until puffed and just set.
  7. Cool about 12 minutes, then scatter 2 cups of washed, dried raw sorrel leaves over the top just before slicing (on a hot quiche they brown).
  8. Best the same day.
Creeping lawyer Rubus parvus 3 recipes

Lawyer berry jelly

Makes 6 jars of 250 ml

Written for Australian native raspberry (Rubus probus), a different bramble; the author does not mention New Zealand lawyers.

Ingredients
  • 1 litre berry juice (fresh or thawed frozen berries put through a cold-press juicer)
  • 300 ml cold-pressed apple juice
  • the juice of 3 lemons
  • 1.3 kg white sugar
  • 10 whole cloves
  • the zest of an orange
Method
  1. Somewhere between a jam and a jelly: the juice is pressed rather than dripped through a bag, so it sets cloudy.
  2. Wash the jars and dry them, open end up, in a 120 °C oven for about 15 minutes, leaving them in the warm oven until needed; boil the lids for 5 minutes and let them air-dry.
  3. Put the berry, apple and lemon juices and the sugar in a jam pan, dissolve the sugar over low heat, then bring to the boil.
  4. Add the cloves and orange zest, skim off foam as it rises, and boil to setting point — about 30 minutes for the author, giving a soft set.
  5. Ladle the hot jelly into the hot jars and seal.

Lawyer berry vinegar

Written for Australian native raspberry (Rubus probus), a different bramble; the author does not mention New Zealand lawyers.

Ingredients
  • 400 g berries
  • 500 ml white wine vinegar
  • 100 g white sugar
Method
  1. Warm the vinegar with the sugar until it has dissolved.
  2. Put the berries in a large clean jar or a non-reactive bowl, pour the warm vinegar over them and leave overnight.
  3. Strain gently through a fine sieve or muslin and pour into a sterilised bottle or jar.

The author uses it to quick-pickle wafer-thin slices of raw beetroot (3 tablespoons of vinegar and ¼ teaspoon of sea salt for 2 small beetroot, soaked at least 15 minutes) served with goat's cheese.

Lady in Red cocktail

Written for Australian native raspberry (Rubus probus), a different bramble; the author does not mention New Zealand lawyers.

Method
  1. For one drink.
  2. Muddle a big handful of berries (fresh or frozen) in a cocktail shaker, add 60 ml tequila blanco, 30 ml Cointreau, 60 ml fresh lime juice and 60 ml sugar syrup, shake hard over ice and pour.
  3. Strain it if you don't want the seeds.
Creeping wood sorrel Oxalis corniculata 12 recipes

Wood sorrel sauce

Ingredients
  • 500 ml (2 cups) wood sorrel leaves
  • 30 ml (2 tbsp) butter
  • 7.5 ml (1½ tsp) lemon juice
  • 7.5 ml (1½ tsp) maple syrup or honey
Method
  1. Melt the butter in a saucepan, add the sorrel and stir for 1–2 minutes, until it wilts.
  2. Put it in a blender or food processor with the lemon juice and the syrup or honey and blend until well combined.
  3. Serve it straight away, or refrigerate it and warm it before use; the author finds it even better the next day.

The author serves it on fish, or as a dipping sauce for grilled prawns.

Wood sorrel 'lemonade'

Method
  1. The amounts are rough: use as much sorrel as you like and enough water to cover it.

Wood sorrel tea

Method
  1. Fresh wood sorrel leaves and boiling water, one part leaves to three parts water.
  2. Put the leaves in a container, pour the boiling water over them and leave to steep for 5–15 minutes.
  3. Strain out the leaves and sweeten to taste if you like.
  4. Drink it hot, or pour it over ice for an iced tea.

Chilled lemony wood sorrel soup

Ingredients
  • 2 cloves garlic, minced
  • olive oil for cooking
  • 2 spring onions, chopped
  • 500 ml (2 cups) vegetable stock
  • 500 ml (2 cups) lightly packed wood sorrel leaves, with the flowers kept back for garnish
  • 3 medium mint leaves
  • 250 ml (1 cup) full-fat coconut milk
  • salt and pepper
Method
  1. Rinse the sorrel well and let it dry, then strip the leaves and flowers from the stems and throw away the bare stalks.
  2. Heat a little olive oil in a small saucepan and sauté the garlic until fragrant, then add the spring onions.
  3. Cover with the stock, bring to the boil and simmer for about 5 minutes, then add the sorrel for about 30 seconds.
  4. Take it off the heat, add the mint and blend smooth with a stick blender (or an ordinary blender).
  5. Stir in the coconut milk and season with salt and pepper.
  6. Refrigerate until well chilled, a couple of hours, and serve garnished with the sorrel flowers.

Spaghetti squash casserole with wood sorrel

Ingredients
  • About 450 g (1 lb) Italian or breakfast sausage
  • 1 medium spaghetti squash
  • several cups of wood sorrel, washed and drained, stems removed (the recipe gives no closer amount)
  • olive oil
  • 1 red onion, halved and sliced
  • 1 egg
  • 125 ml (½ cup) coconut milk or any milk
  • 1 garlic clove, minced
  • 15 ml (1 tbsp) garlic powder
  • 5 ml (1 tsp) tarragon
  • 5 ml (1 tsp) salt
  • 2.5 ml (½ tsp) black pepper
  • 1.25 ml (¼ tsp) ground sage
Method
  1. Heat the oven to 200 °C.
  2. Halve the squash, spoon out the seeds and stringy threads, set the halves cut side up on a baking tray, drizzle with olive oil, season with salt and pepper and bake for 20–25 minutes, until the flesh gives a little when pressed.
  3. Meanwhile cook the sausage and onion in a large pot over medium-high heat.
  4. When the sausage is cooked through, add the garlic and sauté for 2 minutes, then add the sorrel, milk and spices, cover and simmer until the sorrel wilts.
  5. Take the squash out of the oven and scrape out the flesh with a fork (careful, it will be hot).
  6. Combine the mixture with the egg, stirring thoroughly, spread it evenly in a baking dish (or the same pot, if it is ovenproof) and bake for 15–20 minutes, until the top is lightly browned.

Wild weed spread

Makes about 600 g

The author makes it with chickweed, oxalis and onion weed together; the page does not say which oxalis (written for oxalis generally).

Ingredients
  • 500 ml (2 cups) raw cashews
  • 500 ml hot water
  • 80 ml (⅓ cup) extra-virgin olive oil
  • 1 garlic clove
  • the zest and juice of 1 lemon
  • 30 ml (2 tbsp) apple cider vinegar
  • 60 ml (¼ cup) nutritional yeast (or finely grated parmesan, or cream cheese)
  • 625 ml (2½ cups) wild greens, roughly chopped and tightly packed
  • 15 ml (1 tbsp) chopped thyme
  • dill
  • coriander or parsley
  • 5 ml (1 tsp) salt
  • 2.5 ml (½ tsp) pepper
Method
  1. Soak the cashews in the hot water for an hour, then drain them and throw the water away.
  2. Blend everything together until very smooth.
  3. The mixture is stiff, like cream cheese: a stick blender in a jug works best, and a jug blender may need a little water.
  4. Spread it on toast or crackers, or use it in sandwiches and burgers.

Spoon it into a jar or dish with a lid, pour a thin layer of olive oil over the top so it does not discolour, and keep it covered in the fridge; the author says it keeps for a couple of weeks.

Green goddess dip

Makes about 500 ml; it thickens in the fridge

The author's greens were nasturtium, chickweed, onion weed, dandelion, oxalis, dock, plantain and wild mustard; the page does not say which oxalis (written for oxalis generally).

Wild greens soup

Serves 4

The author's greens were puha, sow thistle, nasturtium, onion weed, wild fennel, oxalis, wild mustard and wild celery; the page does not say which oxalis (written for oxalis generally).

Ingredients
  • 1 diced onion
  • 1 potato, scrubbed and diced
  • 2 sliced garlic cloves
  • 500 ml (2 cups) roughly chopped wild greens
  • 45 ml (3 tbsp) olive oil
  • 1.3 litres water
  • 5 ml (1 tsp) salt
  • 30 ml (2 tbsp) nutritional yeast (optional)
  • 1.25 ml (¼ tsp) cracked pepper
Method
  1. Cut the vegetables and greens to similar sizes.
  2. Fry the onion in the oil for a minute, then add the potato and garlic (the method also adds fennel bulb and celery stalks, which the ingredient list leaves out) and fry for another minute.
  3. Add the water, cover, bring to the boil and simmer until the potato is soft.
  4. Add the greens with the salt and yeast and cook for 3 minutes, until just tender — added at the end, they keep their colour.
  5. Blend until smooth (push it through a sieve if your blender isn't powerful), season with the pepper and more salt if needed, and serve drizzled with extra-virgin olive or hemp seed oil and scattered with edible weed flowers.
Curled dock Rumex crispus 14 recipes

Green goddess dip

Makes about 500 ml; it thickens in the fridge

Written for dock generally — the page does not say which; the same author names curled and broad-leaved dock as New Zealand's commonest docks. The author's greens were nasturtium, chickweed, onion weed, dandelion, oxalis, dock, plantain and wild mustard.

Ingredients
  • 500 ml (2 cups) roughly chopped wild greens
  • 125 ml (½ cup) cashews, soaked for an hour in the hottest tap water and drained
  • the zest and juice of 1 lemon
  • 60 ml (¼ cup) apple cider vinegar
  • 60 ml (¼ cup) water, only if your blender needs it
  • 2 peeled garlic cloves
  • 60 ml (¼ cup) extra-virgin olive oil
  • 60 ml (¼ cup) hemp seed oil (or more olive oil)
  • 5 ml (1 tsp) salt
  • 1.25 ml (¼ tsp) pepper
  • 30 ml (2 tbsp) nutritional yeast (optional)
Method
  1. Blend in a high-speed blender until smooth.
  2. Use it as a dip or dressing — with chips, steamed potatoes or kūmara, roast vegetables, an omelette or quiche, or stirred through pasta.

Weedolata (wild-greens gremolata)

Makes about 750 ml (3 cups)

Written for dock generally — the page does not say which; the same author names curled and broad-leaved dock as New Zealand's commonest docks. The author's greens were dock, onion weed, nasturtium, plantain, baby wild mustard, dandelion, sow thistle and a violet leaf.

Ingredients
  • 2 crushed garlic cloves
  • the zest and juice of 1 lemon
  • 500 ml (2 cups) firmly packed wild greens, sliced very thinly
  • 2.5 ml (½ tsp) salt
  • 1.25 ml (¼ tsp) cracked black pepper
  • 125 ml (½ cup) roughly chopped pistachios, walnuts, almonds or brazil nuts
  • 60 ml (¼ cup) hemp hearts, pumpkin or sunflower seeds
  • 60 ml (¼ cup) extra-virgin olive oil, hemp seed oil or a mix
Method
  1. To slice the greens, sort them into similar shapes, bunch and twist a few leaves together and slice as thinly as you can — this suits the tougher, stringier ones such as plantain, mustard and dock.
  2. Toss everything together.
  3. Serve as a side or salad, in a wrap or sandwich, or with pasta, risotto, eggs, or sausages and mash.
  4. It keeps at least 4 days in a sealed jar in the fridge.

Dock dolmas

Written for docks generally (the author names curly and broad-leaved dock).

Method
  1. Blanch 12 large dock leaves in boiling water, one or two at a time, for 30 seconds, and drain them on paper towels.
  2. Sweat ½ diced onion and 2–3 diced garlic cloves in 2–3 tbsp olive oil over a medium heat.
  3. Stir in 500 ml (2 cups) cooked rice, lower the heat, and add the juice of 1 large lemon (about 60 ml), a large handful of chopped mint and parsley, and any chopped dried fruit, nuts or leftover meat; season, add more oil if needed, and take it off the heat.
  4. Put two spoonfuls of the rice on each leaf, fold in the sides and roll up like a burrito.
  5. Drizzle with olive oil and sprinkle with coarse sea salt.

Dock chips

Written for curled and broad-leaved dock.

Method
  1. Wash the leaves and dry them very well, toss them in oil and lay them in a single layer on a baking tray.
  2. Season with salt or other seasonings and bake at 150 °C for 10–15 minutes, until crisp.
  3. Don't let them brown, which makes them more bitter; if they don't crisp, they were not dry enough.

Dock chowder

Written for curled and broad-leaved dock (from Edible Garden Weeds of Canada, 1978).

Method
  1. Melt 2 tbsp butter and cook 1 chopped onion until lightly browned.
  2. Add 750 ml (3 cups) washed, shredded dock leaves and cook 1–2 minutes until wilted.
  3. Add 750 ml (3 cups) milk, 750 ml (3 cups) fresh clams or oysters (or cod), and 2 large potatoes, cooked and cubed but still slightly firm.
  4. Cover and simmer over a low heat for 10–15 minutes, until the seafood is cooked.
  5. Season with salt and pepper.

Dock casserole

Written for curled and broad-leaved dock (from Edible Garden Weeds of Canada, 1978).

Method
  1. Bring 60 ml (¼ cup) water to the boil, add 500 ml (2 cups) washed dock leaves and simmer 3–4 minutes, then drain.
  2. Melt 60 ml (¼ cup) butter, cook 2 tbsp grated onion (and 2 pressed garlic cloves, if you like) for 1 minute, stir in 2 tbsp flour, then slowly stir in 250 ml (1 cup) milk; season and cook until thick.
  3. In a greased small casserole dish, layer the dock with 3 sliced hard-boiled eggs, pour the sauce over, and top with 125 ml (½ cup) panko and 125 ml (½ cup) grated aged cheddar.
  4. Bake at 180 °C for 20–25 minutes.

Dock seed crackers

Makes about 30

Written for the seeds of broad-leaved and curled dock.

Method
  1. Spread the gathered seeds on a baking tray and dry them in a low oven until slightly toasted, without burning them (about 625 ml, 2½ cups, of dried seed makes 250 ml, 1 cup, of flour); soaking them overnight first takes out some bitterness.
  2. Heat the oven to 180 °C.
  3. Grind the seeds in a coffee grinder or food processor, and mix 250 ml (1 cup) of the dock flour with 250 ml (1 cup) oat flour, ½ tsp salt, 1 tsp onion powder and 1 tbsp yeast flakes.
  4. Add up to 125 ml (½ cup) water a little at a time until it holds together without sticking, knead, cover and rest 10 minutes.
  5. Roll half the dough out about 2 mm thick on a greased, floured tray, cut into crackers or leave whole, and bake for 15 minutes; repeat with the rest.
  6. Cool completely.
  7. They keep a week in an airtight container.

Dock seed pasta

Written for curly (curled) dock — the author does not vouch for broad-leaved dock seed.

Method
  1. Soak 125 ml (½ cup) dock seed flour in 60 ml (¼ cup) boiling water, covered, until cool (see Preparation).
  2. Combine it with 500 ml (2 cups) flour, 5 eggs, 5 ml (1 tsp) salt and 125 ml (½ cup) water into a dough, and make pasta from it as usual; unsoaked dock flour can make the pasta brittle or tear.
  3. The same proportions work for a dock seed porridge or polenta.

The author served it with brown butter, morels and wild greens.

Dock seed and starch crackers

Serves 4

Written for curly (curled) dock — the author does not vouch for broad-leaved dock seed.

Method
  1. Heat the oven to 150 °C.
  2. Lightly toast the dock seeds in a pan over a medium heat for a few minutes before grinding them.
  3. In a small saucepan (about 1 litre), whisk together 80 ml (⅓ cup) dock flour, 3 tbsp cornflour or acorn starch (or 60 ml, ¼ cup, fine cornmeal), a tiny pinch of salt, 250 ml (1 cup) water, 1 tbsp cooking oil and 1½ tsp maple syrup.
  4. Bring to a simmer over a medium-high heat and cook until it forms a thick paste.
  5. While it is still warm, spread it as thinly and evenly as you can on a baking tray lined with baking paper or a silicone mat, and scatter with 1 tsp lightly toasted sesame seeds if you like.
  6. Bake for 45 minutes, until brittle, then break into pieces.
  7. Kept airtight they last for weeks; crisp them again in a warm oven if they soften.

Classic dock flour crackers

Written for curly (curled) dock — the author does not vouch for broad-leaved dock seed.

Method
  1. Pour 125 ml (½ cup) hot water over 52 g (⅔ cup) dock flour, mix, cover and leave to cool.
  2. In a larger bowl mix 1 tbsp (16 g) maple syrup and ½ tsp salt, then add the cooled dock flour.
  3. Mix in 160 g (1 cup) plain or other flour, then mash in 2 tbsp (22 g) unsalted butter or oil with a fork to a soft dough; it will look crumbly, so press it together, adding another tablespoon of warm water if it won't hold.
  4. Flatten into a disc, wrap and refrigerate for 20 minutes.
  5. Roll it out very thinly and evenly, about 3 mm, between two sheets of baking paper.
  6. Peel off the top sheet, cut the dough into strips, then cut across diagonally into diamonds.
  7. Bake at 180 °C for 15–20 minutes, until crisp enough to snap.
  8. Store covered at room temperature; if they soften, warm them at 120 °C for 10 minutes.

Rumex crisps (toasted dock seed crackers)

Makes about 36 crackers of 5 cm

Written for curly (curled) dock.

Method
  1. Toast the seeds first (see Preparation).
  2. Heat the oven to 150 °C.
  3. Mix 125 ml (½ cup) toasted dock seeds, 185 ml (¾ cup) rye flour and ¼ tsp sea salt in a large bowl, then stir in 1 tbsp grapeseed oil, ½ tbsp honey and 80 ml (⅓ cup) water.
  4. Work in up to another 60 ml (¼ cup) rye flour until the dough is moist but no longer sticky.
  5. Roll each half out cracker-thin on a floured bench, cut into squares, diamonds or strips and set them close together on a baking tray; re-roll the scraps.
  6. Brush lightly with grapeseed oil, sprinkle with a little more salt and bake for 15–20 minutes (thicker crackers take longer).
  7. Cool on a rack.
  8. They keep two weeks airtight; crisp them again in the oven if they soften.
  9. Good with creamy cheeses and dried apricots.

Potato gnocchi with porcini sauce and wilted dock

Serves 4

Written for curly (curled) dock; dandelion or other greens work too.

Method
  1. Gnocchi: boil 900 g golden potatoes, halved, in salted water until tender, 25–30 minutes, and keep the water.
  2. Lift them out one at a time, peel while hot and fluff with a fork without overmashing.
  3. Cool 5 minutes, then drizzle over 60 ml (¼ cup) lightly beaten egg, scatter over 185 ml (¾ cup) plain flour and fold together; knead very gently, adding up to 60 ml (¼ cup) more flour if it is gummy.
  4. Cut into 8, roll each into a thumb-thick log and cut every 2 cm; press each piece lightly with a fork and dust with flour.
  5. Sauce: heat 375 ml (1½ cups) low-salt chicken stock with 20 g rinsed dried porcini to a simmer, cover off the heat for 5 minutes, then lift out and dice the mushrooms, keeping the liquid.
  6. Fry them in 1 tbsp olive oil over a medium heat with 1 minced small shallot and 1 sliced garlic clove until lightly browned, 2–4 minutes.
  7. Add the liquid, 80 ml (⅓ cup) dry white wine and another 1 tbsp olive oil, scraping up the browned bits, and boil hard, whisking now and then, about 5 minutes.
  8. Stir in 2 tbsp chopped parsley, season and keep warm.
  9. To serve: bring the potato water back to the boil and cook the gnocchi in small batches, lifting them out about 10 seconds after they rise.
  10. Lay them straight onto a platter of a few handfuls of washed, trimmed dock leaves, which wilt in the heat.
  11. Top with the sauce, a drizzle of olive oil, salt, pepper and parmesan shavings.

Dock, dandelion and nettle puddings

Makes about 35 small puddings or one large one

Written for broad-leaved dock; the author says any dock picked in spring, before flowering, will do, and so will other strong-tasting spring greens such as nettle, plantain or watercress.

Method
  1. Boil 400 g pearl barley until tender, drain and cool.
  2. Hard-boil 4 eggs and chop finely.
  3. Finely chop 2 large leeks and soften them in a little oil.
  4. Blanch about half a supermarket bag of washed dock and dandelion leaves in boiling water for 20 seconds, then plunge into iced water (skip this and the puddings are more bitter and less green); squeeze out every drop of water and chop finely.
  5. Mix the chopped eggs, leeks, barley and greens with 3 raw eggs, and season very well.
  6. Wrap the mixture tightly in a tray covered with cling film, set in a larger tray of hot water, or seal it in a pudding bowl with baking paper, foil and string and stand it in a pan of water reaching at least halfway up.
  7. Steam for 45–60 minutes, in a 160 °C oven or on the hob, until it holds together; if it won't roll into balls, mix in 1–2 more raw eggs and steam longer.
  8. Turn out and serve as one pudding, or roll into small balls while warm and chill them (they freeze individually at this stage).
  9. Roll the chilled balls in flour, then in 1 beaten egg, then in 4 tbsp fine dry breadcrumbs (mixed, if you like, with 4 tbsp dried, ground nettle), and deep-fry for 1–2 minutes until browned; or simply flour and fry them.
  10. Serve warm, with garlic mayonnaise.

Dock soup with rice, egg and lemon

Serves 4

Written for dock generally — the author thinks his leaves were most likely curled dock but is not certain; the New Zealand version below names curled and broad-leaved dock.

Ingredients
  • 170 g young dock leaves, washed
  • salt
  • 30 ml (2 tbsp) extra-virgin olive oil, plus more to finish
  • 50 g (¼ cup) basmati or other long-grain rice
  • 1 small onion, chopped
  • 15 ml (1 tbsp) chopped garlic
  • 15 ml (1 tbsp) chopped fresh oregano
  • a pinch of ground cumin
  • 1 litre chicken or vegetable stock
  • 1 large egg, beaten
  • plenty of fresh lemon juice
Method
  1. Use young leaves.
  2. Drop the dock leaves into boiling salted water for a few seconds until just wilted, cool them in cold water and squeeze them dry (about 180 ml).
  3. Toast the rice on a tray in a 175 °C oven until just golden, then simmer it, covered, with a pinch of salt and 125 ml water until tender.
  4. Chop the dock both ways so no long strands are left.
  5. In a 3-litre pot, soften the onion and garlic in the oil over medium heat for about 10 minutes, until just browning at the edges.
  6. Add the stock, dock, oregano and cumin, bring to a simmer and season, then add the rice and simmer gently for about 10 minutes.
  7. Pour the beaten egg in a spiral over the surface without stirring and let it heat through.
  8. Serve with a drizzle of olive oil, black pepper and enough lemon juice that the soup tastes lemony.
  9. For a simpler version without egg (Wild Botanica, New Zealand): soften the onion and garlic, wilt the chopped leaves (central veins removed) in the pot, add the stock, cumin, oregano and salt, simmer for 10 minutes, then stir through about 50 g cooked rice and finish with lemon juice.
Dandelion Taraxacum officinale 9 recipes

Pickled dandelion buds

Makes about 500 ml

Method
  1. Pick the smallest, tightest unopened buds — under the first bud of a plant there are often several pea-sized younger ones deeper in the rosette.
  2. Bring 160 ml vinegar, 80 ml water and 1 tsp salt to the boil, stirring until the salt dissolves.
  3. Pack about 475 ml of buds into a jar and pour the hot brine over them.
  4. Cap and keep in the fridge, or process in a water-bath canner for 10 minutes with about 1 cm headspace.

Dandelion root coffee

Method
  1. Wash the roots, trim off any blemished parts and thin strands, and chop into small pieces of even size.
  2. Roast at 200 °C for 30–45 minutes on a tray lined with baking paper (or in an air fryer lined with foil), checking and shaking every 5–8 minutes and taking out any pieces that are done early.
  3. Cool completely and store airtight in a cool, dry place.
  4. Steep the pieces like tea, or grind them and use them like ground coffee in a plunger, with about the same number of scoops.
  5. LifeSource Natural Foods reckons on ten or more roots per cup, and brews 2 tsp of roasted root by bringing it to the boil in about 475 ml water, simmering for 15 minutes and straining.

Sautéed dandelion greens

For 6

Method
  1. Cut the tough base off about 900 g of dandelion leaves, cut them into bite-sized pieces and swish them in a bowl of cold water, rinsing until no grit is left.
  2. Warm 4–6 tbsp olive oil with 60 ml chopped or sliced garlic and a pinch of chilli flakes if you like in a large pan over medium heat, until the garlic softens, colours lightly and smells fragrant.
  3. Add the leaves, in batches if needed, and cook for a few minutes, stirring now and then, until as tender as you like and the liquid has cooked off; add a splash of water or cover the pan to steam if it gets dry.
  4. Season with salt and pepper and serve hot or at room temperature, with lemon juice if you like.
  5. To take out some of the bitterness, blanch the leaves first in salted boiling water — even a minute helps — then plunge them into iced water and squeeze out as much water as you can before sautéing.

Cleavers and dandelion cold infusion

Written for cleavers and dandelion together.

Ingredients
  • A handful of cleavers
  • a few dandelion flowers
  • 1 lemon (the author uses an organic one), sliced with its skin
Method
  1. Put them in a jug, top it up with filtered water and leave it overnight to steep before drinking.

Climbing dock salsa verde

Makes about 8 servings

Written for climbing (rambling) dock in Australia, with farmer's friend and dandelion leaves; the dandelion is a quarter of the leaves.

Method
  1. Put 500 ml (2 cups) climbing dock leaves, 125 ml (½ cup) farmer's friend leaves, 125 ml (½ cup) dandelion leaves, 250 ml (1 cup) parsley leaves, 2 crushed garlic cloves and 2 tbsp drained capers in a food processor and chop finely.
  2. With the motor running, add 125 ml (½ cup) extra-virgin olive oil, a dash of red wine vinegar, 1 tsp Dijon mustard and the juice of 1 lemon, and process until combined.
  3. Season with sea salt and pepper and leave it to stand for 10 minutes.
  4. Serve as a dipping sauce, condiment or marinade.

Dock, dandelion and nettle puddings

Makes about 35 small puddings or one large one

Written for dandelion and broad-leaved dock together; the author says other strong-tasting spring greens such as nettle, plantain or watercress can replace them.

Method
  1. Boil 400 g pearl barley until tender, drain and cool.
  2. Hard-boil 4 eggs and chop finely.
  3. Finely chop 2 large leeks and soften them in a little oil.
  4. Blanch about half a supermarket bag of washed dock and dandelion leaves in boiling water for 20 seconds, then plunge into iced water (skip this and the puddings are more bitter and less green); squeeze out every drop of water and chop finely.
  5. Mix the chopped eggs, leeks, barley and greens with 3 raw eggs, and season very well.
  6. Wrap the mixture tightly in a tray covered with cling film, set in a larger tray of hot water, or seal it in a pudding bowl with baking paper, foil and string and stand it in a pan of water reaching at least halfway up.
  7. Steam for 45–60 minutes, in a 160 °C oven or on the hob, until it holds together; if it won't roll into balls, mix in 1–2 more raw eggs and steam longer.
  8. Turn out and serve as one pudding, or roll into small balls while warm and chill them (they freeze individually at this stage).
  9. Roll the chilled balls in flour, then in 1 beaten egg, then in 4 tbsp fine dry breadcrumbs (mixed, if you like, with 4 tbsp dried, ground nettle), and deep-fry for 1–2 minutes until browned; or simply flour and fry them.
  10. Serve warm, with garlic mayonnaise.

Wild summer salad

Written for the author's mixed wild leaves.

Method
  1. Mix pungent and mild leaves about 1 to 3, then scatter a few extras over the top.
  2. Dress simply with walnut oil, elderberry vinegar, salt, pepper and a little mustard; he finishes it with fermented greens and pickled buds.

Of his lists, this catalogue covers dandelion and hairy bittercress (pungent), chickweed and young hawthorn leaves (mild), and gorse flowers and flowering currant blossom (extras). He warns to be extra careful identifying leaves before the plants flower.

Darwin's barberry Berberis darwinii 1 recipe

Barberry jelly

Written for Darwin's barberry (Berberis darwinii) by the horticulturist who grew it.

Method
  1. Simmer 900 g ripe berries in 2 litres of water until soft.
  2. Push the fruit and liquid through a sieve to hold back the skins and most of the seeds, then squeeze the pulp through muslin to get as much juice as you can.
  3. Heat the juice with the juice of one lemon, stirring in about 400 g (2 cups) sugar a cupful at a time as it warms.
  4. Boil hard for about 25 minutes, until it starts to set, then spoon into sterilised jars and cover.

The author got two good-sized jars. The source calls its strained liquid both '3 cups' and '1.5 L', which do not agree, so measure your own. Metric sugar is converted from the source's cups.

Dwarf mallow Malva neglecta 2 recipes

Palestinian sautéed mallow (khobiza)

Serves 2

Written for mallow in general; the author gives khobiza as Malva neglecta or Malva parviflora.

Ingredients
  • ½ tbsp olive oil
  • 1 small onion, thinly sliced
  • about 500 ml roughly chopped mallow leaves
  • a pinch of salt and black pepper
  • a squeeze of lemon
Method
  1. Heat the oil in a frying pan over medium heat and fry the onion for 2–3 minutes.
  2. Add the mallow, toss, and season with salt and pepper.
  3. Add the lemon and serve straight away.
  4. Keep leftovers in the fridge for a couple of days only.

Blended mallow soup

Written for mallow (Malva neglecta and other species) by herbalist Erin Smith.

Ingredients
  • 2–3 large handfuls of mallow leaves
  • 2 tbsp (30 g) butter
  • 1 large or 2 small onions, finely sliced
  • 1 large carrot, chopped
  • 2 celery sticks, chopped
  • 1 large garlic clove, crushed
  • about 1 litre of good chicken or vegetable stock
  • 1 medium potato
  • 2 tbsp (30 ml) yoghurt or crème fraîche (optional)
  • a pinch of grated nutmeg
  • salt and black pepper
Method
  1. Soften the onion, carrot, celery and garlic in the butter without browning.
  2. Add the stock (and the potato, chopped — the page lists it but does not say when it goes in), bring to the boil and simmer until the potato and carrot mash with a fork.
  3. Add the mallow leaves and simmer 5–10 minutes.
  4. Season with salt, pepper and nutmeg, purée with a hand blender, return to the pan, stir in the cream and warm through.
  5. Serve with a little yoghurt or cream and chopped herbs (chives, parsley, rosemary or thyme).
Dwarf stinging nettle Urtica urens 5 recipes

Classic stinging nettle soup

Serves 6

Written for common stinging nettles in North America (Urtica dioica 'and similar'); Forager Chef's nettle guide counts Urtica urens among the common nettles it covers.

Method
  1. Blanch about 225 g nettles in salted boiling water until just wilted, about 30 seconds, cool them in cold water, squeeze dry and finely chop a handful to finish the soup.
  2. Sweat 1 cup diced celery, 2 cups diced leeks, 2 diced shallots and 1 small diced onion in 3 tbsp butter over medium heat for 5 minutes.
  3. Add about 1 litre chicken stock and 2 cups diced potato and simmer until tender, about 15 minutes.
  4. Add the blanched nettles and blend, then stir in the chopped nettles and 120 ml cream and season with salt and white pepper.
  5. Serve with crème fraîche and chopped dill or mint.

Steamed nettles with ricotta

Serves 2

Written for common stinging nettles in North America (Urtica dioica 'and similar'); Forager Chef's nettle guide counts Urtica urens among the common nettles it covers.

Method
  1. Wash about 225 g young nettle tops and steam them, covered, over boiling water for 2–3 minutes until tender.
  2. Meanwhile warm 3 large crushed garlic cloves in 3 tbsp olive oil over medium heat for at least 10 minutes until browned, then discard the garlic.
  3. Add the steamed nettles to the oil, season with salt, pepper and a pinch of chilli flakes, gently fold in ½ cup ricotta and warm through.
  4. Serve warm or at room temperature with olive oil and lemon wedges.

Nettle spanakopita

Serves 6

Written for stinging nettle in Britain; the recipe does not name the species.

Method
  1. Brown 25 g butter in a large frying pan, add 150 g washed, still-damp nettle leaves and cook for about 6 minutes until wilted; cool, squeeze out the liquid and chop roughly.
  2. Mix with 200 g crumbled feta, 50 g grated parmesan, the zest and juice of a lemon, two-thirds of a beaten egg, grated nutmeg and seasoning.
  3. Heat the oven to 200 °C (180 °C fan).
  4. Lay 3 filo sheets end to end, overlapping by about 5 cm, brush with some of 75 g melted butter, add 3 more sheets and more butter, then a seventh sheet in the middle.
  5. Spoon the filling in a line about 2 cm wide along one long edge, tuck in the ends, roll into a long sausage and coil it into a spiral about 20 cm across in a shallow round pan or on a buttered baking tray.
  6. Brush with the rest of the egg, scatter with 1 tbsp sesame seeds and bake for 40–45 minutes until golden.

Nettle and blue cheese rarebit

Serves 4

Written for stinging nettle in Britain; the recipe does not name the species.

Method
  1. Wilt 200 g nettle leaves in 1 tbsp hot olive oil for 1 minute, cool briefly and chop roughly.
  2. Mix with a 200 ml tub of crème fraîche, 1 tsp wholegrain mustard, half of 140 g crumbled blue cheese, and seasoning.
  3. Toast 4 slices of sourdough on both sides under the grill, spread with the nettle mix, top with the rest of the cheese and grill until golden and bubbling.

Nettle saag

Written for stinging nettles in California; the recipe says Urtica dioica or U. urens will do.

Method
  1. Bring about 4 litres of water to the boil, add about 450 g nettle leaves stripped from their stems and boil for 5 minutes, then drain in a colander.
  2. In a frying pan, heat 4 tbsp ghee, olive oil or coconut oil and fry 4 finely chopped garlic cloves (or 1 tsp asafoetida), a 2.5 cm piece of finely chopped fresh ginger, 1 small finely chopped jalapeño or serrano chilli if you like, 1 tbsp turmeric powder, 1 tsp ground coriander and 1 tsp whole cumin seed.
  3. Add about 450 g coarsely chopped spinach or chard and stir to coat in the spices, then add the nettles and a few pinches of salt.
  4. Cover and cook, stirring every few minutes and adding a little water if it sticks, until the greens are soft but not mushy.
  5. Take off the heat, stir in 120 ml cream (or crème fraîche or coconut cream), adjust the salt and serve warm with dal and rice or chapati.
Elder (flowers & berries) Sambucus nigra 7 recipes

Elderflower cordial

No method written yet.

Elderflower fritters

No method written yet.

Elderberry jelly

No method written yet.

Pickled green elderberry capers

No method written yet.

Elderflower champagne

Method
  1. Pick 10–16 large elderflower heads in full bloom, on a sunny morning if you can.
  2. Shake out the insects but don't wash them.
  3. One tablespoon of dried elderflowers replaces one good-sized fresh head.
  4. Dissolve 650 g sugar in 4.5 litres of cold water — warm a little of the water to dissolve the sugar, cool it, then mix it into the rest.
  5. In a large bucket or bowl, combine the sweetened water with the flowers or leaves, the juice and quartered skin of 1 lemon, and 3 tbsp white wine or apple cider vinegar.
  6. Cover and leave for 4 days, somewhere cool; if mould forms, scrape off the mat of flowers on top and carry on.
  7. Fine-strain into clean screw-top plastic fizzy-drink bottles, leaving a few centimetres of air at the top.
  8. Never use corked bottles, and avoid glass flip-tops: you can't feel the pressure through them, and they are far more dangerous if they burst.
  9. Leave at room temperature for 6–14 days.
  10. From day 6, squeeze the bottles every day, and as soon as the bases start to round out, ease the caps to let off some (not all) of the pressure.
  11. A forgotten bottle can explode.
  12. Once it tastes right, move it to the fridge to slow the fermentation, and keep checking the pressure.
  13. It is lightly alcoholic after a week and starts turning vinegary at 5–6 weeks; the author drinks it between 2 and 4 weeks.
  14. The yeast comes from the flowers, so in a cool room it can take a while to get going.
  15. For a twist, the author sometimes uses an infused vinegar, such as pineapple weed vinegar.

The author uses whole heads, green stalks and all, as is traditional; to be careful, snip the flowers off the thick stalks.

Elderflower champagne vinegar

Method
  1. If a batch of elderflower champagne goes flat and vinegary, mix it with some live vinegar (with its mother).
  2. Leave it in an open container covered with cheesecloth, somewhere dark, for 2–3 months, until it reaches about 3–4% acidity.
  3. Use it for pickling.

Elderberry vinegar

Method
  1. Strip the ripe berries off every stalk — freezing the heads first makes this easier — and weigh them.
  2. For every 350 g of berries add 500 ml apple cider vinegar (or white wine vinegar) in a jar or tub.
  3. Cover and leave for 5 days, stirring now and then.
  4. Strain, pressing the berries, and throw them away.
  5. Add 350 g sugar for every 260 ml of liquid, simmer for 10 minutes, and bottle.
  6. The final simmer is essential: raw elderberries are not safe to eat.
  7. Less sugar works too, if you want it less sweet.
European barberry Berberis vulgaris 2 recipes

Barberry jelly

From a German herbal page about the common barberry (Berberis vulgaris).

Method
  1. Bring 1 kg washed berries to the boil with 500 ml water and simmer for about 20 minutes.
  2. Pour through a sieve and catch the juice.
  3. Mix the juice with 500 g jam-setting sugar of the 2:1 kind (Gelierzucker 2:1, sugar with added pectin for twice its weight of fruit), bring back to the boil, boil hard for about 4 minutes and pour into sterilised jars.

Barberry jam

Written for European barberry (Berberis vulgaris) by a forager who picks it wild from hedgerows in Vermont.

Method
  1. Put 450 g stemmed barberries (about 3 cups) in a saucepan with 250 ml water and simmer gently for about 5 minutes, mashing, until the berries fall apart and release their juice.
  2. Press through a fine sieve to remove the seeds, stalk bits and black flower remains; sieve again through a finer sieve or muslin for a smoother jam.
  3. Expect about 375 ml of pulp.
  4. Return it to a clean pan with the same volume of sugar (about 375 ml for 375 ml of pulp), bring to a rolling boil over high heat and boil, stirring all the time, for 2 to 3 minutes, until it starts to gel; test on a plate chilled in the freezer.
  5. It sets soft and spoonable.
  6. Ladle into two hot, sterilised 250 ml jars, leaving 6 mm headspace.
  7. Keep in the fridge or freezer, or process in a boiling water bath for 10 minutes (15 minutes above about 1,800 m) and leave undisturbed for 12 to 24 hours before checking the seals.
  8. Sealed jars keep 12 to 18 months; once opened, refrigerate and use within 2 to 3 weeks.
Fat hen Chenopodium album 3 recipes

Fat hen sauerkraut

Fat hen kimchi

No method written yet.

Wild Whangaroa pesto

Makes about 350 g

The author's greens, picked from roadsides around Whangaroa Harbour, were nasturtium, kawakawa, fennel, samphire, fat hen, wild mustard, cleavers and clover. The author warns that New Zealand roadsides are still sprayed with glyphosate, so pick with care.

Ingredients
  • 1 garlic clove (or 4 peeled onion weed bulbs)
  • 60 ml (¼ cup) macadamia nuts and 60 ml (¼ cup) grated parmesan
  • dairy or plant-based — or 125 ml (½ cup) macadamias and no parmesan — 750 ml (3 cups) roughly chopped wild greens
  • the zest and juice of 1 lemon
  • 125 ml extra-virgin olive oil
  • salt and pepper
Method
  1. Fat hen is eaten raw here.
  2. In a Lincoln University test of a raw fat hen pesto (22% leaves, with parmesan), the pesto held 58.5 mg of soluble oxalate per 100 g, about a quarter of its oxalate, because the leaves are diluted and the calcium in the parmesan binds much of the rest; the calcium in that test came from the parmesan (Savage & Vanhanen 2018).
  3. Blend in a food processor to the texture you like.
  4. Serve on crackers or toast, as a side, or stirred through soup, pasta or rice.
Feijoa Acca sellowiana 1 recipe

Dried feijoas

Method
  1. Cut ripe feijoas in half across their width and scoop each half out whole.
  2. Dip them in acidulated water (water with lemon juice) before drying.
  3. Lightly oil perforated trays (pizza trays with holes work) with coconut or a neutral oil such as rice bran so they don't stick, and arrange the halves cut side down with space between them, one kind of fruit to a tray.
  4. Dry in a 60 °C oven for about 12 hours, or in a dehydrator, then store in sterilised glass jars.
Field mushroom Agaricus campestris 2 recipes

Pickled button field mushrooms

No method written yet.

Field mushroom pâté

Makes about 350 g

The author made it with foraged field mushrooms (Agaricus campestris) and says any mushrooms will do.

Ingredients
  • 1 sliced onion
  • 500 ml (2 cups, 200 g) roughly chopped mushrooms
  • 2 sliced garlic cloves
  • 60 ml (¼ cup) extra-virgin olive oil
  • 15 ml (1 tbsp) balsamic reduction or brandy
  • the zest and juice of 1 lemon
  • 60 ml (¼ cup) walnuts
  • 60 ml (¼ cup) sunflower seeds
  • 15 ml (1 tbsp) tahini
  • 5 ml (1 tsp) smoked paprika
  • 5 ml (1 tsp) miso paste
  • 2.5 ml (½ tsp) salt
  • 1.25 ml (¼ tsp) cracked black pepper
Method
  1. Fry the onion in the oil over medium heat for about 10 minutes, until it starts to caramelise.
  2. Add the mushrooms and garlic and cook 5 minutes more, then the balsamic or brandy and the lemon, and cook a couple of minutes until no liquid is left.
  3. Let it cool.
  4. Pulse the walnuts and sunflower seeds in a food processor, add the mushroom mixture and the rest, and pulse to the texture you like.
  5. Line a ramekin with a beeswax wrap or cling film, edges overhanging, then with nasturtium leaves, flowers or herbs; pack in the pâté, fold the edges over and chill for 2 hours before turning it out.
  6. Or keep it in a jar in the fridge for up to a week.
Fishpole bamboo Phyllostachys aurea 1 recipe

Preparing fresh shoots

Method
  1. The husk is many overlapping brown layers covered in fine, sharp hairs, so handle it with that in mind.
  2. Run a knife up the side of the shoot to slit it, then unwrap and throw away the layers until you reach the pale core; cut off the pointed tip and the fibrous base.
  3. The sweetest shoots of the genus, with very little bitterness, and reported edible raw — but the warning on this page stands, so cook them unless you have reason to trust that claim.
  4. Cover with water, boil, change the water once, and simmer until tender; they need far less of this than the others.
  5. The canes are slender, about 15 mm, thicker in their native China.
  6. Cut them at about 8 cm above the ground, 5 cm below the soil.
Flowering currant Ribes sanguineum 5 recipes

Flowering currant syrup and cordial

Method
  1. Use the flowers and young leaves; with flowers alone the results turn pink.
  2. For a syrup, infuse them into a syrup of equal parts sugar and water by heating gently, without boiling.
  3. Cool, and keep in sterilised jars in the fridge for a couple of weeks.
  4. Pour it over puddings or add it to gin.
  5. For a cordial, sharpen the syrup to taste with lemon juice, citric acid or the flowering currant vinegar below; this also helps it keep.
  6. Dilute to drink.

Flowering currant vinegar and shrub

Method
  1. Use the flowers and young leaves; with flowers alone the results turn pink.
  2. Infuse them straight into cold white wine or apple cider vinegar for a week, then strain.
  3. Use it in salad dressings or as a pickling liquor — the author pickles magnolia petals and Japanese knotweed in it.
  4. Mix the vinegar with flowering currant syrup or cordial for a shrub, or with honey for an oxymel, to add fruity sourness to gin drinks.

Flowering currant liqueur

Method
  1. Use the flowers and young leaves; with flowers alone the results turn pink.
  2. Infuse them in vodka for no more than a week, strain, and sweeten to taste with flowering currant cordial, syrup or shrub.

Flowering currant champagne

The author's elderflower champagne with flowering currant flowers in place of the elderflowers. He gives no amount; for a subtler flower he says to use more plant material than the 10–16 elderflower heads.

Method

  5. Never use corked bottles, and avoid glass flip-tops: you can't feel the pressure through them, and they are far more dangerous if they burst.
  6. Leave at room temperature for 6–14 days.
  7. From day 6, squeeze the bottles every day, and as soon as the bases start to round out, ease the caps to let off some (not all) of the pressure.
  8. A forgotten bottle can explode.

Wild summer salad

Written for the author's mixed wild leaves.

Method
  1. Mix pungent and mild leaves about 1 to 3, then scatter a few extras over the top.
  2. Dress simply with walnut oil, elderberry vinegar, salt, pepper and a little mustard; he finishes it with fermented greens and pickled buds.

Of his lists, this catalogue covers dandelion and hairy bittercress (pungent), chickweed and young hawthorn leaves (mild), and gorse flowers and flowering currant blossom (extras). He warns to be extra careful identifying leaves before the plants flower.

French mallow Malva nicaeensis 1 recipe

Stuffed mallow leaves

Serves 8

Written for mallow in general; the page names no species. The author was told by Turkish cooks that large mallow leaves are used as wrappers for dolma, and went looking for the biggest-leaved mallows they could find.

Ingredients
  • Large mallow leaves
  • about 240 ml short- or medium-grain rice (not long-grain, which sets hard when cold)
  • ½ onion, finely chopped
  • about 225 g fresh mushrooms, finely chopped
  • about 80 ml pesto
  • 3 tbsp (45 ml) olive oil
  • salt
Method
  1. Prepare the leaves as you would grape leaves: boil them in salted water for a minute or two, cool them in iced water, and let them drip dry.
  2. Heat a large frying pan over high heat, add the mushrooms and shake the pan often until they give up their water.
  3. When most of it has gone, add the onion, oil and a little salt and fry until the onion starts to colour.
  4. Add the rice, toast it for a minute, then add about 240 ml water and a little salt, turn the heat to medium and cook until the water is absorbed.
  5. Take off the heat, let the rice cool, and stir in the pesto (with a little more oil if the pesto is dry).
  6. Lay a leaf smooth side down, base towards you, and put about a tablespoon of filling in a short log across the bottom, leaving at least 1 cm clear at each side.
  7. Fold the bottom up once, fold in the sides and roll up; leaves vary in size, so adjust the filling to suit.
  8. Pack the rolls seam side down and snug together in a steamer insert or on the bottom of a large pot, up to three layers deep; mallow rolls do not need weighing down.
  9. Add water to just below the rolls, cover, bring to the boil and steam gently for 40 minutes to an hour, until the rice is no longer crunchy.
  10. Serve warm or at room temperature; they keep a week in the fridge.
Giant puffball Calvatia gigantea 2 recipes

Giant puffball, fried or roasted whole

Method
  1. Thickly slice a giant puffball and fry the slices like steaks with bacon and garlic.
  2. Diced, it also makes a creamy addition to a mushroom risotto.

The author's favourite is to hollow out a smallish one, fill it with devilled kidneys, wrap it in bacon and roast it whole.

Ultimate wild mushroom risotto

Serves 4

Written for mixed wild mushrooms — the author's are puffball (giant or common), dried porcini (cep) and chanterelles.

Method
  1. Heat 2 litres of good chicken or vegetable stock in a separate pan and add 30 g dried porcini to soak and enrich it.
  2. Sweat 150 g chanterelles in a frying pan until they give up their juice, and strain the juice into the stock.
  3. In a heavy pan, sweat 1 finely diced large onion, 3 garlic cloves, 3 bay leaves, and 1 courgette and 1 small leek (both finely diced) in 50 g of 100 g butter until soft and translucent.
  4. Add a few sprigs of thyme and 300 g unwashed carnaroli or arborio rice and stir over a medium heat for a couple of minutes.
  5. Add 300 ml dry white wine and keep stirring as it absorbs.
  6. Add 150 g puffball, peeled and cut into 1 cm cubes, and the soaked porcini with a little hot stock.
  7. Keep stirring briskly, adding the stock a ladleful at a time; when about half is in, add the chanterelles.
  8. Season, and stop when the rice keeps a hint of bite (no chalkiness) and the risotto is like a very thick soup — you may not need all the stock.
  9. Off the heat, stir in half of 300 g finely grated parmesan, the remaining 50 g butter, 75 ml double cream and a handful of finely chopped parsley and tarragon.
  10. Cover and rest for a few minutes.
  11. Stir in the juice of 1 lemon, check the seasoning and add a little more stock if needed.
  12. Serve in shallow bowls with a few drops of truffle oil, the rest of the cheese on the side, and, if you like, a sprinkle of hairy bittercress for a sharp note.
Gorse Ulex europaeus 6 recipes

Gorse flower wine

No method written yet.

Gorse-infused vinegar

No method written yet.

Gorse flower tea

No method written yet.

Gorse flower cordial

Ingredients
  • 500 ml (2 cups) gorse flowers
  • 1 litre water
  • 375 ml (1½ cups) sugar
  • 15 ml (1 tbsp) grated lemon
  • lime and/or orange zest
  • the juice of 1 lemon
  • lime or orange
Method
  1. Pick the flowers on a sunny day — the author finds them a little bitter otherwise — plucking small clusters gently; gloves help with the spines.
  2. Bring the sugar, water and zest to the boil and simmer for 5 minutes.
  3. Take it off the heat, stir in the juice and the flowers, and leave until cool, or overnight.
  4. Strain through muslin into a sterilised bottle, cap it and keep it in the fridge.
  5. To keep it longer than a month, bring it back to a simmer and pour it into a warm sterilised bottle; it seals as it cools.
  6. To drink, stir 45–60 ml (3–4 tbsp) into 500 ml of hot, cold or fizzy water.

Gorse flower jelly cups

Ingredients
  • 500 ml (2 cups) gorse flower cordial (the recipe above)
  • 500 ml (2 cups) water — or 250 ml water and 250 ml coconut milk or cream — 10 ml (2 tsp) agar powder
  • the juice of 1 lime (optional)
Method
  1. Put the cordial and water in a pot and sprinkle in the agar.
  2. Bring it to the boil, stirring so the agar dissolves evenly, then simmer for 2 minutes.
  3. Take it off the heat, add the lime juice and pour into cups, small bowls or ramekins.
  4. It sets at room temperature as it cools, or in the fridge.
  5. Serve with coconut yoghurt or whipped cream.
  6. Covered in the fridge, the jellies keep 1–2 weeks.

Wild summer salad

Method

  2. Dress simply with walnut oil, elderberry vinegar, salt, pepper and a little mustard; he finishes it with fermented greens and pickled buds.

Greater plantain Plantago major 3 recipes

Kopakopa chips

For 24 large leaves

Written for kopakopa (greater plantain).

Ingredients
  • 10 ml (2 tsp) olive oil
  • 1.25 ml (¼ tsp) salt
  • 2.5 ml (½ tsp) seasoning, such as a Moroccan spice mix or garlic powder
Method
  1. Heat the oven to 150 °C.
  2. Wash the leaves and dry them well, then toss them in the oil until coated.
  3. Spread them in a single layer on a baking tray, sprinkle with the salt and seasoning, and bake for about 10 minutes, until crisp.

Spiced plantain seed mix

Written for plantain seeds, greater or narrow-leaved.

Method
  1. Mix 1 handful of plantain seeds with 3 handfuls of pumpkin seeds and 3 of sesame seeds.
  2. Toast them lightly in a non-stick frying pan, then add a generous splash of tamari or soy sauce, toss to coat, and let any excess cook off.
  3. Tip into bowls and eat as a snack.

Plantain side dish

Written for greater plantain (Plantago major).

Method
  1. Boil the leaves in salted water for 4 minutes, then drop them straight into iced water.
  2. Drain, and dress with 15 ml (1 tbsp) sesame oil, 15 ml (1 tbsp) soy sauce, 1 garlic clove and a sprinkle of sesame seeds.
  3. Mix and let it rest for 5 minutes.
  4. The leaves come out with something of the texture and translucence of seaweed.
Ground-ivy Glechoma hederacea 5 recipes

Ground-ivy tempura

Serves 3

Method
  1. Pick the leaves off 2 handfuls of ground-ivy.
  2. In a chilled bowl, mix 150 g plain flour, 1 tbsp cornflour, 1 egg, 300 ml cold water and a pinch of salt into a thin batter.
  3. Heat 100 g rapeseed oil until very hot.
  4. Dip the leaves one at a time until well coated, fry until lightly crisp, and drain on kitchen paper.
  5. Serve with soy sauce, honey or both.

Ground-ivy, butternut squash and bacon quiche

Serves 6

Method
  1. Toss 200 g butternut squash, in 1 cm dice, with ½ tbsp olive oil, salt and pepper, add 100 g diced bacon, and roast on a tray at 180 °C for 20–25 minutes, turning halfway, until the squash is soft and the bacon crisp; let it cool.
  2. Beat 3 eggs with 300 ml double cream.
  3. Keep back about 12 leaves from a handful of ground-ivy and roughly chop the rest.
  4. Spread the squash and bacon over a blind-baked pastry case, scatter over the chopped ground-ivy and 100 g grated cheddar, pour in the egg and cream, and dot the kept leaves on top.
  5. Bake at 180 °C for about 25 minutes, until golden and just set, and let it cool to warm before serving.

Ground-ivy ale

Makes about 15 litres

Method
  1. Mash 2.3 kg malted barley in water at 65 °C for 90 minutes, then sparge, slowly adding boiling water until 15 litres in all has gone in.
  2. Add 85 g dried ground-ivy and boil for an hour.
  3. Strain, stir in 900 g brown sugar until it dissolves, cool to 21 °C, pour into the fermenter and add the yeast.
  4. Ferment for about a week, until it has finished, then siphon into bottles each primed with ½ tsp sugar, cap and store.
  5. It is ready to drink in 10 days to two weeks.

Ground-ivy vinegar

Method
  1. Fill a bottle of apple cider vinegar with plenty of fresh ground-ivy — leaves, stems and flowers — and leave for a day or more.
  2. No heating is needed.

The author keeps a 5-litre bottle going and uses it as the vinegar in his pickles, especially pickled mushrooms.

Ground-ivy shrub

Method
  1. Half fill a jar with ground-ivy leaves and top up with apple cider vinegar.
  2. Leave for at least 24 hours, better 48.
  3. Strain, and mix with a sugar syrup, starting at about 60 parts vinegar to 40 syrup and adjusting until it is sharp but not harsh.
  4. Use the shrub in cocktails or as a pickling liquor.

The author's syrup is burdock root, washed and finely sliced, steeped for at least 24 hours in a syrup of 2 parts sugar to 1 part water.

Hedge woundwort Stachys sylvatica 4 recipes

Hedge woundwort velouté

Written for Stachys sylvatica in Belgium.

Method
  1. Chop 2 large onions, and peel and chop 2 large potatoes.
  2. Roughly chop 3 big handfuls of washed hedge woundwort leaves.
  3. Soften the onions in 1 tbsp butter for a few minutes, add the potatoes and cook 5 minutes over a medium heat, then add the leaves for 30 seconds and stir in 1 tbsp flour.
  4. Add a stock cube and pour in milk, stirring, then simmer gently for 15 minutes, stirring now and then and adding a little water if it gets too thick.
  5. Blend for 1–2 minutes until smooth, add a little cream if you like, and season.
  6. Croutons, pumpkin or sunflower seeds, or the flowers make a garnish.

Hedge woundwort cream sauce

Written for Stachys sylvatica in Belgium.

Method
  1. Finely chop 3 shallots and sweat them in 50 ml olive oil in a small pan.
  2. Add 300 ml chicken stock and let it reduce, then pour in 300 ml crème fraîche, heat through and season.
  3. Add 2 good handfuls of washed, drained hedge woundwort leaves, cook 5 minutes, then blend.
  4. Serve with chicken, fish or pasta.

Hedge woundwort and seed spread

Written for Stachys sylvatica in Belgium.

Method
  1. The night before, soak 50 g sunflower seeds and 50 g walnut halves in water; drain them the next day.
  2. Wash 2 handfuls of young hedge woundwort leaves well and chop them finely.
  3. Fry 1 small onion for a few minutes, add the leaves and cook about 5 minutes.
  4. Blend everything with 2 tbsp rapeseed oil and a pinch each of flaky salt and pepper.
  5. Keep it in a jar in the fridge and spread it on toast or bread like a vegetable pâté.

Hedge woundwort malfatti

Serves 4

Written for Stachys sylvatica in Belgium.

Method
  1. Finely chop 130 g of young hedge woundwort leaves and soften them for a few minutes, steamed or in a pan with a little olive oil; squeeze out as much liquid as you can and let them cool.
  2. Bring 200 ml cream (or plant cream) to the boil with the remaining 20 g of leaves, blend, season and leave to infuse.
  3. Mix the chopped leaves with 350 g ricotta, 1 egg, 60 g grated parmesan, 60 g flour, salt and pepper, and chill 15 minutes until firm.
  4. With wet hands, roll walnut-sized balls, coat them in flour and lay them on a floured cloth.
  5. Drop them one at a time into a large pan of boiling salted water and lift them out with a slotted spoon as soon as they rise, like gnocchi.
  6. Serve under the hot sauce with more parmesan and black pepper.
Henbit deadnettle Lamium amplexicaule 3 recipes

Spicy henbit

Written for henbit in the United States.

Method
  1. Chop about 4 cups of henbit shoots, cover with water and boil for 10 minutes.
  2. In another pan melt 3 tbsp butter with 1 tsp curry powder, 2 whole cloves and ¼ tsp ground cinnamon, cook for a minute, stir in 2 tbsp flour and cook a minute more.
  3. Whisk in ½ cup (120 ml) of the henbit's cooking water until smooth, then add the drained henbit and ¾ cup (180 ml) sour cream and cook on low for 15 minutes.

Henbit noodles with mushroom sauce

Serves 4

Written for henbit.

Method
  1. Wilt about 225 g henbit leaves in ¼ cup water over medium heat, about 5 minutes; drain, cool under cold water and squeeze dry.
  2. Purée with 1 garlic clove, 2 eggs and 1 tbsp olive oil.
  3. Mix 1 cup plain flour (keep another ½ cup for dusting) with ¾ cup semolina, make a well, work in the purée and knead for 10 minutes into a dough that no longer sticks.
  4. Rest it under a damp cloth for 30 minutes, roll each quarter thin, roll up loosely and cut into noodles 6–12 mm wide.
  5. For the sauce, cook 1 cup chopped mushrooms in 2 tbsp butter with salt until their liquid has evaporated, stir in 2 tbsp flour for 2 minutes, then add 1 cup mushroom or other stock a splash at a time with a little thyme until thick; finish with ½ cup light cream.
  6. Boil the noodles in salted water for 3 minutes, drain, toss with the sauce and ¼ cup grated parmesan, and serve with more cheese.

Cannellini bean and henbit soup

Serves about 6

Written for henbit in Tennessee.

Method
  1. Fry 4 chopped garlic cloves and 1 chopped onion in 2 tbsp olive oil until translucent, about 10 minutes.
  2. Add 6 cups (about 1.5 litres) chicken or vegetable stock and bring to the boil, then add a 440 g can of cannellini beans, drained and rinsed, and 1 cup small pasta and boil until the pasta is tender, 8–10 minutes.
  3. Add 1½ cups chopped henbit and cook a few minutes until wilted.
  4. Season and serve with parmesan.
Herb robert Geranium robertianum 1 recipe

Herb robert and basil pesto

Method
  1. Blend 30 g herb robert leaves, 30 g basil leaves and 1 crushed garlic clove with enough olive oil to make a loose purée.
  2. Stir in 40 g finely grated Parmesan or Pecorino and the juice of half a lemon, then season with salt and black pepper to taste.
