Horse mushroom Agaricus arvensis 1 recipe

Horse mushrooms stuffed with sweet cicely

Serves 2 as a starter

Method
  1. Carefully twist the stems out of 2 large horse mushrooms — still domed, with pale gills and firm caps — without damaging the caps, and chop the stems finely.
  2. Fry them gently with 2 finely diced shallots in a little butter for 4 minutes.
  3. Stuff the caps, set them on a baking tray, season, drizzle with a little olive oil and bake in a medium oven for 10 minutes.
  4. Meanwhile toast 2 slices of sourdough and sauté any other mushrooms you found.
  5. Put a cap on each toast (on a slice of a big mushroom, if you have one) and dress with the smaller mushrooms and a few wild herbs and flowers.

Finely shred a handful of sweet cicely leaves (the author says tarragon works instead), keeping a few fronds to garnish, and mix with the stems, shallots and 2 tbsp cream cheese; season.

Japanese cherry Prunus serrulata 7 recipes

Preserved cherry blossoms (sakura no shiozuke) and sakura vinegar

Makes about 1 cup of flowers and 240 ml of vinegar

Ingredients
  • About 50 g cherry blossoms (around 5 very loosely packed cups)
  • 2 tsp flaky or fine sea salt
  • 240 ml rice vinegar and
  • if you have it
  • 2 tsp umezu (Japanese plum vinegar)
Method
  1. Pick from an unsprayed tree, choosing flowers that are just starting to open.
  2. Soak them gently in clean water, stirring lightly with your hands, then lay them out to dry or give them a quick, gentle spin in a salad spinner.
  3. Layer the flowers in a small jar, sprinkling each layer with salt, and refrigerate for 3 days.
  4. Cover with the vinegar and refrigerate for another 3 days.
  5. To keep them as a pickle, simply leave the flowers in the vinegar in the fridge; they keep 6 months or more but fade.
  6. To dry them, drain off and keep the vinegar, then spread the flowers on a tray lined with a soft cloth in a warm, dry place out of direct sun, checking once a day — about three days on average — or dry them in a dehydrator at about 35 °C for 6–8 hours.
  7. Keep the heat low, or they turn brown.
  8. Flowers left a little moist can be tossed with a further 5 g (1 tsp) sea salt.
  9. Dried blossoms keep at room temperature, out of direct sun, for about a year; the pink sakura vinegar keeps in the fridge for 6 months or more and is salty, so use it like a seasoned vinegar, for example in sushi rice.

Cherry blossom syrup

Ingredients
  • A handful of clean
  • fresh cherry blossoms with the stalks removed
  • water and white sugar
Method
  1. Put the blossoms in a dish, pour over enough water just off the boil to cover them, cover the dish and leave to infuse for 24 hours.
  2. Strain the liquid through a fine sieve, muslin or a paper filter into a small pan and add the same quantity of sugar — 500 g sugar to 500 ml of liquid.
  3. Heat gently until the sugar dissolves, bring to the boil and simmer for 3 to 5 minutes.
  4. Funnel into a sterilised bottle and seal.
  5. Once opened, keep it in the fridge and use within 2 months.
  6. Use it as you would a subtle orgeat or maraschino, in drinks.

Quick cherry blossom syrup

Ingredients
  • About 200 g (1 cup) sugar
  • 240 ml (1 cup) water
  • 1 cup of fresh cherry blossoms
Method
  1. Rinse the blossoms, put everything in a pan, bring to a boil and stir for 10–15 minutes, until it turns syrupy.
  2. Strain out the blossoms and pour the syrup into a jar.
  3. The blossoms turn green in heat, so the syrup comes out light green; the author suggests taking every bit of green stem off the petals if you mind the colour.
  4. Good in iced or hot tea, drizzled over yoghurt, or soaked into a cake.

Cherry blossom vinegar

Ingredients
  • About 2 cups of fresh cherry blossoms
  • about 475 ml raw apple cider vinegar
Method
  1. Pick flower buds and open blooms from a tree you know has not been sprayed or contaminated, leaving the leaves behind — the author advises against harvesting leaves with the flowers.
  2. Fill a glass jar about two-thirds full of flowers and top up with the vinegar.
  3. If the jar has a metal lid, put a piece of parchment or wax paper between jar and lid so the vinegar does not eat the metal.
  4. Leave to infuse for at least 2–4 weeks; pink blossoms turn it a golden fuchsia.
  5. Strain, and use it in salad dressings, marinades, soda water or cocktails.

Sakura martini

For one drink

Ingredients
  • 75 ml sake
  • 15 ml dry gin
  • 15 ml of the cherry blossom vodka
  • 1 tsp cherry blossom syrup
  • a salted cherry blossom (or a small pinch of salt)
Method
  1. First infuse a handful of clean, fresh cherry blossoms in vodka for a couple of days, then fine-strain.
  2. Stir with ice, strain, and garnish with a fresh or salted cherry blossom.

Cherry blossom mirin

Method
  1. Cherry blossoms and good mirin.
  2. Put the blossoms in a jar, cover them with mirin and leave for four days, then strain out the flowers and keep the mirin in the fridge.

The author drizzles it over mild, crunchy salads and spoons it over silken tofu as a dessert.

Cucumber and cherry blossom salad

Ingredients
  • 1 Japanese or 5 Persian cucumbers
  • 1 tbsp (15 ml) sesame seeds toasted in a pan
  • 3 tbsp (45 ml) cherry blossom mirin (above)
  • 2 tsp (10 ml) rice vinegar
  • ¼ tsp sea salt
  • cherry blossoms to scatter
Method
  1. Crush the cucumbers lightly with the back of a knife or a rolling pin, break them into chunks and put them in a bowl.
  2. Roughly crush the sesame seeds in a mortar.
  3. Whisk the mirin, vinegar and salt together in a small bowl, add them to the cucumber with the sesame seeds and toss well.
  4. Turn onto a serving dish, scatter over the blossoms and serve at once.
  5. The salad can be left to macerate in the mirin mixture for an hour to develop its flavour, but add the sesame seeds and blossoms just before serving.
Japanese knotweed Reynoutria japonica 3 recipes

Knotweed purée

Method
  1. Pick young shoots under 50 cm long.
  2. Serve with Greek yoghurt, or make a fool.
  3. Cook or burn every trimming, or bag it for the transfer station — never compost it (see Warnings).
  4. Avoid patches that may have been sprayed; the author watches a patch for at least a year before eating from it.

The author doesn't peel them: he chops the whole shoot, stews it with a little sugar and water until soft, and passes it through a fine sieve. He also says it is high in oxalic acid and to eat it sparingly if you have a kidney complaint or have been told to avoid spinach or rhubarb.

Knotweed juice

Method
  1. Juice whole raw young stems.
  2. Drink the green juice in smoothies, or pass it through a fine cloth for a clear purple juice — the author uses it as a gin mixer.

The author composts the pulp; don't — cook or burn it, or bag it for the transfer station (see Warnings).

Knotweed and sweet cicely vodka

Method
  1. Fill a jar a third full with chopped young knotweed shoots, then to halfway with chopped sweet cicely seeds, leaves and stems, and top up with vodka.
  2. Leave for at least 2 months, shaking daily for the first 2 weeks, then strain.
  3. Sweeten to taste after steeping rather than before.
  4. Cook or burn every trimming, or bag it for the transfer station — never compost it (see Warnings).
Japanese mahonia Berberis japonica 1 recipe

Mahonia berry oat bars

Written for the berries of Japanese and leatherleaf mahonia picked in Victoria, British Columbia.

Ingredients
Base
  • 375 ml flour
  • 440 ml rolled oats
  • 125 ml chopped walnuts
  • 250 ml packed dark brown or coconut sugar
  • 1 tsp baking powder
  • ½ tsp fine salt
  • 1 tsp vanilla
  • 250 ml melted butter
Topping
  • 625 ml mahonia berries
  • 250 ml sugar
  • 2 tsp grated orange or lemon zest
  • two lavender buds
Method
  1. Heat the oven to 180°C and line a 20–23 cm square tin with baking paper, leaving it overhanging the sides.
  2. Stir the dry ingredients together, then work in the melted butter until crumbly.
  3. Press three-quarters of it firmly into the tin and bake for 10 minutes.
  4. Cook the berries with the sugar, zest and lavender over low heat for 15 minutes, until they pop open; cool for 5 minutes and take out the lavender.
  5. Pour over the base, scatter the rest of the crumble on top and bake for 30–40 minutes, until golden.
  6. Cool completely in the tin, lift out by the paper and cut into 12 large bars or 24 squares.
Karengo Pyropia / Porphyra spp. 6 recipes

Crisped karengo

No method written yet.

Karengo salt

No method written yet.

Karengo pudding

No method written yet.

Laverbread

Written for laver (Porphyra), the European relative of karengo; the author's are purple, tough and winter laver.

Method
  1. Rinse the laver thoroughly of sand and silt.
  2. Put it in a pan with fresh water — just a little if it is fresh or already soaked, as it gives off plenty more, but more if it is dried.
  3. Cover and keep it at a bare simmer for about 3 hours, adding water so it never boils dry; a little apple cider vinegar shortens the cooking and helps it keep.
  4. When very soft, drain and squeeze it, keeping the liquid: reduced further, it is a rich stock to freeze in ice-cube trays for soups, sauces and glazes.
  5. The paste left behind is laverbread.
  6. Spread it on toast, fry it into patties (with or without oats), or use it in stews, soups, tarts and pâtés.
  7. It doesn't keep well, so freeze what you won't use soon in small tubs.

Laverbread patties

Written for laver (Porphyra), the European relative of karengo; the author's are purple, tough and winter laver.

Method
  1. Blitz cooked, drained laverbread (see the laverbread recipe), then stir in pinhead oatmeal — between half and an equal volume of oatmeal to laver — until thick enough to shape.
  2. Season to taste with soy sauce, miso, dried sea lettuce or chopped fried mushrooms, and add a squeeze of lemon juice for acidity.
  3. Leave for at least an hour, better overnight, so the oatmeal swells.
  4. Shape into patties with wet hands and shallow-fry in hot (not smoking) fat — bacon fat is best — pressing them flat with a spatula until the oatmeal bronzes into a crust.
  5. They freeze well once cooled and reheat in the oven.

Toasted laver

Written for laver (Porphyra), the European relative of karengo; the author's are purple, tough and winter laver.

Method
  1. Simply dried, laver is chewy and awkward to use.
  2. Toast dried laver briefly in the oven or a dry frying pan until it turns slightly green and tastes of nori.
  3. Tossing it first in a little runny honey, sesame oil and sesame seeds makes it even better.
  4. Don't over-toast it: it burns very quickly and turns bitter.

Onion weed, kawakawa and kelp super salt

Makes about 200 g

Written for onion weed, kawakawa and kelp together.

Ingredients
  • 250 ml (1 cup) onion weed stems and leaves, sliced 5 mm thick
  • 8–10 kawakawa leaves
  • 2 hand-sized pieces of kelp or other seaweed (the author forages hers, and says bought seaweed works)
  • 125 ml (½ cup) rock salt
Method
  1. If you can, dry the kawakawa for a few days beforehand.
  2. Spread each ingredient in a single layer on its own tray, because they dry at different speeds — in a dehydrator, or on a lined perforated tray in a 50–60 °C oven — until crisp to the touch, 2 to 4 hours.
  3. Sprinkle it on anything, or use it in place of stock.

Grind them with the salt in batches, in a spice grinder or with a mortar and pestle, to the texture you like, and keep it in a sealed jar; the author says it lasts indefinitely.

Wild Whangaroa pesto

Makes about 350 g

The author's greens, picked from roadsides around Whangaroa Harbour, were nasturtium, kawakawa, fennel, samphire, fat hen, wild mustard, cleavers and clover. The author warns that New Zealand roadsides are still sprayed with glyphosate, so pick with care.

  • dairy or plant-based — or 125 ml (½ cup) macadamias and no parmesan — 750 ml (3 cups) roughly chopped wild greens
  • the zest and juice of 1 lemon
  • 125 ml extra-virgin olive oil

Method

  2. Serve on crackers or toast, as a side, or stirred through soup, pasta or rice.
Larch Larix decidua 1 recipe

Larch needle cordial

Written for European larch. The author makes it by his nettle cordial method, with young larch needles in place of the nettle.

Method
  1. Pick the young needles in spring, while they are bright, almost luminous green and taste sharp, like sorrel.
  2. Make a syrup of equal parts water and white sugar (for example 50 ml water to 50 g sugar), heating gently and stirring until the sugar dissolves.
  3. Add at least a cupful (about 250 ml) of needles, pressed down, or more.
  4. Keep it on the heat until steaming hot, but don't let it boil.
  5. Leave to cool and strain.
  6. Stir in a little citric acid or lemon juice, or a dash of white wine or cider vinegar, until it tastes slightly sharp.
  7. It keeps for several weeks in the fridge.
Least yellow sorrel Oxalis exilis 12 recipes

Wood sorrel sauce

Ingredients
  • 500 ml (2 cups) wood sorrel leaves
  • 30 ml (2 tbsp) butter
  • 7.5 ml (1½ tsp) lemon juice
  • 7.5 ml (1½ tsp) maple syrup or honey
Method
  1. Melt the butter in a saucepan, add the sorrel and stir for 1–2 minutes, until it wilts.
  2. Put it in a blender or food processor with the lemon juice and the syrup or honey and blend until well combined.
  3. Serve it straight away, or refrigerate it and warm it before use; the author finds it even better the next day.

The author serves it on fish, or as a dipping sauce for grilled prawns.

Wood sorrel 'lemonade'

Ingredients
  • About 500 ml (2 cups) wood sorrel leaves, stems or flowers, or a mix
  • about 250 ml (1 cup) water
  • maple syrup or another sweetener to taste
Method
  1. The amounts are rough: use as much sorrel as you like and enough water to cover it.
  2. Blend the sorrel and water until well combined, strain through a fine sieve and sweeten to taste.

Wood sorrel syrup

Ingredients
  • Equal parts wood sorrel
  • sugar and water

The author usually uses 250 ml (1 cup) of each.

Method
  1. Put everything in a saucepan and bring it to a simmer, stirring.
  2. Simmer for 15–30 minutes, strain through muslin (cheesecloth) and pour into sterilised bottles.
  3. Use it in tea, topped up with soda water, over ice cream or fresh strawberries, or in vinaigrettes and marinades.

Wood sorrel cooler

Ingredients
  • About 950 ml (1 US quart) water
  • 125 ml (½ cup) wood sorrel leaves, stems
  • flowers and seed pods
  • 15 ml (1 tbsp) agave nectar or honey
  • a dash of salt
Method
  1. Mix everything in a blender.
  2. If you can, leave it in the fridge overnight before drinking.

Wood sorrel tea

Method
  1. Fresh wood sorrel leaves and boiling water, one part leaves to three parts water.
  2. Put the leaves in a container, pour the boiling water over them and leave to steep for 5–15 minutes.
  3. Strain out the leaves and sweeten to taste if you like.
  4. Drink it hot, or pour it over ice for an iced tea.

Chilled lemony wood sorrel soup

Ingredients
  • 2 cloves garlic, minced
  • olive oil for cooking
  • 2 spring onions, chopped
  • 500 ml (2 cups) vegetable stock
  • 500 ml (2 cups) lightly packed wood sorrel leaves, with the flowers kept back for garnish
  • 3 medium mint leaves
  • 250 ml (1 cup) full-fat coconut milk
  • salt and pepper
Method
  1. Rinse the sorrel well and let it dry, then strip the leaves and flowers from the stems and throw away the bare stalks.
  2. Heat a little olive oil in a small saucepan and sauté the garlic until fragrant, then add the spring onions.
  3. Cover with the stock, bring to the boil and simmer for about 5 minutes, then add the sorrel for about 30 seconds.
  4. Take it off the heat, add the mint and blend smooth with a stick blender (or an ordinary blender).
  5. Stir in the coconut milk and season with salt and pepper.
  6. Refrigerate until well chilled, a couple of hours, and serve garnished with the sorrel flowers.

Spaghetti squash casserole with wood sorrel

Ingredients
  • About 450 g (1 lb) Italian or breakfast sausage
  • 1 medium spaghetti squash
  • several cups of wood sorrel, washed and drained, stems removed (the recipe gives no closer amount)
  • olive oil
  • 1 red onion, halved and sliced
  • 1 egg
  • 125 ml (½ cup) coconut milk or any milk
  • 1 garlic clove, minced
  • 15 ml (1 tbsp) garlic powder
  • 5 ml (1 tsp) tarragon
  • 5 ml (1 tsp) salt
  • 2.5 ml (½ tsp) black pepper
  • 1.25 ml (¼ tsp) ground sage
Method
  1. Heat the oven to 200 °C.
  2. Halve the squash, spoon out the seeds and stringy threads, set the halves cut side up on a baking tray, drizzle with olive oil, season with salt and pepper and bake for 20–25 minutes, until the flesh gives a little when pressed.
  3. Meanwhile cook the sausage and onion in a large pot over medium-high heat.
  4. When the sausage is cooked through, add the garlic and sauté for 2 minutes, then add the sorrel, milk and spices, cover and simmer until the sorrel wilts.
  5. Take the squash out of the oven and scrape out the flesh with a fork (careful, it will be hot).
  6. Combine the mixture with the egg, stirring thoroughly, spread it evenly in a baking dish (or the same pot, if it is ovenproof) and bake for 15–20 minutes, until the top is lightly browned.

Wood sorrel, kūmara and potato gratin

  • Two handfuls of wood sorrel, chopped
  • potato and kūmara, boiled first and sliced
  • cream
Method
  1. Layer the sorrel with the sliced potato and kūmara in a baking dish, pour cream over the top and bake at 180 °C until it starts to brown.

Potato salad with wood sorrel

Method
  1. Chop a big handful of wood sorrel leaves, stalks and flowers and mix them through a potato salad dressed with a cream dressing.

Wild weed spread

Makes about 600 g

The author makes it with chickweed, oxalis and onion weed together; the page does not say which oxalis (written for oxalis generally).

Ingredients
  • 500 ml (2 cups) raw cashews
  • 500 ml hot water
  • 80 ml (⅓ cup) extra-virgin olive oil
  • 1 garlic clove
  • the zest and juice of 1 lemon
  • 30 ml (2 tbsp) apple cider vinegar
  • 60 ml (¼ cup) nutritional yeast (or finely grated parmesan, or cream cheese)
  • 625 ml (2½ cups) wild greens, roughly chopped and tightly packed
  • 15 ml (1 tbsp) chopped thyme
  • dill
  • coriander or parsley
  • 5 ml (1 tsp) salt
  • 2.5 ml (½ tsp) pepper
Method
  1. Soak the cashews in the hot water for an hour, then drain them and throw the water away.
  2. Blend everything together until very smooth.
  3. The mixture is stiff, like cream cheese: a stick blender in a jug works best, and a jug blender may need a little water.
  4. Spread it on toast or crackers, or use it in sandwiches and burgers.

Spoon it into a jar or dish with a lid, pour a thin layer of olive oil over the top so it does not discolour, and keep it covered in the fridge; the author says it keeps for a couple of weeks.

Green goddess dip

Makes about 500 ml; it thickens in the fridge

The author's greens were nasturtium, chickweed, onion weed, dandelion, oxalis, dock, plantain and wild mustard; the page does not say which oxalis (written for oxalis generally).

Ingredients
  • 500 ml (2 cups) roughly chopped wild greens
  • 125 ml (½ cup) cashews, soaked for an hour in the hottest tap water and drained
  • the zest and juice of 1 lemon
  • 60 ml (¼ cup) apple cider vinegar
  • 60 ml (¼ cup) water, only if your blender needs it
  • 2 peeled garlic cloves
  • 60 ml (¼ cup) extra-virgin olive oil
  • 60 ml (¼ cup) hemp seed oil (or more olive oil)
  • 5 ml (1 tsp) salt
  • 1.25 ml (¼ tsp) pepper
  • 30 ml (2 tbsp) nutritional yeast (optional)
Method
  1. Blend in a high-speed blender until smooth.
  2. Use it as a dip or dressing — with chips, steamed potatoes or kūmara, roast vegetables, an omelette or quiche, or stirred through pasta.

Wild greens soup

Serves 4

The author's greens were puha, sow thistle, nasturtium, onion weed, wild fennel, oxalis, wild mustard and wild celery; the page does not say which oxalis (written for oxalis generally).

Ingredients
  • 1 diced onion
  • 1 potato, scrubbed and diced
  • 2 sliced garlic cloves
  • 500 ml (2 cups) roughly chopped wild greens
  • 45 ml (3 tbsp) olive oil
  • 1.3 litres water
  • 5 ml (1 tsp) salt
  • 30 ml (2 tbsp) nutritional yeast (optional)
  • 1.25 ml (¼ tsp) cracked pepper
Method
  1. Cut the vegetables and greens to similar sizes.
  2. Fry the onion in the oil for a minute, then add the potato and garlic (the method also adds fennel bulb and celery stalks, which the ingredient list leaves out) and fry for another minute.
  3. Add the water, cover, bring to the boil and simmer until the potato is soft.
  4. Add the greens with the salt and yeast and cook for 3 minutes, until just tender — added at the end, they keep their colour.
  5. Blend until smooth (push it through a sieve if your blender isn't powerful), season with the pepper and more salt if needed, and serve drizzled with extra-virgin olive or hemp seed oil and scattered with edible weed flowers.

Lemon balm Melissa officinalis 6 recipes

Lemon balm syrup and cordial

Method
  1. Use the tender leaves.
  2. For a syrup, infuse them into a syrup of equal parts sugar and water by heating gently, without boiling.
  3. Cool, and keep in sterilised jars in the fridge for a couple of weeks.
  4. Pour it over puddings or use it in cocktails.
  5. For a cordial, sharpen the syrup to taste with lemon juice, citric acid or the lemon balm vinegar below; it keeps better and gains a pleasing sharpness.
  6. Dilute to drink, or use it for a gimlet.

Lemon balm vinegar and shrub

Method
  1. Use the tender leaves.
  2. Infuse them straight into cold apple cider vinegar for a week, then strain.
  3. Use it in salad dressings or as a pickling liquor.
  4. Mix the vinegar with lemon balm syrup or cordial for a shrub, or with honey for an oxymel, to give cocktails a gentle acidity.

Lemon balm liqueur

Method
  1. Use the tender leaves.
  2. Infuse them in vodka for no more than 3 days, strain, and sweeten to taste with lemon balm cordial, syrup or shrub.

Lemon balm champagne

The author's elderflower champagne with lemon balm leaves in place of the elderflowers, or added to them. He gives no amount for the leaves.

Method
  1. Dissolve 650 g sugar in 4.5 litres of cold water — warm a little of the water to dissolve the sugar, cool it, then mix it into the rest.
  2. In a large bucket or bowl, combine the sweetened water with the flowers or leaves, the juice and quartered skin of 1 lemon, and 3 tbsp white wine or apple cider vinegar.
  3. Cover and leave for 4 days, somewhere cool; if mould forms, scrape off the mat of flowers on top and carry on.
  4. Fine-strain into clean screw-top plastic fizzy-drink bottles, leaving a few centimetres of air at the top.
  5. Never use corked bottles, and avoid glass flip-tops: you can't feel the pressure through them, and they are far more dangerous if they burst.
  6. Leave at room temperature for 6–14 days.
  7. From day 6, squeeze the bottles every day, and as soon as the bases start to round out, ease the caps to let off some (not all) of the pressure.
  8. A forgotten bottle can explode.
  9. Once it tastes right, move it to the fridge to slow the fermentation, and keep checking the pressure.
  10. It is lightly alcoholic after a week and starts turning vinegary at 5–6 weeks; the author drinks it between 2 and 4 weeks.
  11. The yeast comes from the flowers, so in a cool room it can take a while to get going.

Wild mojinto

Method
  1. Muddle lemon balm and wood sorrel (oxalis) leaves with a little sugar and crab-apple verjuice.
  2. Add cracked ice, then gin and soda to taste.

The Elderbalm

Method
  1. Muddle lemon balm in a glass with 70 ml gin, 35 ml elderflower limoncello, 35 ml dry vermouth and 15 ml pineapple weed shrub (pineapple weed vinegar mixed with syrup).
  2. Top up with a splash of lemon balm champagne, to taste.
  3. Garnish with lemon balm and elderflowers placed so that you smell them as you drink.
Lesser burdock Arctium minus 14 recipes

Roasted burdock root

Written for lesser burdock.

Method
  1. Heat the oven to 200 °C and grease a baking tray.
  2. Wash and peel 1–3 large roots, slice them into 1 cm rounds, and drop them into water with a little vinegar as you go.
  3. Drain, pat dry and toss in 1–2 tsp sesame oil (or another oil).
  4. Spread in one layer and roast for 20–30 minutes, turning once halfway.
  5. Season with salt and pepper.

Burdock root tea

Written for lesser burdock. From fresh root: wash, peel and grate 1 root into 1 litre of boiling water, simmer 10 minutes, then take it off the heat and leave to steep 15–20 minutes.

Method
  1. To dry the root: wash, peel and cut it into small pieces and dry them in a dehydrator, or the oven on its lowest setting, for 3–4 hours until hard.
  2. Grind 1–2 tsp per cup as you need it.

Burdock root and carrot stir-fry

Written for greater or lesser burdock.

Method
  1. Peel the roots and keep them in water with a capful of cider vinegar so they don't darken.
  2. Cut them into matchsticks along with the same amount of carrot, scaled to how many you are feeding.
  3. Stir-fry in a heavy frying pan or wok, add a little tamari or soy sauce, and keep stir-frying for 5–7 minutes, until the vegetables just start to caramelise.
  4. Peeled, sliced raw root is also good on its own with a little sea salt.

Burdock root simmered in soy sauce

Serves 1–2 as a side

Written for greater and lesser burdock (after a Japanese dish, from Roger Phillips' Wild Food).

Method
  1. Clean the roots, peeling large ones a little, or cutting away the woody outer layer and using the core.
  2. Slice into thin strips or matchsticks and put them in a small pan with just enough water to cover and a good splash of soy sauce.
  3. Simmer for 15–20 minutes, until the liquid has evaporated and the roots have soaked up the soy; they stay a little crunchy.

Burdock root chips

Written for greater and lesser burdock.

Method
  1. Prepare thin strips of root as for burdock simmered in soy sauce.
  2. Line a plate or two with kitchen paper.
  3. Heat oil in a frying pan over a medium-high heat; it is ready when a test piece floats up and the oil bubbles.
  4. Fry the strips for a few minutes, until crisp and lightly browned, lift them onto the paper with a slotted spoon and sprinkle with sea salt.
  5. For a sesame version, dip the raw strips in cornflour before frying, and toss the hot chips in sesame seeds mixed with a little salt; serve straight away.

Kinpira gobo

Written for burdock root generally — the author treats lesser and greater burdock roots as interchangeable in recipes.

Ingredients
  • 225 g burdock root (1 large)
  • 225 g carrot (1 large)
  • 1 tbsp maple syrup or sugar
  • 1 tbsp mirin or apple cider vinegar
  • 2 tbsp soy sauce
  • 1 tsp fish sauce (optional)
  • 310 ml (1¼ cups) water
  • 2 tbsp toasted sesame oil
  • ½ tsp finely grated ginger (optional)
  • to garnish
  • 2 tsp toasted sesame seeds
  • 2 tsp sliced Chinese chives (both optional)
Method
  1. Wash the burdock well, trim the ends and cut it into 15 cm lengths.
  2. Peel each length, cut it on the diagonal into 5 mm-thick ovals, stack a few and cut into 5 mm matchsticks, dropping them into cold water as you go.
  3. Cut the carrot the same way and keep it separate.
  4. In a 25 cm pan or wok, bring the burdock and 250 ml of the water to the boil, cover and cook 5 minutes, then pour off the water.
  5. Add the carrot, maple syrup, mirin or vinegar, fish sauce, soy sauce, ginger, half the sesame oil and the remaining 60 ml water, and stir-fry over a high heat until the pan is nearly dry.
  6. Off the heat, stir in the rest of the sesame oil and the chives.
  7. Garnish with the sesame seeds; serve warm, cool or at room temperature.

Braised burdock and mushrooms

Written for burdock root generally — the author treats lesser and greater burdock roots as interchangeable in recipes.

Ingredients
  • 225 g cleaned, peeled burdock root
  • 225 g mild fresh mushrooms
  • 2 tbsp soy sauce
  • 2 tbsp apple cider or rice wine vinegar
  • a good pinch of chilli flakes (optional)
  • 2 tbsp ghee, lard or cooking oil
  • a small handful of sliced fresh herbs such as coriander, mint or basil
  • 30 g sliced spring onions or green garlic
Method
  1. Cut the burdock on the diagonal into 5 mm rounds, stack them and cut into 5 mm matchsticks; slice the mushrooms 5 mm thick.
  2. Boil the burdock in water for 5 minutes and drain well.
  3. Heat the fat in a wide pan and cook the burdock and mushrooms until the mushrooms are cooked.
  4. Add the soy sauce, chilli and vinegar and cook down, tossing.
  5. Season with salt and chilli, toss in the spring onions and herbs, fry a minute more and serve hot.
  6. To serve it cold as a salad, use oil (sesame oil is good) instead of ghee or lard.

Burdock mashed potatoes

Written for burdock root generally — the author treats lesser and greater burdock roots as interchangeable in recipes.

Ingredients
  • 170 g burdock root
  • 500 ml (2 cups) cream
  • 900 g floury potatoes
  • 2 tbsp unsalted butter
  • salt and white pepper
Method
  1. Peel the roots into water sharpened with lemon juice, then slice them thinly.
  2. Heat the oven to 120 °C.
  3. Bring the burdock and cream to a simmer in a small non-reactive pot, season lightly, then cover and bake until the burdock is tender, 30–45 minutes.
  4. Blend until smooth, adding a little water if needed, and pass through a sieve; the purée can be made a day or two ahead, or frozen (blend it again after thawing).
  5. Peel the potatoes and boil them in lightly salted water until tender, about 15 minutes.
  6. Rice them, stir in the burdock cream and the butter, and season.
  7. Serve with a knob of butter and some sliced chives.

Yamagobo (pickled burdock root)

Written for burdock root generally — the author treats lesser and greater burdock roots as interchangeable in recipes.

Ingredients
  • 225 g burdock root
  • 60 ml (¼ cup) water
  • 190 ml (¾ cup) rice or apple cider vinegar
  • 1 tsp salt or 1 tbsp soy sauce
  • 4 tbsp sugar
Optional spices
  • a few slices of fresh ginger
  • 2 tsp turmeric (or 1 tbsp carrot or beetroot powder, for colour)
  • 1 tsp Sichuan peppercorns
  • 2 dried hot chillies
  • 1 large garlic clove
  • 1 tsp yellow mustard seed
  • 2 makrut lime leaves
  • 5 allspice berries
  • a small handful of dill
Method
  1. Peel the burdock and cut it into 7.5 cm lengths.
  2. Bring everything else to the boil, then cool, and take out the dill.
  3. Spoon the spices into the bottom of the jar or jars, pack in the burdock, and pour in the liquid, leaving 1 cm headspace; put the lids on.
  4. Lower the jars into a large pot of boiling water that covers them by 5 cm, cover, and once it is back at the boil, process for 10 minutes.
  5. Lift out, label, date and store in the pantry.

Pork rib and burdock soup

Written for burdock root generally — the author treats lesser and greater burdock roots as interchangeable in recipes.

Ingredients
  • 900 g pork spare ribs in 2–3-bone pieces
  • 250 ml (1 cup) carrots, quartered and sliced 5 mm
  • 250 ml (1 cup) each diced onion, leek (white and tender green) and fennel
  • 500 ml (2 cups) puréed canned whole tomatoes
  • 2 litres chicken stock
  • 750 ml (3 cups) peeled burdock root sliced into 5 mm rounds
  • 225 g shiitake caps sliced 5 mm
  • 2 tbsp minced fresh ginger
  • 60 ml (¼ cup) oil
  • salt
  • a bouquet garni of 5 allspice berries
  • 5 cloves
  • 2 star anise
  • 2 fresh bay leaves
  • a halved bulb of garlic
Method
  1. Sweat the leek, onion and ginger in the oil, add everything else and simmer gently for about 2 hours, until the meat comes off the bones.
  2. Season with salt and cool quickly, then pick the meat off the ribs, chop it and return it to the soup.
  3. Just before serving, add cooked millet to taste (or quinoa, wild rice, sorghum or lentils).
  4. Serve with hot chilli oil, or sesame oil and chopped chilli, and fresh coriander.

The author says to treat the amounts as a rough guide.

Burdock flower stalks à la barigoule

Written for the flower stalks of greater and lesser burdock.

Ingredients
  • 450 g peeled burdock flower stalks (see Preparation)
  • 1 small carrot
  • 1 celery stick
  • 1 small onion
  • about 1 litre (4 cups) vegetable stock, enough to cover
  • a sprig of fresh thyme
  • 1 bay leaf
  • fresh herbs
  • salt
  • 125 ml (½ cup) white wine if you like
Method
  1. Cut the vegetables into 7.5 cm pieces.
  2. Put everything in a pan, bring to a simmer, season the liquid lightly with salt, and cook gently until the burdock is just tender, 30–45 minutes.
  3. Let the stalks cool in the liquid, then strain out and discard the aromatic vegetables, keeping the burdock and the liquid (it can cook another batch, or be the broth for a vegetable stew).
  4. Keep the cooked stalks in a sealed container in the fridge.
  5. To serve, cut them into 1 cm pieces and reheat them in their liquid, or brown them in butter.
  6. Using red wine for half the stock stains them purple, and suits roast meat.

Burdock flower stalks with carrots

Written for the flower stalks of greater and lesser burdock.

Ingredients
  • 225 g peeled carrot and 225 g peeled burdock flower stalk, each roll-cut into pieces about 2 cm (slice on the diagonal, turn the piece half a turn, slice again)
  • lemon water
  • 1 small garlic clove
  • a good pinch of sugar
  • 2 tbsp butter
  • salt and black pepper
  • and, optionally, a small handful of greens such as parsley, and chives, mint or basil to finish
Method
  1. Keep the peeled stalks in the lemon water.
  2. Bring a small pot of lightly salted water to the boil.
  3. Blanch the carrots about 30 seconds, until just al dente, and cool them in cold water; blanch the stalks 60 seconds (less for very young ones) and cool them too.
  4. Drain both.
  5. Crush the garlic and warm it in the butter for a minute or two, without letting it burn.
  6. Add the vegetables and cook until tender, seasoning with the sugar, salt and pepper.
  7. Toss in the greens and herbs and serve hot.

Burdock flower stalk noodles

Makes two small servings

Written for the flower stalks of greater and lesser burdock.

Ingredients
  • 4 thick sections of flower stalk about 15 cm long, skinned and peeled
  • 1 litre (4 cups) vegetable stock
  • ¼ lemon
  • salt
Method
  1. Shave the stalks as thin as you can into long noodles with a vegetable peeler or a mandoline.
  2. Simmer gently in the stock with the lemon, seasoned lightly with salt, until just tender, 15–20 minutes, and drain.
  3. Warm a little oil or fat in a pan and cook the noodles over a medium-low heat until very lightly browned.
  4. They turn grey as they cook, which is normal.

The author finished them with sesame oil, sliced hot chilli and sesame seeds.

Burdock flower stalk soup

Written for the flower stalks of greater and lesser burdock.

Ingredients
  • 115 g each of carrot, onion and celery
  • 225 g peeled burdock flower stalks
  • 1–2 bay leaves
  • 3 tbsp flavourless oil
  • about 1 litre (4 cups) really good stock, enough to cover
  • optionally 115 g diced mushrooms or 250 ml (1 cup) chopped cooked meat
  • salt
  • a dash of lemon juice
  • fresh herbs such as chives or parsley
Method
  1. Dice the vegetables, and cut the burdock into slightly larger pieces, keeping it in lemon water until needed.
  2. Sweat the vegetables (and the mushrooms, if using) in the oil, then add the stock, burdock and bay leaves and simmer until tender, about 20 minutes.
  3. Add the meat, if using, and warm it through.
  4. Season with salt, add the lemon juice and herbs, and serve with a drizzle of olive oil or another flavoured oil.
Magnolia Magnolia spp. 1 recipe

Pickled magnolia petals

Makes one 300 ml jar

The author's tree is a lily magnolia (Magnolia liliiflora) hybrid with deep pink flowers; the author suggests M. campbellii, M. grandiflora and 'Forrest Pink' as other pink ones.

Ingredients
  • 1 litre (4 cups, lightly packed) magnolia petals
  • 5 ml (1 tsp) salt for the blanching water
  • 250 ml (1 cup) apple cider vinegar
  • 125 ml (½ cup) unrefined cane sugar
  • another 5 ml (1 tsp) salt
  • a 2 cm piece of fresh ginger, thinly sliced (optional)
Method
  1. Shake the bees out of picked flowers, or gather fallen petals from under a large tree.
  2. Sterilise the jar in a 150 °C oven and keep it warm there.
  3. Blanch the petals in boiling salted water for 30 seconds — it keeps their colour — then drain, cool them under the cold tap and gently squeeze out the water.
  4. Bring the vinegar, sugar, salt and ginger to the boil and simmer until dissolved.
  5. Pack the petals into the warm jar, pour the hot liquid over and seal.
  6. Sealed, it keeps a long time; once opened, keep it in the fridge.
  7. Use it like pickled ginger with sushi, or in burgers, sandwiches and salads.

The author notes that magnolia flowers are not safe to eat while pregnant.

Naples garlic Allium neapolitanum 5 recipes

Naples garlic and cauliflower bake

Written for Naples garlic by a Chilean forager, who says it suits onion weed too.

Method
  1. Cook a cauliflower (or broccoli, or another vegetable in season) in very little water until soft.
  2. Meanwhile chop the stems, leaves and bulbs of the Naples garlic small and fry them gently.
  3. Drain the cauliflower, chop it small and mix it in a bowl with 3 eggs, the fried Naples garlic, and salt and pepper to taste.
  4. Pour into a dish and bake at 180 °C for about 30 minutes, until the top is golden; grated cheese on top is optional.
  5. Cut into pieces and serve with a salad or a grain.

The source gives no amounts for the cauliflower or the Naples garlic.

Boiled and fried Naples garlic, Jódar style

Method
  1. Recorded in Jódar, in Spain's Sierra Mágina, where it was prepared the same way as wild leek.
  2. Cut up the bulbs, the white bases of the leaves, or both, as you prefer.
  3. Boil them first with a little salt.
  4. Then fry them and eat them as they are, or scramble them with egg, or make them into an omelette.
  5. Or let the boiled pieces cool to room temperature and dress them with a little vinegar.

Naples garlic frittata

From a Roman garden club's note on Naples garlic: make a frittata with the leaves, the tender stems and the flower clusters. The source gives no amounts or method beyond that.

Pasta with ricotta and Naples garlic flowers

From the same Roman note.

Method
  1. Mix ricotta with a good handful of Naples garlic flowers, salt and olive oil, and toss it through cooked pasta.

The source gives no other amounts.

Chicory with Naples garlic leaves

From the same Roman note.

Method
  1. Boil Naples garlic leaves together with chicory, which tones down their flavour.
  2. Or fry cooked chicory in a pan with the leaves, in place of a garlic clove.

Green goddess dip

Makes about 500 ml; it thickens in the fridge

The author's greens were nasturtium, chickweed, onion weed, dandelion, oxalis, dock, plantain and wild mustard (the page does not say which plantain).

Ingredients
  • 500 ml (2 cups) roughly chopped wild greens
  • 125 ml (½ cup) cashews, soaked for an hour in the hottest tap water and drained
  • the zest and juice of 1 lemon
  • 60 ml (¼ cup) apple cider vinegar
  • 60 ml (¼ cup) water, only if your blender needs it
  • 2 peeled garlic cloves
  • 60 ml (¼ cup) extra-virgin olive oil
  • 60 ml (¼ cup) hemp seed oil (or more olive oil)
  • 5 ml (1 tsp) salt
  • 1.25 ml (¼ tsp) pepper
  • 30 ml (2 tbsp) nutritional yeast (optional)
Method
  1. Blend in a high-speed blender until smooth.
  2. Use it as a dip or dressing — with chips, steamed potatoes or kūmara, roast vegetables, an omelette or quiche, or stirred through pasta.

Weedolata (wild-greens gremolata)

Makes about 750 ml (3 cups)

The author's greens were dock, onion weed, nasturtium, plantain, baby wild mustard, dandelion, sow thistle and a violet leaf (the page does not say which plantain).

Ingredients
  • 2 crushed garlic cloves
  • the zest and juice of 1 lemon
  • 500 ml (2 cups) firmly packed wild greens, sliced very thinly
  • 2.5 ml (½ tsp) salt
  • 1.25 ml (¼ tsp) cracked black pepper
  • 125 ml (½ cup) roughly chopped pistachios, walnuts, almonds or brazil nuts
  • 60 ml (¼ cup) hemp hearts, pumpkin or sunflower seeds
  • 60 ml (¼ cup) extra-virgin olive oil, hemp seed oil or a mix
Method
  1. To slice the greens, sort them into similar shapes, bunch and twist a few leaves together and slice as thinly as you can — this suits the tougher, stringier ones such as plantain, mustard and dock.
  2. Toss everything together.
  3. Serve as a side or salad, in a wrap or sandwich, or with pasta, risotto, eggs, or sausages and mash.
  4. It keeps at least 4 days in a sealed jar in the fridge.

Plantain seed crackers

Makes about 28

Method
  1. Made with narrow-leaved plantain seeds; the author suggests greater plantain's longer heads if you find them.
  2. In early summer, rub the green seeds off the heads.
  3. Mix 135 g pumpkin seeds, 20 g linseeds, 20 g (about 125 ml) green plantain seeds, 5 ml (1 tsp) sea salt or seaweed salt, 100 g plain flour and 100 g wholemeal or spelt flour, plus 7 g nettle powder if you like.
  4. Stir in 50 ml vegetable oil and 200 ml just-boiled water; it will look like stiff, soggy porridge.
  5. Heat the oven to 140 °C (120 °C fan).
  6. Roll the dough out between two sheets of baking paper to the thickness of one seed, cut it into rectangles about 5 × 2 cm, and lift them onto two baking trays.
  7. Bake for an hour or a little more, until light brown and crisp.
  8. Cool, and keep in an airtight container.

Plantain seed heads with butter and thyme

Written for plantain seed heads, greater or narrow-leaved; the author's own plant is narrow-leaved plantain.

Method
  1. Pick the brown seed heads, with or without their stalks.
  2. Heat enough butter or olive oil to coat a cast-iron pan over medium-high heat, add the heads and toss them for 2 minutes.
  3. Add thyme and a sprinkle of salt and cook 1–2 minutes more, stirring now and then, until the heads are toasted and the seeds just begin to fall off; add more fat if they look dry.
  4. Serve straight away.
  5. The stalks make handles, but are too tough to eat.

Spiced plantain seed mix

Written for plantain seeds, greater or narrow-leaved.

Method
  1. Mix 1 handful of plantain seeds with 3 handfuls of pumpkin seeds and 3 of sesame seeds.
  2. Toast them lightly in a non-stick frying pan, then add a generous splash of tamari or soy sauce, toss to coat, and let any excess cook off.
  3. Tip into bowls and eat as a snack.

Wild Whangaroa pesto

Makes about 350 g

The author's greens, picked from roadsides around Whangaroa Harbour, were nasturtium, kawakawa, fennel, samphire, fat hen, wild mustard, cleavers and clover. The author warns that New Zealand roadsides are still sprayed with glyphosate, so pick with care.

Ingredients
  • 1 garlic clove (or 4 peeled onion weed bulbs)
  • 60 ml (¼ cup) macadamia nuts and 60 ml (¼ cup) grated parmesan
  • dairy or plant-based — or 125 ml (½ cup) macadamias and no parmesan — 750 ml (3 cups) roughly chopped wild greens
  • the zest and juice of 1 lemon
  • 125 ml extra-virgin olive oil
  • salt and pepper
Method
  1. Blend in a food processor to the texture you like.
  2. Serve on crackers or toast, as a side, or stirred through soup, pasta or rice.

Wild greens soup

Serves 4

The author's greens were puha, sow thistle, nasturtium, onion weed, wild fennel, oxalis, wild mustard and wild celery.

Ingredients
  • 1 diced onion
  • 1 potato, scrubbed and diced
  • 2 sliced garlic cloves
  • 500 ml (2 cups) roughly chopped wild greens
  • 45 ml (3 tbsp) olive oil
  • 1.3 litres water
  • 5 ml (1 tsp) salt
  • 30 ml (2 tbsp) nutritional yeast (optional)
  • 1.25 ml (¼ tsp) cracked pepper
Method
  1. Cut the vegetables and greens to similar sizes.
  2. Fry the onion in the oil for a minute, then add the potato and garlic (the method also adds fennel bulb and celery stalks, which the ingredient list leaves out) and fry for another minute.
  3. Add the water, cover, bring to the boil and simmer until the potato is soft.
  4. Add the greens with the salt and yeast and cook for 3 minutes, until just tender — added at the end, they keep their colour.
  5. Blend until smooth (push it through a sieve if your blender isn't powerful), season with the pepper and more salt if needed, and serve drizzled with extra-virgin olive or hemp seed oil and scattered with edible weed flowers.

Nasturtium and nipplewort stuffed mushrooms

Serves 4

Written for nasturtium and nipplewort together.

Ingredients
  • 6 large field mushrooms
  • 60 ml (¼ cup) extra-virgin olive oil plus another 60 ml for frying
  • the leaves of 1 rosemary stalk, chopped
  • salt and pepper
  • 600 g nasturtium and nipplewort leaves, roughly chopped
  • 250 ml water
  • 1 onion (the method uses one, though the ingredient list leaves it out)
  • 4 crushed garlic cloves
  • the zest and juice of 1 lemon
  • 250 ml (1 cup) roughly chopped walnuts
  • 240 g cream cheese, plant-based or dairy
  • 1 slice of bread, broken into small pieces
Method
  1. Toss the mushrooms with the first 60 ml of oil, the rosemary, 2.5 ml (½ tsp) salt and 1.25 ml (¼ tsp) pepper and leave them to marinate.
  2. Cook the greens in a lidded pot with the water and 5 ml (1 tsp) salt on high heat for 5 minutes, turning them, until wilted; cool them under cold running water, squeeze out the water and chop again.
  3. Heat the oven to 180 °C.
  4. Fry the mushrooms in the second 60 ml of oil, open side down, for 4 minutes, then 4 minutes on the other side, until golden, and set them open side up in a baking dish.
  5. In the same pan soften the onion for 5 minutes, add the garlic, greens, lemon zest and juice and cook a minute, then stir in the walnuts, cream cheese, bread, 2.5 ml (½ tsp) salt and 1.25 ml (¼ tsp) pepper off the heat.
  6. Pack the filling into the mushrooms, drizzle with olive oil and bake for 20 minutes until golden.
  7. Serve on soft polenta or mashed kūmara and potato.

Nasturtium flower pasta

Serves 4

Ingredients
  • 400 g dried pasta
  • 5 ml (1 tsp) salt for the water
Sauce
  • 1 litre (4 cups) nasturtium flowers
  • 375 ml (1½ cups) ricotta or soaked cashews
  • 30 ml (2 tbsp) parmesan or nutritional yeast
  • 2 garlic cloves
  • 1 chilli (optional)
  • 1 tomato
  • 60 ml (¼ cup) extra-virgin olive oil
  • the zest and juice of 2 lemons
  • 7.5 ml (1½ tsp) salt
  • 5 ml (1 tsp) black pepper
  • 80 ml (⅓ cup) water from the pasta pot
To finish
  • 1 bunch asparagus, ends snapped off
  • 4 roughly chopped tomatoes
  • 30 ml (2 tbsp) extra-virgin olive oil
  • salt and pepper
  • 80 ml (⅓ cup) grated parmesan or hemp parmesan
  • 80 ml (⅓ cup) finely chopped parsley
Method
  1. Boil the pasta in the salted water for about 8 minutes, until just cooked, adding the asparagus for the last 2 minutes.
  2. Meanwhile blend the sauce ingredients until smooth.
  3. Drain the pasta, take out the asparagus and cool it briefly under cold water, then return the hot pasta to the pot and stir in the sauce.
  4. Season the tomatoes with salt, pepper and the second measure of oil.
  5. Top the pasta with the asparagus, tomatoes, parsley, parmesan and a few more nasturtium flowers and leaves.

Oat Avena sativa 2 recipes

Oatstraw infusion

Made from the green straw — stems and leaves cut before the grain hardens, then dried.

Method
  1. Put about 60 ml of dried oatstraw in a 1 litre jar, fill with boiling water and cover.
  2. Leave it to steep several hours or overnight, then strain.
  3. Drink through the day; it keeps about three days in the fridge.

Avena latte

Built on the infusion above.

Method
  1. Warm about 350 ml of milk — dairy, almond or soy — with 2 tbsp honey and 1/2 tsp cinnamon, stirring until smooth.
  2. Pour into a jar with 175 ml of strained oatstraw infusion, cover and shake before serving.
Onion weed Allium triquetrum 22 recipes

Onionweed salsa verde

No method written yet.

Onionweed herb butter

No method written yet.

Onionweed flower tempura

No method written yet.

Pickled onionweed bulbs

No method written yet.

Onion weed frittata

Serves 4

Written for three-cornered leek (onion weed).

Method
  1. Heat the grill.
  2. Beat 6 eggs with 3 tbsp milk or cream, season well, and stir in most of 50 g grated hard cheese (Parmesan, Gruyère or aged Cheddar).
  3. Heat 2 tbsp olive oil in a 24 cm ovenproof frying pan over medium heat; if you like, soften a thinly sliced courgette in it for 3–4 minutes.
  4. Add 80 g roughly chopped onion weed leaves and stems and stir for 1 minute, until just wilted.
  5. Pour in the eggs and cook undisturbed on medium-low for 5–6 minutes, until the edges set.
  6. Scatter the rest of the cheese over and grill 3–4 minutes, until set and lightly golden.
  7. Scatter a small handful of raw onion weed flowers over the top and serve in wedges, warm or at room temperature.

Onion weed soup

Serves 4

Written for three-cornered leek (onion weed).

Method
  1. Soften a diced white onion in 2 tbsp olive oil or butter for 8 minutes.
  2. Add 2 sliced garlic cloves and a peeled, diced potato (or a small cauliflower) and cook 2 minutes.
  3. Add 1 litre of vegetable or light chicken stock and simmer 12–15 minutes, until the potato is tender.
  4. Stir in 300 g onion weed leaves and stems and cook exactly 3 minutes, to keep the colour.
  5. Off the heat, add 3 tbsp crème fraîche (or coconut cream) and the juice of half a lemon, and blend smooth straight away.
  6. Season with salt and white pepper and serve at once, with more crème fraîche and a few flowers; it loses colour if it sits.

Korean-style onion weed salad

Written for three-cornered leek (onion weed).

Method
  1. Cut onion weed into pieces about 4 cm long, enough to fill 2 cups (about 500 ml).
  2. Toss them in a bowl with a quarter of a cucumber, thinly sliced, 1 tsp each of red pepper flakes, sugar, sesame seeds and salt, and 1 tbsp each of sesame oil and white vinegar.
  3. Serve straight away while crunchy, or leave a few minutes to marinate.
  4. Good with fishcakes, grilled meat or rice.

Buttered onion weed flower stalks

Written for three-cornered leek (onion weed).

Method
  1. Pick the flower stalks with their buds before the buds open.
  2. Melt some butter in a pan, add a large bunch of the stalks, cover to keep the moisture in, and stir; they cook very quickly.
  3. Finish with sea salt and lemon juice.

Tagliatelle with onion weed

From an Italian wild-herb cookbook, written for aglio triangolo (Allium triquetrum).

Method
  1. Stew the bulbs and leaves of a generous bunch of onion weed in extra-virgin olive oil with a little water, keeping the flowers aside.
  2. Toss tagliatelle cooked al dente through it and add the flowers.

The authors prefer it plain to taste the garlic best; or toss the tagliatelle for 10 minutes with 500 g cherry tomatoes before adding the flowers.

Onion weed and wild asparagus omelette

Written for lágrimas de la Virgen (Allium triquetrum).

Method
  1. Cut the tenderest part of a bunch of wild asparagus into pieces, and leave a dozen onion weed flower heads whole.
  2. Beat 2 eggs with salt and black pepper, mix in the asparagus and flowers, and pour into a frying pan with a little olive oil.
  3. When set underneath, turn it over with a plate and cook a few minutes more.
  4. The flowers give a very mild garlic flavour.

Wild weed spread

Makes about 600 g

The author makes it with chickweed, oxalis and onion weed together.

Ingredients
  • 500 ml (2 cups) raw cashews
  • 500 ml hot water
  • 80 ml (⅓ cup) extra-virgin olive oil
  • 1 garlic clove
  • the zest and juice of 1 lemon
  • 30 ml (2 tbsp) apple cider vinegar
  • 60 ml (¼ cup) nutritional yeast (or finely grated parmesan, or cream cheese)
  • 625 ml (2½ cups) wild greens, roughly chopped and tightly packed
  • 15 ml (1 tbsp) chopped thyme
  • dill
  • coriander or parsley
  • 5 ml (1 tsp) salt
  • 2.5 ml (½ tsp) pepper
Method
  1. Soak the cashews in the hot water for an hour, then drain them and throw the water away.
  2. Blend everything together until very smooth.
  3. The mixture is stiff, like cream cheese: a stick blender in a jug works best, and a jug blender may need a little water.
  4. Spread it on toast or crackers, or use it in sandwiches and burgers.

Spoon it into a jar or dish with a lid, pour a thin layer of olive oil over the top so it does not discolour, and keep it covered in the fridge; the author says it keeps for a couple of weeks.

Onion weed, kawakawa and kelp super salt

Makes about 200 g

Written for onion weed, kawakawa and kelp together.

Ingredients
  • 250 ml (1 cup) onion weed stems and leaves, sliced 5 mm thick
  • 8–10 kawakawa leaves
  • 2 hand-sized pieces of kelp or other seaweed (the author forages hers, and says bought seaweed works)
  • 125 ml (½ cup) rock salt
Method
  1. If you can, dry the kawakawa for a few days beforehand.
  2. Spread each ingredient in a single layer on its own tray, because they dry at different speeds — in a dehydrator, or on a lined perforated tray in a 50–60 °C oven — until crisp to the touch, 2 to 4 hours.
  3. Sprinkle it on anything, or use it in place of stock.

Grind them with the salt in batches, in a spice grinder or with a mortar and pestle, to the texture you like, and keep it in a sealed jar; the author says it lasts indefinitely.

Tempura onion weed flower clusters

Serves 4–6

Ingredients
  • Two big handfuls of onion weed flower heads on their stems
  • 250 ml (1 cup) plain flour and 125 ml (½ cup) cornflour, or 250 ml (1 cup) gluten-free flour mix
  • 2.5 ml (½ tsp) salt
  • 1.25 ml (¼ tsp) black pepper
  • 300 ml ice-cold soda water
  • about 1 litre of rice bran oil for frying
Method
  1. Whisk the flours and seasoning with the soda water only briefly — leave it a little lumpy — and let it rest.
  2. Heat the oil to 180 °C in a deep-fryer or large wok.
  3. Hold three flower heads together, dip them in the batter and lower them into the oil, turning them so they cook evenly.
  4. When they are light golden, lift them out with a slotted spoon onto a rack over a tray, and salt them.
  5. Serve with sweet chilli sauce and the onion weed mayo.

Onion weed mayo

Makes about 250 ml

Ingredients
  • 10 onion weed bulbs with their white parts, finely chopped
  • 60 ml aquafaba (the liquid from cooked chickpeas) with 1.25 ml (¼ tsp) cream of tartar, or 1 egg yolk
  • 30 ml (2 tbsp) mustard
  • 5 ml (1 tsp) sugar or honey
  • 100 ml rice bran oil and 50 ml extra-virgin olive oil
  • the zest and juice of 1 lemon
  • 10 ml (2 tsp) apple cider vinegar
  • 5 ml (1 tsp) salt and a pinch of black pepper
Method
  1. Blend the onion weed, aquafaba or yolk, mustard and sugar in a mini food processor, then add the oils a little at a time, blending well after each addition.
  2. Once it has emulsified, blend in the lemon, vinegar, salt and pepper.

The author serves it with the tempura flower clusters, and with the more bitter wild greens.

Onion weed fritters

Serves 4–6

Ingredients
  • The batter from the tempura flower clusters — 250 ml (1 cup) plain flour and 125 ml (½ cup) cornflour, or 250 ml (1 cup) gluten-free flour mix
  • 2.5 ml (½ tsp) salt
  • 1.25 ml (¼ tsp) black pepper
  • 300 ml ice-cold soda water
  • 500 ml (2 cups) roughly chopped onion weed flowers
  • 60 ml (¼ cup) grated parmesan (dairy or not)
  • if you like, 300 g mashed tofu
Frying
  • 30 ml (2 tbsp) rice bran or another neutral oil with 1.25 ml (¼ tsp) sesame oil
Method
  1. Whisk the batter, then stir in the onion weed, parmesan and tofu.
  2. Heat the oils in a frying pan over medium-high heat and fry the fritters about five at a time until golden on both sides.
  3. Move them to a cooling rack and repeat until the mixture is used up.
  4. Serve with sweet chilli sauce and avocado mayo, or the onion weed mayo.

Potato, kūmara and crispy chickpea salad with onion weed dressing

Serves 4–6

Ingredients
Dressing
  • 10 onion weed bulbs with their white parts, roughly chopped
  • 60 ml aquafaba (the liquid from cooked chickpeas)
  • 15 ml (1 tbsp) mustard
  • 5 ml (1 tsp) sugar or honey
  • 150 ml extra-virgin olive oil
  • the zest and juice of 1 lemon
  • 10 ml (2 tsp) apple cider vinegar
  • 5 ml (1 tsp) salt and a pinch of black pepper
Method
  1. Blend the onion weed, aquafaba, mustard and sugar in a mini food processor, add the oil a little at a time, blending well between additions, and once it has emulsified blend in the rest.
  2. Salad: scrub 3–4 kūmara and 3–4 potatoes (the author prefers a waxy kind such as peruperu), cut them into bite-sized pieces, steam until cooked and let them cool.
  3. Drain and pat dry 60 ml (¼ cup) capers and 375 ml (1½ cups) cooked chickpeas, fry them in 125 ml (½ cup) rice bran oil until crisp, and drain on paper towel.
  4. In the salad bowl, mix 1 finely sliced celery stick, 1 finely sliced fennel bulb, 60 ml (¼ cup) finely sliced daikon or radish, the juice of half a lemon, 2.5 ml (½ tsp) salt and a pinch of pepper, and leave it a couple of minutes while you slice the rest of the onion weed stems and flowers.
  5. Add everything to the bowl, pour the dressing over generously, toss and serve.

Dandan onion weed flowers with chilli oil

Ingredients
Chilli oil (makes 250 ml)
  • 250 ml (1 cup) rice bran or another neutral oil
  • 45 ml (3 tbsp) chilli flakes
  • 30 ml (2 tbsp) Sichuan pepper
  • 2 star anise
  • 1 cinnamon quill broken in half
  • 2 kawakawa or horopito leaves
  • torn (optional)
  • 2 garlic cloves, squashed and peeled
The dish (serves 4 as a side)
  • a colander full of onion weed flower clusters, about 1 litre (4 cups)
  • 3–4 fresh wood ear (hakeke) or shiitake mushrooms, thinly sliced (optional)
  • 6 flowering tops of ruruhau, mizuna, wild mustard or broccoli (optional)
  • 15 ml (1 tbsp) rice bran or extra-virgin olive oil
  • 15 ml (1 tbsp) butter, plant-based or dairy
  • 30 ml (2 tbsp) tamari or light soy sauce
  • the juice of half a lemon or lime
  • 60 ml (¼ cup) crushed salted peanuts (optional)
Method
  1. Put everything except the oil in a heatproof bowl.
  2. Heat the oil to 180 °C and pour it over carefully — the fresh leaves and garlic spit and bubble.
  3. Leave it to cool and infuse; blend it if you don't want large pieces, and keep it in a lidded jar in the pantry.
  4. Heat the oil and butter on high, add the mushrooms and cook for 1 minute; add the onion weed and the flowering tops and cook another minute; add the tamari and cook 2 minutes more.
  5. Spoon over as much chilli oil as you like, squeeze over the citrus and top with the peanuts.
  6. Serve on noodles or as a side dish.

Pumpkin and feta fritters with onion weed flowers

Makes about 23 fritters; serves 4–6

Ingredients
Fritters
  • a quarter of a pumpkin, peeled and grated
  • 200 g feta (or goat's cheese), cubed or crumbled
  • 1 crushed garlic clove
  • a handful of onion weed flowers (or chives), chopped
  • 100 g sprouted, cooked or canned chickpeas
  • 250 ml (1 cup, 120 g) chickpea flour
  • 60 ml (¼ cup, 30 g) tapioca starch
  • 10 ml (2 tsp) baking powder
  • 2.5 ml (½ tsp) salt
  • 1.25 ml (¼ tsp) cracked black pepper
  • 100 ml milk (dairy or not)
  • 2 eggs — or 30 ml (2 tbsp) ground flaxseed soaked 10 minutes in 80 ml (⅓ cup) water —
  • 80 ml (⅓ cup, 90 g) yoghurt
  • 50 ml rice bran oil for frying
Method
  1. Mix the dry ingredients with the pumpkin, feta, garlic, onion weed and chickpeas, make a well, and mix in the milk, eggs and yoghurt.
  2. Heat 30 ml (2 tbsp) of the oil at a time in a frying pan.
  3. Shape fritters from 2 tablespoons of mix, fry 2–3 at a time, pressed with a spoon to just under 1 cm thick, over gentle heat until golden on both sides.
  4. Salsa: char 1 capsicum under the grill or over a gas flame until the skin is black, steam it in a covered bowl, peel and dice it (a few charred bits are fine).
  5. Mix it with 1 diced tomato, a quarter of a cucumber, cubed, a handful of finely chopped parsley, 3 sprigs of finely chopped mint, 125 ml (½ cup) lentil sprouts, 1 diced avocado, the juice of half a lime, 30 ml (2 tbsp) extra-virgin olive oil, 1.25 ml (¼ tsp) salt and some pepper.
  6. The chickpea and lentil sprouts need starting a couple of days ahead.
  7. Serve the fritters with the salsa.

Fennel and radicchio salad with onion weed

Serves 4

Method
  1. In the salad bowl, mix 1 crushed garlic clove, the zest and juice of 1 lemon and of 1 orange, 45 ml (3 tbsp) extra-virgin olive oil or hemp seed oil, and 15 ml (1 tbsp) honey or coconut nectar.
  2. Add 1–2 finely sliced fennel bulbs, 1 finely sliced radicchio, 60 ml (¼ cup) shelled pistachios, 6 finely sliced onion weed leaves (or 1 spring onion), a handful of roughly chopped coriander, a handful of roughly chopped basil, dandelion tips, baby wild mustard or nasturtium leaves, 2.5 ml (½ tsp) salt and 1.25 ml (¼ tsp) cracked black pepper.
  3. Toss well and serve.

Green goddess dip

Makes about 500 ml; it thickens in the fridge

The author's greens were nasturtium, chickweed, onion weed, dandelion, oxalis, dock, plantain and wild mustard.

Ingredients
  • 500 ml (2 cups) roughly chopped wild greens
  • 125 ml (½ cup) cashews, soaked for an hour in the hottest tap water and drained
  • the zest and juice of 1 lemon
  • 60 ml (¼ cup) apple cider vinegar
  • 60 ml (¼ cup) water, only if your blender needs it
  • 2 peeled garlic cloves
  • 60 ml (¼ cup) extra-virgin olive oil
  • 60 ml (¼ cup) hemp seed oil (or more olive oil)
  • 5 ml (1 tsp) salt
  • 1.25 ml (¼ tsp) pepper
  • 30 ml (2 tbsp) nutritional yeast (optional)
Method
  1. Blend in a high-speed blender until smooth.
  2. Use it as a dip or dressing — with chips, steamed potatoes or kūmara, roast vegetables, an omelette or quiche, or stirred through pasta.

Weedolata (wild-greens gremolata)

Makes about 750 ml (3 cups)

The author's greens were dock, onion weed, nasturtium, plantain, baby wild mustard, dandelion, sow thistle and a violet leaf.

Ingredients
  • 2 crushed garlic cloves
  • the zest and juice of 1 lemon
  • 500 ml (2 cups) firmly packed wild greens, sliced very thinly
  • 2.5 ml (½ tsp) salt
  • 1.25 ml (¼ tsp) cracked black pepper
  • 125 ml (½ cup) roughly chopped pistachios, walnuts, almonds or brazil nuts
  • 60 ml (¼ cup) hemp hearts, pumpkin or sunflower seeds
  • 60 ml (¼ cup) extra-virgin olive oil, hemp seed oil or a mix
Method
  1. To slice the greens, sort them into similar shapes, bunch and twist a few leaves together and slice as thinly as you can — this suits the tougher, stringier ones such as plantain, mustard and dock.
  2. Toss everything together.
  3. Serve as a side or salad, in a wrap or sandwich, or with pasta, risotto, eggs, or sausages and mash.
  4. It keeps at least 4 days in a sealed jar in the fridge.

Wild greens soup

Serves 4

The author's greens were puha, sow thistle, nasturtium, onion weed, wild fennel, oxalis, wild mustard and wild celery.

Ingredients
  • 1 diced onion
  • 1 potato, scrubbed and diced
  • 2 sliced garlic cloves
  • 500 ml (2 cups) roughly chopped wild greens
  • 45 ml (3 tbsp) olive oil
  • 1.3 litres water
  • 5 ml (1 tsp) salt
  • 30 ml (2 tbsp) nutritional yeast (optional)
  • 1.25 ml (¼ tsp) cracked pepper
Method
  1. Cut the vegetables and greens to similar sizes.
  2. Fry the onion in the oil for a minute, then add the potato and garlic (the method also adds fennel bulb and celery stalks, which the ingredient list leaves out) and fry for another minute.
  3. Add the water, cover, bring to the boil and simmer until the potato is soft.
  4. Add the greens with the salt and yeast and cook for 3 minutes, until just tender — added at the end, they keep their colour.
  5. Blend until smooth (push it through a sieve if your blender isn't powerful), season with the pepper and more salt if needed, and serve drizzled with extra-virgin olive or hemp seed oil and scattered with edible weed flowers.
Oregon grape Berberis aquifolium 2 recipes

Oregon grape jelly

Written for Oregon grape (Mahonia aquifolium, or its low relative M. nervosa) by a preserving author in Oregon.

Method
  1. Pick about 1.6 kg berries once they are evenly dark; sliding your fingers down a bunch drops them into the basket.
  2. Simmer the berries, covered, with half their volume of water for 15 minutes, mashing them after the first 10.
  3. Let the juice drip through a jelly bag for several hours, then boil it for 10 minutes to reduce it a little.
  4. Combine about 720 ml (3 cups) juice with about 450 g (2¼ cups) sugar in a wide, heavy, non-reactive pan over medium heat and stir until the sugar dissolves.
  5. Raise the heat and boil, skimming now and then, until it begins to jell, which takes only a few minutes: drops falling from a tipped metal spoon thicken and run together.
  6. Skim, pour into three sterilised half-pint (about 240 ml) jars and process in a boiling-water bath for 5 minutes.
  7. It should be firm by the next day.

The author advises against doubling the batch. Metric amounts are converted from the source's pounds and cups.

Oregon grape jelly with pectin

Written for Oregon grape (Mahonia aquifolium) by a native-plant nursery propagator, reprinted by the Washington Native Plant Society.

Method
  1. Crush about 1.4–2.3 kg (3–5 lb, about 2 quarts) washed, stemmed berries and add about 720 ml (3 cups) water; if you use a hand blender, keep it on low, because over-mixing can make it frothy.
  2. Boil for 10 minutes, mashing a little.
  3. Drain through a cheesecloth-lined colander for a few hours, until you have about 950 ml (4 cups) juice.
  4. Bring the juice and about 1 kg (5 cups) sugar to a rapid boil, add one packet of liquid pectin and return to a full boil for 1 minute.
  5. Pour into warm sterilised jars (the recipe makes six half-pints, about 240 ml each), lid them and process in a boiling-water bath for 10 minutes; leave to set overnight.

The source does not give the size of the pectin packet. Metric amounts are converted from the source's pounds and cups.

Pale-pink wood sorrel Oxalis incarnata 12 recipes

Wood sorrel, kūmara and potato gratin

Ingredients
  • Two handfuls of wood sorrel, chopped
  • potato and kūmara, boiled first and sliced
  • cream
Method
  1. Layer the sorrel with the sliced potato and kūmara in a baking dish, pour cream over the top and bake at 180 °C until it starts to brown.

Potato salad with wood sorrel

Method
  1. Chop a big handful of wood sorrel leaves, stalks and flowers and mix them through a potato salad dressed with a cream dressing.

Wood sorrel sauce

Ingredients
  • 500 ml (2 cups) wood sorrel leaves
  • 30 ml (2 tbsp) butter
  • 7.5 ml (1½ tsp) lemon juice
  • 7.5 ml (1½ tsp) maple syrup or honey
Method
  1. Melt the butter in a saucepan, add the sorrel and stir for 1–2 minutes, until it wilts.
  2. Put it in a blender or food processor with the lemon juice and the syrup or honey and blend until well combined.
  3. Serve it straight away, or refrigerate it and warm it before use; the author finds it even better the next day.

The author serves it on fish, or as a dipping sauce for grilled prawns.

Wood sorrel 'lemonade'

Ingredients
  • About 500 ml (2 cups) wood sorrel leaves, stems or flowers, or a mix
  • about 250 ml (1 cup) water
  • maple syrup or another sweetener to taste
Method
  1. The amounts are rough: use as much sorrel as you like and enough water to cover it.
  2. Blend the sorrel and water until well combined, strain through a fine sieve and sweeten to taste.

Wood sorrel syrup

Ingredients
  • Equal parts wood sorrel
  • sugar and water

The author usually uses 250 ml (1 cup) of each.

Method
  1. Put everything in a saucepan and bring it to a simmer, stirring.
  2. Simmer for 15–30 minutes, strain through muslin (cheesecloth) and pour into sterilised bottles.
  3. Use it in tea, topped up with soda water, over ice cream or fresh strawberries, or in vinaigrettes and marinades.

Wood sorrel cooler

Ingredients
  • About 950 ml (1 US quart) water
  • 125 ml (½ cup) wood sorrel leaves, stems
  • flowers and seed pods
  • 15 ml (1 tbsp) agave nectar or honey
  • a dash of salt
Method
  1. Mix everything in a blender.
  2. If you can, leave it in the fridge overnight before drinking.

Wood sorrel tea

Method
  1. Fresh wood sorrel leaves and boiling water, one part leaves to three parts water.
  2. Put the leaves in a container, pour the boiling water over them and leave to steep for 5–15 minutes.
  3. Strain out the leaves and sweeten to taste if you like.
  4. Drink it hot, or pour it over ice for an iced tea.

Chilled lemony wood sorrel soup

Ingredients
  • 2 cloves garlic, minced
  • olive oil for cooking
  • 2 spring onions, chopped
  • 500 ml (2 cups) vegetable stock
  • 500 ml (2 cups) lightly packed wood sorrel leaves, with the flowers kept back for garnish
  • 3 medium mint leaves
  • 250 ml (1 cup) full-fat coconut milk
  • salt and pepper
Method
  1. Rinse the sorrel well and let it dry, then strip the leaves and flowers from the stems and throw away the bare stalks.
  2. Heat a little olive oil in a small saucepan and sauté the garlic until fragrant, then add the spring onions.
  3. Cover with the stock, bring to the boil and simmer for about 5 minutes, then add the sorrel for about 30 seconds.
  4. Take it off the heat, add the mint and blend smooth with a stick blender (or an ordinary blender).
  5. Stir in the coconut milk and season with salt and pepper.
  6. Refrigerate until well chilled, a couple of hours, and serve garnished with the sorrel flowers.

Spaghetti squash casserole with wood sorrel

Ingredients
  • About 450 g (1 lb) Italian or breakfast sausage
  • 1 medium spaghetti squash
  • several cups of wood sorrel, washed and drained, stems removed (the recipe gives no closer amount)
  • olive oil
  • 1 red onion, halved and sliced
  • 1 egg
  • 125 ml (½ cup) coconut milk or any milk
  • 1 garlic clove, minced
  • 15 ml (1 tbsp) garlic powder
  • 5 ml (1 tsp) tarragon
  • 5 ml (1 tsp) salt
  • 2.5 ml (½ tsp) black pepper
  • 1.25 ml (¼ tsp) ground sage
Method
  1. Heat the oven to 200 °C.
  2. Halve the squash, spoon out the seeds and stringy threads, set the halves cut side up on a baking tray, drizzle with olive oil, season with salt and pepper and bake for 20–25 minutes, until the flesh gives a little when pressed.
  3. Meanwhile cook the sausage and onion in a large pot over medium-high heat.
  4. When the sausage is cooked through, add the garlic and sauté for 2 minutes, then add the sorrel, milk and spices, cover and simmer until the sorrel wilts.
  5. Take the squash out of the oven and scrape out the flesh with a fork (careful, it will be hot).
  6. Combine the mixture with the egg, stirring thoroughly, spread it evenly in a baking dish (or the same pot, if it is ovenproof) and bake for 15–20 minutes, until the top is lightly browned.

Peppermint geranium Pelargonium tomentosum 2 recipes

Peppermint geranium syrup

Makes about 300 ml

Written for rose geranium leaves; the author suggests mint-scented geranium as a substitute.

Ingredients
  • 250 ml water
  • 200 g sugar
  • about five sprigs of peppermint geranium 10–13 cm long
Method
  1. Put the water, sugar and sprigs in a small saucepan, bruising the leaves against the side of the pan with a spoon.
  2. Bring to the boil over moderate heat, cover, take off the heat and leave to stand for at least 30 minutes.
  3. Lift out the leaves and squeeze them into the syrup.
  4. Keeps in the fridge for up to a month, or frozen for up to a year.
  5. The original uses it in a chocolate dessert.

Scented geranium and lemon icing

Written for scented geranium leaves, "preferably with a lemon scent"; no species named.

Method
  1. Tear 2–3 scented geranium leaves into small pieces and mix them with about 250 g sifted icing sugar in a small bowl.
  2. Leave somewhere warm overnight so the sugar takes up the scent, then pick out and discard the leaf pieces.
  3. Beat a generous 60 ml softened cream cheese, 2 teaspoons softened butter and 1 teaspoon grated lemon rind, gradually adding the scented icing sugar, until smooth.
  4. Spread over a cooled carrot cake and decorate, if you like, with lemon balm leaves or candied lemon peel.
Perennial sow thistle Sonchus arvensis 1 recipe

Sow thistle with red onion, goat cheese and pine nuts

Serves 4

Written for the sow thistles of British Columbia's Sea to Sky region, where perennial and annual sow thistle both grow; the card does not say which.

Method
  1. Rinse and chop a large bunch of leaves, boil for a few minutes and drain.
  2. Sauté a quarter of a red onion, minced, in olive oil in a large frying pan for 3 minutes, add a minced clove of garlic and cook a minute more.
  3. Turn the heat to medium and add the leaves a handful at a time, stirring, as they shrink.
  4. Add about 60 ml chicken or vegetable stock, cover and simmer for 10 minutes.
  5. Season with salt and pepper, then stir in a couple of tablespoons of crumbled goat cheese, or to taste, and some pine nuts.

Pineappleweed Matricaria discoidea 2 recipes

Pineapple weed vinegar

Method
  1. Half fill a jar with pineapple weed flower tops, top it up with apple cider vinegar, and leave for a couple of weeks.
  2. Sweetened with sugar it becomes a shrub, or with honey an oxymel, for cocktails or for diluting with cold or hot water as a long drink.

The author uses it as a base for salad dressings and as the vinegar in pickles.

Pineapple weed syrup and cordial

Method
  1. Measure a jug of pineapple weed flowers, then the same volume of water and the same volume of sugar.
  2. Heat gently, stirring until the sugar dissolves, without letting it boil.
  3. Let it cool, heat it gently again, and pour it into jars with the flowers still in.
  4. When cool, keep it in the fridge for a week, then strain out the flowers.
  5. Store it in the fridge or freezer — left out, it will start to ferment.
  6. Stir in citric acid or lemon juice to taste and the syrup becomes a cordial, sweet and sharp.
Porcini Boletus edulis 4 recipes

Porcini on toast

Method
  1. Brush the caps clean — do not wash them, they drink water.
  2. Slice thickly, about the width of a finger.
  3. Melt a good knob of butter in a wide pan until it foams, add the slices in one layer and leave them alone until the undersides are deep brown, then turn.
  4. Salt only at the end, or they weep.
  5. Finish with a squeeze of lemon and a little chopped parsley, pile onto thick toast rubbed with garlic, and eat immediately.
  6. Two medium caps feed one person who has had a good day.

Dried porcini and their stock

Method
  1. The way to keep a glut.
  2. Slice caps and stems 5 mm thick and lay them on a rack or a tea towel in a warm, airy spot — a dehydrator at 45 °C, or an oven on its lowest setting with the door ajar, takes six to eight hours.
  3. They are done when they snap rather than bend.
  4. Store in a jar with a tight lid; the smell will be stronger than the fresh mushroom ever was.
  5. To use, soak a handful in warm water for twenty minutes, lift the pieces out, and pour the soaking liquid through a paper filter to catch the grit — that liquid is the best part, and goes into risotto, soup or gravy.

Ultimate wild mushroom risotto

Serves 4

Written for mixed wild mushrooms — the author's are dried porcini (cep), puffball and chanterelles.

Method
  1. Heat 2 litres of good chicken or vegetable stock in a separate pan and add 30 g dried porcini to soak and enrich it.
  2. Sweat 150 g chanterelles in a frying pan until they give up their juice, and strain the juice into the stock.
  3. In a heavy pan, sweat 1 finely diced large onion, 3 garlic cloves, 3 bay leaves, and 1 courgette and 1 small leek (both finely diced) in 50 g of 100 g butter until soft and translucent.
  4. Add a few sprigs of thyme and 300 g unwashed carnaroli or arborio rice and stir over a medium heat for a couple of minutes.
  5. Add 300 ml dry white wine and keep stirring as it absorbs.
  6. Add 150 g puffball, peeled and cut into 1 cm cubes, and the soaked porcini with a little hot stock.
  7. Keep stirring briskly, adding the stock a ladleful at a time; when about half is in, add the chanterelles.
  8. Season, and stop when the rice keeps a hint of bite (no chalkiness) and the risotto is like a very thick soup — you may not need all the stock.
  9. Off the heat, stir in half of 300 g finely grated parmesan, the remaining 50 g butter, 75 ml double cream and a handful of finely chopped parsley and tarragon.
  10. Cover and rest for a few minutes.
  11. Stir in the juice of 1 lemon, check the seasoning and add a little more stock if needed.
  12. Serve in shallow bowls with a few drops of truffle oil, the rest of the cheese on the side, and, if you like, a sprinkle of hairy bittercress for a sharp note.

Wild mushroom pâté

Written for mixed wild mushrooms; the author strongly recommends that some be dried porcini, for their intensity.

Method
  1. Use 1 kg fresh mushrooms, or 250 g dried (soaked for 30 minutes in boiling water and drained), or a mix.
  2. Chop roughly and cook thoroughly in butter or oil over a medium heat until all the moisture has gone (the juice can be strained off as a mushroom broth).
  3. Cool, then squeeze out any remaining moisture.
  4. Blend in a food processor, then add 200 g finely grated parmesan or another strong hard cheese, up to 500 g cream cheese (less for a more mushroomy pâté), a large handful of sorrel leaves or a squeeze of lemon juice, and salt and pepper, in stages, tasting as you go.
  5. Optional extras: dried porcini powder cooked with the mushrooms, black garlic, finely chopped pickled walnuts stirred through at the end, miso, soy sauce or dried seaweed powder.
  6. Pack into small tubs and label each with the species you used.
  7. Keeps for a week in the fridge, or freeze it.
