Prickly sow thistle Sonchus asper 3 recipes

Pickled sow thistle buds

No method written yet.

Steamed prickly sow thistle tips with butter

Method
  1. Pick the shoot tips just as the flower buds start to appear, allowing 5 tips per person.
  2. Steam them for about a minute and serve with creamy salted butter.

Sow thistle and chickpea fritters

Makes 6 fritters (2–3 portions)

Written for common sow thistle (Sonchus oleraceus) in Croatia; the recipe names prickly sow thistle as edible too, and any edible greens can stand in.

Method
  1. Wash and coarsely chop 140 g leaves.
  2. Heat 2 tbsp water or 1 tbsp oil in a pan over low to medium heat, add the leaves and cook until tender, a minute or two.
  3. Drain 250 g cooked chickpeas, keeping 150 ml of their cooking liquid (aquafaba).
  4. Whisk the aquafaba with 100 g spelt flour, a pinch of baking soda and a pinch of salt.
  5. Mash the chickpeas with a fork, mix in the cooked leaves and a pinch of black pepper or other spices, then stir in the batter.
  6. Heat 1–2 tbsp oil in a pan (or use a dry non-stick pan) over low to medium heat, spoon in ladlefuls and cook 2–3 minutes a side, until golden-brown on both sides.
Purslane Portulaca oleracea 1 recipe

Turkish-style salad with purslane

Serves 4–6

Ingredients
  • 1 small red onion, finely sliced
  • 30 ml (2 tbsp) pomegranate molasses
  • the zest and juice of 1 lemon
  • 5 ml (1 tsp) salt
  • 125 ml (½ cup) pitted green olives
  • 6 quartered tomatoes
  • 1 cucumber quartered lengthways and sliced
  • 250 ml (1 cup) finely chopped parsley and/or coriander
  • 5 sprigs of roughly chopped mint
  • 125 ml (½ cup) purslane tips and leaves
  • 125 ml (½ cup) roughly chopped walnuts
  • 15 ml (1 tbsp) sumac
  • 1.25 ml (¼ tsp) cracked black pepper
  • 60 ml (¼ cup) extra-virgin olive oil
Method
  1. Mix the onion, molasses, lemon and salt in a large bowl and leave it to marinate while you prepare the rest, adding each to the bowl as you go.
  2. Toss and serve.
Pōhutukawa Metrosideros excelsa 1 recipe

Pōhutukawa fizzy drink

Makes 2.5 litres

Ingredients
  • 1 litre (4 cups) pōhutukawa stamens (stripped from the flowers)
  • 250 ml (1 cup) crushed strawberries
  • the flowers of 1 elderflower head (optional)
  • 375 ml (1½ cups) golden sugar
  • 1 sliced lemon
  • 2.5 litres water
Method
  1. The fizz comes from the wild yeasts on the flowers and strawberries, so use spray-free strawberries and don't wash either; keep as much of the pollen as you can when picking.
  2. Stir the sugar, water and lemon in a large jar until the sugar dissolves, then add the stamens, elderflowers and strawberries and push them under.
  3. Cover with muslin held by a rubber band, and date it.
  4. Stir hard every day for 3–4 days; it is fermenting when the flowers rise and bubbles appear as you stir.
  5. Strain through muslin into flip-top bottles and leave at room temperature for 1–2 days, checking the fizz, then refrigerate once it is as fizzy as you like.
  6. Serve cold; the cold slows the fermentation, so it keeps a long time in the fridge.

Stir-fried sow thistle and pork

Serves 2

Written for sow thistle growing in Pennsylvania; the blog does not name the species.

Method
  1. Slice 225 g pork shoulder (pork butt) into thin strips about 5 cm long and 3 mm thick.
  2. Toss with 1 tbsp soy sauce (or teriyaki sauce), 1 tbsp white cooking wine, a pinch of sugar, salt, pepper and a pinch of cornflour, cover and leave in the fridge for about 30 minutes.
  3. Clean and coarsely chop half a shopping bag of leaves.
  4. Heat oil in a frying pan and fry ½ tsp grated ginger on high heat for a few minutes, then add 1 coarsely chopped spring onion for a few minutes more.
  5. Add the pork and stir-fry for about 4 minutes, until no longer pink.
  6. Turn the heat to medium, add the leaves and keep stirring, adding a little water if needed, until tender, about 5 minutes.
  7. Chicken or beef can replace the pork.

Buttery sow thistle

Serves 2

Written for sow thistle growing in Pennsylvania; the blog does not name the species.

Method
  1. Chop 4 big handfuls of leaves into bite-size pieces.
  2. Melt 3 tbsp butter in a large frying pan, add the leaves and stir for a minute.
  3. Add 125 ml chicken stock, cover and simmer for 10 minutes, adding more stock as needed.
  4. Stir in a pinch of nutmeg, pepper and 2 tsp flour, and a pinch of salt if you like.

Method

  2. Fry finely chopped garlic in oil for a few minutes, add the leaves and stir until tender.
  3. Season with salt, pepper and nutmeg.

Sow thistle with red onion, goat cheese and pine nuts

Serves 4

Written for sow thistle growing in Pennsylvania; the blog does not name the species.

Method
  1. Rinse and chop half a shopping bag (packed) of leaves, boil for a few minutes and drain.
  2. Sauté a quarter of a red onion, thinly sliced, in olive oil in a large pan for 3 minutes, add a minced clove of garlic and cook a minute more.
  3. Turn the heat to medium and add the leaves a handful at a time, stirring, as they shrink.
  4. Add about 60 ml chicken stock, cover and simmer for 10 minutes.
  5. Season with salt, pepper and nutmeg, then stir in a couple of tablespoons of crumbled goat cheese and some pine nuts.

Pork wrapped in sow thistle leaves, slow-cooked in coconut milk

Serves 2

Written for sow thistle growing in Pennsylvania; the blog does not name the species.

Ingredients
  • A stand-in for Hawaiian pork laulau
  • using big older leaves in place of taro
Method
  1. Season two 115 g pieces of pork shoulder with salt, pepper and slices of garlic (1–2 cloves).
  2. Lay each on a bed of big old leaves, fold the leaves into a parcel and tie with cooking twine.
  3. Put the parcels in a slow cooker with 1 stalk of lemongrass, cut in pieces, pour over 2 cans of coconut milk and cook on low for at least 8 hours, turning the parcels after 4.
  4. Serve with rice, drizzled with fish sauce and/or mirin, and fried onions.

Sow thistle and hazelnut salsa

Serves 4

Written for smooth sow thistle (Sonchus oleraceus) in Britain.

Method
  1. Soak 100 g hazelnuts in boiling water for an hour, then drain and rinse well.
  2. Soak 60 g leaves in cold water for an hour, then strip the blades off the larger leaves and discard their stalks; small leaves can go in whole, as their stalks are not yet stringy.
  3. Crush 2 garlic cloves.
  4. Pulse the hazelnuts, shredded leaves, 15 g parsley, the garlic, ¼ tsp sea salt, 2 tsp Korean red chilli flakes and 4 tbsp olive oil in a food processor until chopped and thick, not runny.
  5. Spoon over 10 chopped cherry tomatoes and fork them through.
  6. Serve as a sauce with grilled meat, or with nut loaf, baked tempeh, roast vegetables or potatoes.

Makes 6 fritters (2–3 portions)

Buttered young sow thistle leaves

Written for young common sow thistle (Sonchus oleraceus) leaves 5–10 cm long, picked while they are not bitter.

Method
  1. Heat butter or oil in a pan, add 1–2 handfuls of leaves and stir until coated.
  2. Add a generous splash of beef stock or water, cover and simmer for 5–10 minutes.
  3. Stir in a pinch of nutmeg, 1 tsp flour, salt and pepper, then melt in another knob of butter over low heat.

Weedolata (wild-greens gremolata)

Makes about 750 ml (3 cups)

The author's greens were dock, onion weed, nasturtium, plantain, baby wild mustard, dandelion, sow thistle and a violet leaf.

Ingredients
  • 2 crushed garlic cloves
  • the zest and juice of 1 lemon
  • 500 ml (2 cups) firmly packed wild greens, sliced very thinly
  • 2.5 ml (½ tsp) salt
  • 1.25 ml (¼ tsp) cracked black pepper
  • 125 ml (½ cup) roughly chopped pistachios, walnuts, almonds or brazil nuts
  • 60 ml (¼ cup) hemp hearts, pumpkin or sunflower seeds
  • 60 ml (¼ cup) extra-virgin olive oil, hemp seed oil or a mix
Method
  1. To slice the greens, sort them into similar shapes, bunch and twist a few leaves together and slice as thinly as you can — this suits the tougher, stringier ones such as plantain, mustard and dock.
  2. Toss everything together.
  3. Serve as a side or salad, in a wrap or sandwich, or with pasta, risotto, eggs, or sausages and mash.
  4. It keeps at least 4 days in a sealed jar in the fridge.

Wild greens soup

Serves 4

The author's greens were puha, sow thistle, nasturtium, onion weed, wild fennel, oxalis, wild mustard and wild celery.

Ingredients
  • 1 diced onion
  • 1 potato, scrubbed and diced
  • 2 sliced garlic cloves
  • 500 ml (2 cups) roughly chopped wild greens
  • 45 ml (3 tbsp) olive oil
  • 1.3 litres water
  • 5 ml (1 tsp) salt
  • 30 ml (2 tbsp) nutritional yeast (optional)
  • 1.25 ml (¼ tsp) cracked pepper
Method
  1. Cut the vegetables and greens to similar sizes.
  2. Fry the onion in the oil for a minute, then add the potato and garlic (the method also adds fennel bulb and celery stalks, which the ingredient list leaves out) and fry for another minute.
  3. Add the water, cover, bring to the boil and simmer until the potato is soft.
  4. Add the greens with the salt and yeast and cook for 3 minutes, until just tender — added at the end, they keep their colour.
  5. Blend until smooth (push it through a sieve if your blender isn't powerful), season with the pepper and more salt if needed, and serve drizzled with extra-virgin olive or hemp seed oil and scattered with edible weed flowers.

Watercress and puha salad

Written for watercress and puha together.

Ingredients
Balsamic reduction (makes about 600 ml; you need 45 ml)
  • 1 litre balsamic vinegar
  • 150 g unrefined cane sugar
  • 1 rosemary stalk
  • half a diced red onion (optional)
Method
  1. Raw watercress: use only watercress that is bought or cultivated, or picked from water with no sheep or cattle grazing above it (see this entry's liver fluke warning).
  2. Stir until the sugar dissolves, bring to the boil, then simmer, stirring often, until reduced by half.
  3. Cool, remove the rosemary and bottle it.
  4. Maple walnuts: toast 125 ml (½ cup) walnuts on a lined tray in a 180 °C oven for 10 minutes, then stir through 5 ml (1 tsp) ground cinnamon, 30 ml (2 tbsp) maple syrup and 2.5 ml (½ tsp) salt and return to the oven for 2 minutes; cool.
  5. Salad (serves 4): scrunch half a finely sliced red onion with 45 ml (3 tbsp) balsamic reduction and 5 ml (1 tsp) salt in a large bowl and leave it while you prepare the rest, adding each to the bowl without mixing: 1 orange, peeled and segmented; 1 litre (4 cups) watercress, the stems finely chopped (they can be stringy) and the leaves roughly chopped; 250 ml (1 cup) young puha leaves, roughly chopped; 4 finely sliced radishes; and 250 ml (1 cup) ruruhau, mizuna, alfalfa or broccoli sprouts (optional).
  6. Drizzle with 30 ml (2 tbsp) extra-virgin olive oil, add the walnuts, toss and serve.
Quince Cydonia oblonga 1 recipe

Tamarillo and quince baked cheesecake

Makes one 26 cm cake

Quinces must be cooked; the author warns not to bite into a raw one.

Ingredients
Base
  • 175 g softened butter
  • 115 g unrefined cane sugar
  • 100 g (1 cup) plain flour
  • 50 g rice flour
  • 50 g cornflour
  • 100 g coarsely ground macadamia nuts
Filling
  • 370 g cream cheese at room temperature
  • 350 g ricotta
  • 60 g (¼ cup) liquid honey
  • 6 eggs
  • 250 g unrefined cane sugar
  • 5 ml (1 tsp) vanilla paste
  • 30 ml (2 tbsp) cornflour mixed with 30 ml (2 tbsp) water
Topping
  • 2 quinces
  • the juice and zest of 1 lemon
  • 3 tamarillos
  • 125 g unrefined cane sugar
Method
  1. Line a 26 cm springform tin and heat the oven to 160 °C.
  2. Cream the butter and sugar, mix in the flours and nuts on slow speed until it just comes together, press it evenly into the tin, prick it and bake 20 minutes.
  3. Beat the cream cheese, ricotta and honey until smooth and scrape into a large bowl.
  4. Whisk the eggs, sugar and vanilla on medium-high for about 10 minutes, until light and fluffy; fold half into the cheese mixture, whisk in the cornflour paste, then fold in the rest gently so you keep the air.
  5. Pour onto the base (set on top, not coloured) and bake 1 hour 40 minutes, until set with a slight wobble.
  6. For the topping, peel, core and chop the quinces, cover with water and cook until soft, then add the lemon, the scooped-out tamarillo flesh and the sugar, simmer and stir 10 minutes, cool and blend or mash.
  7. When the cake is cold, remove the tin's ring, chill about an hour, move it to a plate and spread the purée on top.
Red clover Trifolium pratense 10 recipes

Red clover tabbouleh

Ingredients
  • A handful of young red clover leaves and 3 flower heads
  • 4 tbsp couscous
  • a quarter of a red pepper
  • half an onion
  • 2 cloves of garlic
  • the juice of half a lemon
  • 1 tbsp olive oil and salt
Method
  1. Make the couscous up and let it cool.
  2. Whisk the lemon juice, oil and salt into a dressing.
  3. Chop the leaves, pepper, onion and garlic finely, pull the flower heads apart, and fold everything through the couscous.
  4. Leave it to sit for 15 minutes before serving so the dressing soaks in.

Red clover lemonade

Ingredients
  • About 700 ml of flower heads
  • 1 litre of water
  • 250 ml lemon juice
  • 200 g sugar
Method
  1. Bring the flower heads and water to the boil and simmer for 10 minutes.
  2. Add the lemon juice and sugar and simmer another 5 minutes, stirring until the sugar has gone.
  3. Let it cool, strain the flowers out, and chill.

Red clover jam

Ingredients
  • About 500 ml of flower heads packed down, with no stems or leaves
  • 600 ml boiling water
  • 60 ml lemon juice
  • 800 g sugar
  • a sachet of pectin
Method
  1. Pour the boiling water over the flower heads and leave them to infuse overnight, then strain and press the liquid out.
  2. Bring the infusion, lemon juice, sugar and pectin to a rolling boil for about 20 minutes, testing on a cold saucer until it sets, then jar it hot.

Red clover rice

Ingredients
  • 500 ml brown or wild rice
  • 500 ml red clover florets pulled out of the flower heads
  • 125 ml butter
  • 1 tsp salt
Method
  1. Cook the rice in 1.5 litres of water until done.
  2. While it is still hot, mix in the florets, butter and salt, and serve hot.
  3. For a sweet version, add 125 ml honey and some chopped nuts; quinoa or another grain can stand in for the rice.

Red clover rice salad

Ingredients
  • 500 ml rice
  • 45 ml olive oil
  • 90 ml lemon juice
  • 1½ tsp salt
  • 6 spring onions minced
  • 250 ml walnuts or other nuts
  • 250 ml chopped dried fruit (raisins, cranberries, dates or apricots)
  • 500 ml red clover flowers pulled out of the flower heads
Method
  1. Cook the rice in 1.5 litres of water until done, without stirring it while it cooks or cools, and let it cool completely so it does not go sticky.
  2. Mix everything together thoroughly and serve chilled or at room temperature.
  3. Wild rice, alone or half and half with brown or white rice, makes it special; boil the wild rice separately, since it cooks in a different time, and mix the two once cooked.

Red clover blossom syrup

Makes a little over 500 ml

Ingredients
  • About 1 litre of red clover blossoms
  • 1 litre water
  • 4 cups (about 800 g) sugar
  • if you like, half a lemon or orange chopped up, peel and all

Leave the citrus out for the pure clover flavour.

Method
  1. Put the blossoms and water in a pot and simmer gently for 15–20 minutes, then turn off the heat, cover and leave overnight.
  2. Next day, strain and press the liquid out of the spent flowers.
  3. Add the sugar and citrus and heat slowly, stirring now and then, for several hours, until it has reduced to a thick, honey-like syrup, then bottle it in jars.
  4. It comes out brownish, like a light maple syrup; a tablespoon of beetroot or berry juice colours it.

Red clover pancakes

Ingredients
  • 250 ml wholemeal flour
  • 250 ml white flour
  • 1 tsp salt
  • 2 tsp baking powder
  • 2 eggs
  • 60 ml oil
  • 125 ml honey or red clover blossom syrup
  • 500 ml milk
  • 250 ml red clover flowers pulled out of the flower heads
Method
  1. Mix the dry ingredients first, then add all the wet ingredients before mixing them in thoroughly, which keeps the batter from going lumpy.
  2. Add a little milk or flour until the batter pours but is not runny.
  3. Cook on an oiled griddle and serve with butter and red clover blossom syrup.

Red clover almond biscuits

Ingredients
  • 500 ml wholemeal flour
  • 3 tsp baking powder
  • 60 ml butter
  • 125 ml almonds
  • 125 ml buttermilk
  • 2 eggs
  • ¼ tsp almond extract
  • 375 ml red clover flowers pulled out of the flower heads
Method
  1. Whizz the flour, baking powder and almonds in a food processor until the almonds are chopped fine, add the butter and whizz again to a crumbly mixture, then add the eggs, buttermilk, almond extract and clover and whizz just until the dough forms a lump.
  2. Shape into biscuits and bake on an ungreased tray at 230 °C for 10–15 minutes, until golden brown.
  3. They are sweet, dessert-like biscuits; serve hot with butter and jam.

Red clover sun tea

Method
  1. Put red clover flowers in a jar with some mint, a few slices of lemon and lime, and anything else good to hand, fill it with water and stand it in the sun for a while.
Red deadnettle Lamium purpureum 5 recipes

Red deadnettle rice pilaf

Serves 4

Written for Lamium purpureum in the United States.

Method
  1. Rinse about 2 cups of red deadnettle leaves and pat dry.
  2. Melt 2 tbsp butter in a lidded saucepan, soften 1 large diced shallot for 4–6 minutes, add 2 minced garlic cloves for a minute or two, then stir in 1 cup long-grain rice to coat it in the butter.
  3. Add the leaves and cook until wilted, 3–5 minutes, with 1 tsp salt.
  4. Pour in 2 cups (about 500 ml) chicken stock, bring to the boil, cover and simmer for 15 minutes, then fluff with a fork.

Deadnettle and chickweed fritters

Makes 4

Written for Lamium purpureum.

Method
  1. Melt 1 tbsp coconut oil in a frying pan over medium-low heat, add about 60 g finely chopped red deadnettle flowering tops and 2 minced garlic cloves and sauté for about 10 minutes, until the garlic just browns.
  2. Add about 45 g finely chopped chickweed and cook until tender, about 2 minutes.
  3. Tip into a bowl, mix in 1 beaten egg, add another 1 tbsp oil to the pan, and fry the mixture as 4 flattened fritters 5–7 cm across, 1–2 minutes a side, until brown and crisp.

Deadnettle with mint and smoked garlic

Serves 2

Written for Lamium purpureum.

Method
  1. Warm 1 tbsp butter or olive oil over low heat and sauté 1 finely chopped smoked garlic clove for 1–3 minutes.
  2. Add about 85 g red deadnettle flowering tops and 15 wild mint leaves and cook for about 5 minutes.
  3. Season with the juice of half a lime, salt and pepper, and scatter a few fresh deadnettle flowers on top.

Red deadnettle rice bake

Serves 4

Written for Lamium purpureum; stinging nettle, dandelion or spinach work too.

Method
  1. Heat the oven to 180 °C and grease a 20 cm square baking dish.
  2. Warm 1 tbsp olive oil in a heavy frying pan over medium heat and sauté 1 small chopped onion, 1 minced garlic clove and 1 cup (about 250 ml) washed, dried red deadnettle leaves for 2 minutes.
  3. Tip into a bowl and mix well with 1 cup cooked and cooled rice, 1 egg, 1 cup (250 ml) milk, ¼ cup grated cheddar, 1 tbsp soy sauce, and 1 tsp each of salt and pepper.
  4. Pour into the dish and bake for 30 minutes.
  5. Serve straight away.

Red deadnettle pesto

Written for Lamium purpureum.

Method
  1. Pick the leafy flowering tops, rinse and dry them.
  2. In a food processor, blend 3 packed cups of the tops with 5 garlic cloves, ⅓ cup nuts (pecans, walnuts or pine nuts), 1 tsp Italian seasoning, 2½ tbsp lemon juice, 2–4 pinches of sugar and ¾ cup grated parmesan.
  3. With the motor running, drizzle in ½–1 cup (125–250 ml) olive oil until it is as thick as you like, then taste and adjust the seasoning.
  4. To freeze it, leave out the parmesan and stir it in after thawing.
Red raspberry Rubus idaeus 2 recipes

Wild raspberry jam

Written for wild raspberries.

Method
  1. Fills 2 jam jars.
  2. About 570 ml (1 UK pint) wild raspberries, pressed down, and about 285 ml (½ pint) sugar — half the volume of the berries.
  3. Put a small saucer in the freezer.
  4. Stir well, then heat on low to medium, stirring until the last of the sugar has dissolved.
  5. Bring to a gentle boil; once bubbles cover the surface, skim off the froth and boil for 12 minutes (longer for a bigger batch).
  6. Take off the heat and drop a blob on the cold saucer: as it cools it should set to jam.
  7. If not, boil 2 minutes more and test again.
  8. Pour into sterilised jars and seal with sterilised lids.
  9. It is ready once cool and keeps six months unopened in a cupboard.
  10. It uses about half the sugar of many jams.

Put the berries and sugar in a heavy-based pan, mash them gently with a potato masher and leave somewhere warm for half an hour so the sugar starts to dissolve in the juice; the author says this cold step keeps more of the flavour.

Raspberry fool

Serves 6

Written for raspberries; the page names no species.

Ingredients
  • 800 g raspberries
  • 55 g icing sugar plus extra for dusting
  • 2 teaspoons rosewater
  • 2 drops vanilla essence
  • the finely grated zest of ¼ lemon
  • 450 ml cream (35% fat)
Method
  1. Put 300 g of the raspberries into 6 glasses.
  2. Blend 350 g more with the icing sugar to a thick purée, then stir in the rosewater, vanilla and lemon zest.
  3. Set aside 170 g of the purée and drizzle a generous tablespoon of the rest over the berries in each glass.
  4. Whip the cream to soft peaks and fold the reserved 170 g of purée through it.
  5. Share this between the glasses, spoon over the last of the purée, top with the remaining raspberries and dust with icing sugar.
  6. The original serves it with home-made sponge fingers (6 eggs, 180 g caster sugar, 180 g plain flour, piped and baked at 220 °C for 7–9 minutes); bought ones will do.
Rose geranium Pelargonium ×asperum 10 recipes

Rose geranium syrup

Method
  1. Put 250 ml water, 200 g sugar and 10–12 rose geranium leaves in a small saucepan, bruising the leaves against the side of the pan with a spoon.
  2. Bring to the boil over moderate heat, cover, take off the heat and leave to stand for at least 30 minutes.
  3. Lift out the leaves and squeeze them into the syrup.
  4. Keeps in the fridge for up to a month, or frozen for up to a year.
  5. The original uses it in a chocolate dessert.

Apple and rose geranium jelly

Makes about eight 250 ml jars

Method
  1. Firmly pack about 125 ml of fresh rose geranium leaves and tender stems.
  2. Bring them to the boil with part of 750 ml apple juice, take off the heat and steep for at least 15–20 minutes.
  3. Strain, add the rest of the juice and top up to 750 ml if needed.
  4. Put the juice in a heavy, large saucepan over high heat, add one packet of powdered fruit pectin and bring to a full rolling boil that cannot be stirred down.
  5. Add 1.2 kg sugar, keep stirring and bring back to a full rolling boil; boil hard for 1 minute, stirring all the time.
  6. Take off the heat, skim off the foam quickly and pour at once into sterilised jars — a whole leaf can be pressed into each jar.
  7. Seal and process for 5 minutes in a hot water bath, following the jar maker's instructions.
  8. The ingredient list also has 30 ml lemon juice, but the method does not say when it goes in.
  9. Use it to fill or glaze cakes, as a sauce for fresh fruit, or with poultry.

Pineapple and rose geranium ice

Method
  1. Warm 1 litre pineapple juice slightly, add 10 rose geranium leaves and bruise them gently against the side of the container with a spoon.
  2. Cover and steep for 30 minutes, then take out the leaves.
  3. Stir in 400 g sugar and 125 ml lime juice until dissolved (warm it a little to speed this up).
  4. Cool, then freeze in an ice-cream maker, or pour into a shallow tray in the freezer and stir with a fork every 30–45 minutes until frozen.
  5. Garnish with scented geranium leaves and flowers.

Rose geranium cream

Method
  1. Heat 300 ml fresh cream, 4 tablespoons caster sugar and 4–6 rose geranium leaves in a pot, stirring often, until just short of boiling; do not let it boil.
  2. Take off the heat and cool.
  3. Mash 6–8 boysenberries (fresh, or frozen and thawed) to a purée.
  4. Beat 250 g sour cream (or cream cheese) until smooth, then beat in the purée.
  5. Strain the leaves out of the cream and fold the cream gently into the berry mixture with a spoon.
  6. Cover and chill for several hours or overnight.
  7. Serve on fresh berries or fruit salad instead of cream or yoghurt.

Rose geranium rhubarb crumble

Method
  1. Roughly chop 6–8 rose geranium leaves and warm them gently with the juice of two oranges plus about 190 ml more orange juice; bring almost to the boil, take off the heat and leave to infuse as it cools.
  2. Cut 10–12 trimmed rhubarb stalks into 1 cm pieces — enough to fill, not overfill, the baking dish.
  3. In a large bowl mix about 150 g brown sugar, 2 tablespoons cornflour and the grated rind of the two oranges, tip in the rhubarb and mix well.
  4. Grease the dish and spread the rhubarb mixture back into it.
  5. Strain the juice over it.
  6. For the topping, mix about 150 g brown sugar, about 90 g whole rolled oats, about 90 g fine rolled oats and about 125 ml mixed seeds and chopped nuts (such as pumpkin, sesame and sunflower seeds, walnuts, almonds), then rub in 2 tablespoons soft butter.
  7. Press the topping firmly over the rhubarb and around the edges.
  8. Bake at 180 °C for 45 minutes.

Rose geranium sugar

Method
  1. Fill a dry glass jar with alternate layers of rose geranium leaves and caster sugar.
  2. Cover tightly and leave somewhere warm for a week or more, shaking it now and then.
  3. Sieve out the leaves before use, and sprinkle the sugar on custards, biscuits or sponge cakes.

Rose geranium lemonade

Method
  1. Boil 250 ml water with 200 g sugar in a small pot.
  2. Take off the heat, add 10 clean rose geranium leaves and leave until cold, overnight if you can.
  3. Squeeze the leaves out well (repeat with fresh leaves for a stronger flavour).
  4. Add 250 ml fresh lemon or lime juice, bottle and refrigerate; it keeps for up to two weeks.
  5. To serve, stir 1 tablespoon of the concentrate into each cup of chilled water and pour over ice, garnished with a small scented geranium leaf or rose petals.

Berries in rose geranium cream

Serves 6

Method
  1. Put 250 ml cream (heavy cream in the original), 2 tablespoons finely chopped rose geranium leaves and about 50 g sugar in a metal bowl over a pot of simmering water.
  2. Stir until the sugar dissolves and the cream is hot but not boiling, about 5 minutes.
  3. Take off the heat, cool a little, then chill until cold, about 45 minutes.
  4. Strain and discard the leaves.
  5. Beat about 115 g softened cream cheese with the cream using an electric mixer until thickened but not holding peaks, about 2 minutes.
  6. Divide about 370 g blackberries and 225 g blueberries between six bowls or glasses and spoon the cream over.
  7. The cream can be beaten up to 4 hours ahead and kept covered in the fridge.

Rose geranium shortbread

Makes 8 wedges

Method
  1. Cream about 170 g room-temperature unsalted butter with about 60 g icing sugar until light and fluffy.
  2. Mix about 190 g plain flour with ¼ teaspoon salt, add it to the butter, then mix in 1 teaspoon rose water.
  3. Gather into a ball, wrap and refrigerate for 4–6 hours.
  4. Let it soften a little.
  5. Butter and flour the base of a 20 cm round tin.
  6. On baking paper, pat the dough out to the size of the tin.
  7. Press 8 large, washed and dried rose geranium leaves into the dough in a circle about 2.5 cm in from the edge, lay the dough in the tin leaf side down and pat it out to the edges.
  8. Score it lightly into 8 wedges, sprinkle with 2 tablespoons sugar (or scented sugar) and chill for 45 minutes.
  9. Bake at 160 °C for about 20 minutes, until it just starts to colour.
  10. Cool in the tin, turn out, peel the baked leaves off the base, cut along the marks and serve with fresh rose geranium leaves.

Scented geranium and lemon icing

Written for scented geranium leaves, "preferably with a lemon scent"; no species named.

Method
  1. Tear 2–3 scented geranium leaves into small pieces and mix them with about 250 g sifted icing sugar in a small bowl.
  2. Leave somewhere warm overnight so the sugar takes up the scent, then pick out and discard the leaf pieces.
  3. Beat a generous 60 ml softened cream cheese, 2 teaspoons softened butter and 1 teaspoon grated lemon rind, gradually adding the scented icing sugar, until smooth.
  4. Spread over a cooled carrot cake and decorate, if you like, with lemon balm leaves or candied lemon peel.

Pineapple and rose geranium ice

Written for "rose-scented geranium" leaves without naming a species.

Method

Rowan tree Sorbus aucuparia 4 recipes

Rowan bud syrup

Written for rowan buds.

Method
  1. They contain a cyanogenic glycoside (see Warnings), so treat this as a flavouring in small amounts.
  2. Put young rowan flower buds and very young leaf buds in a pan and cover them with cold water, measuring the water.
  3. Add 100 g sugar per 100 ml of water, or up to twice that for a thicker syrup that keeps better.
  4. Heat, stirring to dissolve the sugar, and hold it at about 70 °C for a few minutes; the author simmers for no more than 5 minutes, as the almond flavour fades fast with heat.
  5. Leave to cool and strain into sterilised bottles, keeping the syrupy buds for the liqueur.
  6. Add 10% vodka to help it keep, or store it in the fridge, or freeze it in plastic bottles.

Rowan bud liqueur

Written for rowan buds.

Method
  1. They contain a cyanogenic glycoside (see Warnings), so treat this as a flavouring in small amounts.
  2. Put the syrup-coated buds left from the rowan bud syrup in a jar, cover with cheap vodka and leave for about 4 weeks, shaking now and then.
  3. Strain, then sweeten to taste with the rowan bud syrup — the author aims for about 20–25% alcohol.

Adding more fresh buds to the vodka strengthens the almond flavour, but also extracts more of the cyanogenic glycoside; the author says to drink correspondingly less.

The Godfather of Soil

Written for rowan buds.

Method
  1. They contain a cyanogenic glycoside (see Warnings), so treat this as a flavouring in small amounts.
  2. Stir 20 ml rowan bud liqueur and 60 ml whisky (Scotch, or bourbon) over ice.
  3. Bruise a rowan bud and float it on top for its aroma.

The Godfather of Sour

Written for rowan buds.

Method
  1. They contain a cyanogenic glycoside (see Warnings), so treat this as a flavouring in small amounts.
  2. Shake 30 ml whisky (bourbon is best here), 30 ml rowan bud liqueur, 30 ml lemon juice, 10 ml rowan bud syrup (or to taste) and 1 egg white with ice.
  3. Strain out the ice and shake again (a reverse dry shake, which makes the egg white creamy; leave it out if you prefer), then fine-strain into a glass.
Saffron milk cap / Pine mushroom Lactarius deliciosus 7 recipes

Sautéed saffron milk caps

Ingredients
  • 12 medium caps
  • 15 ml olive oil
  • salt and pepper
Method
  1. Brush the caps clean — rinse only if they are really dirty — and pat them dry.
  2. Cut them to size, or leave the small ones whole.
  3. Get the oil hot in a skillet over medium-high, add the mushrooms, season, and stir now and then for about 5 minutes until they have shrunk down and cooked through.
  4. Serve hot alongside something else.

Pickled saffron milk caps

Ingredients
  • 250 g caps, cleaned and sliced
  • 200 ml sherry or red wine vinegar
  • 200 ml water
  • 100 g sugar
  • 50 ml olive oil
  • 1 tsp salt
  • 5 black peppercorns
  • 3 cloves garlic
  • 3 juniper berries
  • 2 bay leaves
  • a sprig each of rosemary and thyme
Method
  1. To clean them first, soak them in 1.4 litres of water with 120 ml white vinegar.
  2. Bring everything but the mushrooms to the boil, add them, and simmer one minute.
  3. Off the heat, let it cool, then pack into a sterilised jar with the liquid above the mushrooms.
  4. Keeps up to 6 months in the fridge as long as they stay covered.

Saffron milk cap, sage and thyme strudel

Ingredients
  • 400 g caps sliced
  • 4 cloves garlic chopped
  • oil
  • 80 ml white wine
  • 125 g cream cheese
  • 3 tbsp sour cream
  • 2 sage leaves
  • 2 sprigs thyme
  • salt and pepper
  • 1 sheet butter puff pastry
  • 1 egg yolk beaten with 1 tsp water
Method
  1. Fry the garlic a minute in oil over medium heat, add the mushrooms and cook 5 minutes, adding oil as they take it.
  2. Stir in the wine, cream cheese, sour cream, sage and thyme and cook until creamy and tender, then season and cool completely — this can be done a day ahead.
  3. Heap the filling down the centre of the pastry sheet, seal the edges with the egg wash, press the ends with a fork, and bake at 220 °C for 30 minutes until golden.

Hungarian mushroom soup

Ingredients
  • An onion and 2 cloves garlic, finely diced
  • oil
  • 50 g butter
  • 3 tbsp flour
  • 200 g sliced caps
  • 3 tsp paprika
  • 600–700 ml chicken stock
  • 60 ml sour cream
  • parsley
Method
  1. Fry the onion and garlic in oil a few minutes until translucent, melt the butter into them, then stir in the flour.
  2. Add the mushrooms, paprika and stock and simmer about 10 minutes until the mushrooms are done.
  3. Serve with a spoonful of sour cream and parsley.

Saffron milk cap risotto

Ingredients
  • 125 ml olive oil
  • 2 tbsp butter
  • 300 g caps with the stems off and thinly sliced
  • 3 sprigs thyme
  • an onion
  • 105 g arborio rice
  • 1 litre chicken stock
  • a pinch of saffron
  • 165 ml dry white wine
  • 125 ml cream
  • 25 g blue cheese
Method
  1. Sauté the mushrooms in half the oil with the butter over medium-high until coated, season, and set aside.
  2. Soften the diced onion in the rest of the oil over medium heat for 5–8 minutes without browning, while the stock warms with the saffron in another pan.
  3. Stir the rice through the onion, then add the hot stock a ladle at a time, letting each one absorb, for 15–20 minutes until the rice is cooked.
  4. Stir in the wine, blue cheese and cream, fold through half the mushrooms, and season.
  5. Plate it with the remaining mushrooms on top and chopped chives or parsley.

Saffron milk cap and ash brie tart

Ingredients
Pastry
  • 480 g plain flour
  • 150 g cold cubed butter
  • 240 ml finely grated parmesan
  • 2.5 ml fine salt
  • 1 egg
  • 120–160 ml ice water
Filling
  • 5 caps
  • 4 cloves garlic sliced
  • olive oil
  • 150 g ash brie sliced
  • 3 eggs
  • 240 ml cream
  • 240 ml parmesan
  • 2 sprigs thyme
Method
  1. Clean the mushrooms in 1.4 litres of water with 120 ml white vinegar.
  2. Pulse the flour, butter, cheese and salt in a food processor, add the egg and 120 ml water and run it until it starts to come together, then knead on a floured bench with more water if it needs it and rest it 30 minutes at room temperature.
  3. Line an oiled 31 × 21 cm loose-bottomed tart tin, trim, and chill 45 minutes or freeze 15.
  4. Meanwhile fry the garlic then the mushrooms — whole if small, sliced if not — in oil over medium heat only until they keep their bright orange, then season and cool a little.
  5. Blind bake at 200 °C under oiled foil and weights for 20 minutes, then 10–15 minutes uncovered.
  6. Drop the oven to 150 °C, whisk the eggs, cream and parmesan, pour it into the case, arrange the mushrooms and brie on top with the thyme, and bake 30–35 minutes until just set.
  7. Serve warm.

Saffron milk cap and chestnut gnocchi

Ingredients
  • 500 g desiree potatoes
  • 125 g Italian 00 flour plus extra for the bench
  • 40 g chestnut flour (or just more 00)
  • 2 tbsp oil
  • an onion sliced
  • 2 cloves garlic sliced thinly
  • 250 g caps with the stalks off and sliced
  • 150 g bacon or prosciutto (optional)
  • 100 g roasted and peeled chestnuts
  • 100 g butter
  • salt and pepper
Method
  1. Boil the potatoes in their skins until tender, rice them, and cool completely before working the flours in.
  2. Roll the dough into logs, cut it into pieces, and shape each one against a fork.
  3. Boil them and lift them out as they float.
  4. Fry the onion and garlic until fragrant, add the mushrooms until the edges crisp, then the bacon, chestnuts and butter.
  5. Add the drained gnocchi and fry until golden all over, then season.
Samphire Salicornia quinqueflora 1 recipe

Wild Whangaroa pesto

Makes about 350 g

The author's greens, picked from roadsides around Whangaroa Harbour, were nasturtium, kawakawa, fennel, samphire, fat hen, wild mustard, cleavers and clover; the author calls it marsh samphire, picked around the Whangaroa area. The author warns that New Zealand roadsides are still sprayed with glyphosate, so pick with care.

Ingredients
  • 1 garlic clove (or 4 peeled onion weed bulbs)
  • 60 ml (¼ cup) macadamia nuts and 60 ml (¼ cup) grated parmesan
  • dairy or plant-based — or 125 ml (½ cup) macadamias and no parmesan — 750 ml (3 cups) roughly chopped wild greens
  • the zest and juice of 1 lemon
  • 125 ml extra-virgin olive oil
  • salt and pepper
Method
  1. Blend in a food processor to the texture you like.
  2. Serve on crackers or toast, as a side, or stirred through soup, pasta or rice.

Sea celery herb butter

Shamrock wood sorrel Oxalis articulata 12 recipes

Wood sorrel sauce

Ingredients
  • 500 ml (2 cups) wood sorrel leaves
  • 30 ml (2 tbsp) butter
  • 7.5 ml (1½ tsp) lemon juice
  • 7.5 ml (1½ tsp) maple syrup or honey
Method
  1. Melt the butter in a saucepan, add the sorrel and stir for 1–2 minutes, until it wilts.
  2. Put it in a blender or food processor with the lemon juice and the syrup or honey and blend until well combined.
  3. Serve it straight away, or refrigerate it and warm it before use; the author finds it even better the next day.

The author serves it on fish, or as a dipping sauce for grilled prawns.

Wood sorrel 'lemonade'

Ingredients
  • About 500 ml (2 cups) wood sorrel leaves, stems or flowers, or a mix
  • about 250 ml (1 cup) water
  • maple syrup or another sweetener to taste
Method
  1. The amounts are rough: use as much sorrel as you like and enough water to cover it.
  2. Blend the sorrel and water until well combined, strain through a fine sieve and sweeten to taste.

Wood sorrel syrup

Ingredients
  • Equal parts wood sorrel
  • sugar and water

The author usually uses 250 ml (1 cup) of each.

Method
  1. Put everything in a saucepan and bring it to a simmer, stirring.
  2. Simmer for 15–30 minutes, strain through muslin (cheesecloth) and pour into sterilised bottles.
  3. Use it in tea, topped up with soda water, over ice cream or fresh strawberries, or in vinaigrettes and marinades.

Wood sorrel cooler

Ingredients
  • About 950 ml (1 US quart) water
  • 125 ml (½ cup) wood sorrel leaves, stems
  • flowers and seed pods
  • 15 ml (1 tbsp) agave nectar or honey
  • a dash of salt
Method
  1. Mix everything in a blender.
  2. If you can, leave it in the fridge overnight before drinking.

Wood sorrel tea

Method
  1. Fresh wood sorrel leaves and boiling water, one part leaves to three parts water.
  2. Put the leaves in a container, pour the boiling water over them and leave to steep for 5–15 minutes.
  3. Strain out the leaves and sweeten to taste if you like.
  4. Drink it hot, or pour it over ice for an iced tea.

Chilled lemony wood sorrel soup

Ingredients
  • 2 cloves garlic, minced
  • olive oil for cooking
  • 2 spring onions, chopped
  • 500 ml (2 cups) vegetable stock
  • 500 ml (2 cups) lightly packed wood sorrel leaves, with the flowers kept back for garnish
  • 3 medium mint leaves
  • 250 ml (1 cup) full-fat coconut milk
  • salt and pepper
Method
  1. Rinse the sorrel well and let it dry, then strip the leaves and flowers from the stems and throw away the bare stalks.
  2. Heat a little olive oil in a small saucepan and sauté the garlic until fragrant, then add the spring onions.
  3. Cover with the stock, bring to the boil and simmer for about 5 minutes, then add the sorrel for about 30 seconds.
  4. Take it off the heat, add the mint and blend smooth with a stick blender (or an ordinary blender).
  5. Stir in the coconut milk and season with salt and pepper.
  6. Refrigerate until well chilled, a couple of hours, and serve garnished with the sorrel flowers.

Spaghetti squash casserole with wood sorrel

Ingredients
  • About 450 g (1 lb) Italian or breakfast sausage
  • 1 medium spaghetti squash
  • several cups of wood sorrel, washed and drained, stems removed (the recipe gives no closer amount)
  • olive oil
  • 1 red onion, halved and sliced
  • 1 egg
  • 125 ml (½ cup) coconut milk or any milk
  • 1 garlic clove, minced
  • 15 ml (1 tbsp) garlic powder
  • 5 ml (1 tsp) tarragon
  • 5 ml (1 tsp) salt
  • 2.5 ml (½ tsp) black pepper
  • 1.25 ml (¼ tsp) ground sage
Method
  1. Heat the oven to 200 °C.
  2. Halve the squash, spoon out the seeds and stringy threads, set the halves cut side up on a baking tray, drizzle with olive oil, season with salt and pepper and bake for 20–25 minutes, until the flesh gives a little when pressed.
  3. Meanwhile cook the sausage and onion in a large pot over medium-high heat.
  4. When the sausage is cooked through, add the garlic and sauté for 2 minutes, then add the sorrel, milk and spices, cover and simmer until the sorrel wilts.
  5. Take the squash out of the oven and scrape out the flesh with a fork (careful, it will be hot).
  6. Combine the mixture with the egg, stirring thoroughly, spread it evenly in a baking dish (or the same pot, if it is ovenproof) and bake for 15–20 minutes, until the top is lightly browned.

Wood sorrel, kūmara and potato gratin

Ingredients
  • Two handfuls of wood sorrel, chopped
  • potato and kūmara, boiled first and sliced
  • cream
Method
  1. Layer the sorrel with the sliced potato and kūmara in a baking dish, pour cream over the top and bake at 180 °C until it starts to brown.

Potato salad with wood sorrel

Method
  1. Chop a big handful of wood sorrel leaves, stalks and flowers and mix them through a potato salad dressed with a cream dressing.

Wild weed spread

Makes about 600 g

The author makes it with chickweed, oxalis and onion weed together; the page does not say which oxalis (written for oxalis generally).

Ingredients
  • 500 ml (2 cups) raw cashews
  • 500 ml hot water
  • 80 ml (⅓ cup) extra-virgin olive oil
  • 1 garlic clove
  • the zest and juice of 1 lemon
  • 30 ml (2 tbsp) apple cider vinegar
  • 60 ml (¼ cup) nutritional yeast (or finely grated parmesan, or cream cheese)
  • 625 ml (2½ cups) wild greens, roughly chopped and tightly packed
  • 15 ml (1 tbsp) chopped thyme
  • dill
  • coriander or parsley
  • 5 ml (1 tsp) salt
  • 2.5 ml (½ tsp) pepper
Method
  1. Soak the cashews in the hot water for an hour, then drain them and throw the water away.
  2. Blend everything together until very smooth.
  3. The mixture is stiff, like cream cheese: a stick blender in a jug works best, and a jug blender may need a little water.
  4. Spread it on toast or crackers, or use it in sandwiches and burgers.

Spoon it into a jar or dish with a lid, pour a thin layer of olive oil over the top so it does not discolour, and keep it covered in the fridge; the author says it keeps for a couple of weeks.

Green goddess dip

Makes about 500 ml; it thickens in the fridge

The author's greens were nasturtium, chickweed, onion weed, dandelion, oxalis, dock, plantain and wild mustard; the page does not say which oxalis (written for oxalis generally).

Ingredients
  • 500 ml (2 cups) roughly chopped wild greens
  • 125 ml (½ cup) cashews, soaked for an hour in the hottest tap water and drained
  • the zest and juice of 1 lemon
  • 60 ml (¼ cup) apple cider vinegar
  • 60 ml (¼ cup) water, only if your blender needs it
  • 2 peeled garlic cloves
  • 60 ml (¼ cup) extra-virgin olive oil
  • 60 ml (¼ cup) hemp seed oil (or more olive oil)
  • 5 ml (1 tsp) salt
  • 1.25 ml (¼ tsp) pepper
  • 30 ml (2 tbsp) nutritional yeast (optional)
Method
  1. Blend in a high-speed blender until smooth.
  2. Use it as a dip or dressing — with chips, steamed potatoes or kūmara, roast vegetables, an omelette or quiche, or stirred through pasta.

Wild greens soup

Serves 4

The author's greens were puha, sow thistle, nasturtium, onion weed, wild fennel, oxalis, wild mustard and wild celery; the page does not say which oxalis (written for oxalis generally).

Ingredients
  • 1 diced onion
  • 1 potato, scrubbed and diced
  • 2 sliced garlic cloves
  • 500 ml (2 cups) roughly chopped wild greens
  • 45 ml (3 tbsp) olive oil
  • 1.3 litres water
  • 5 ml (1 tsp) salt
  • 30 ml (2 tbsp) nutritional yeast (optional)
  • 1.25 ml (¼ tsp) cracked pepper
Method
  1. Cut the vegetables and greens to similar sizes.
  2. Fry the onion in the oil for a minute, then add the potato and garlic (the method also adds fennel bulb and celery stalks, which the ingredient list leaves out) and fry for another minute.
  3. Add the water, cover, bring to the boil and simmer until the potato is soft.
  4. Add the greens with the salt and yeast and cook for 3 minutes, until just tender — added at the end, they keep their colour.
  5. Blend until smooth (push it through a sieve if your blender isn't powerful), season with the pepper and more salt if needed, and serve drizzled with extra-virgin olive or hemp seed oil and scattered with edible weed flowers.
Sheep sorrel Rumex acetosella 2 recipes

Sorrel soup

Serves 4 as a side

Written for sheep sorrel (adapted from Anja Dunk's sorrel soup).

Method
  1. Put 80–100 g sheep sorrel leaves (about 4 handfuls) and 60 g spinach leaves (2 large handfuls) in a bowl of water, swish them about, let the dirt settle, then lift them out and dry them.
  2. In a saucepan over a medium heat, melt 40 g butter, add 30 g plain flour and stir for 3 minutes, until light tan.
  3. Whisk in about 1 litre of hot vegetable stock in a slow stream, then barely simmer for 10 minutes, until thickened.
  4. Meanwhile heat 2 tbsp neutral oil in a frying pan and wilt the greens for 60 seconds (the sorrel turns yellowish).
  5. Stir the greens into the soup, blend, and season with salt and black pepper.

Sheep's sorrel salsa

Written for sheep sorrel (Alutiiq Museum, Kodiak, Alaska; recipe by Gayla Pedersen, there called a sauce or pesto).

Ingredients
Optional
  • 60 ml grated parmesan
  • 60 ml basil
  • ½ tsp black pepper
  • 1 tsp chilli flakes, or walnuts or oregano
Method
  1. A raw sauce, so keep it to a spoonful or two each — 60 ml of chopped leaves serves 2.
  2. Rinse the leaves well in cool water and chop them finely.
  3. Put 60 ml (¼ cup) chopped sheep sorrel in a jar with 45 ml (3 tbsp) olive oil, 15 ml (1 tbsp) red wine vinegar and ½ tsp salt, or a pinch to taste, and stir.
  4. Scale it up or down keeping 3 parts oil to 1 part vinegar; use a blender for a larger batch.
  5. Lemon or lime juice can stand in for the vinegar and any oil you like for the olive oil.
Snowdrop wood sorrel Oxalis magellanica 12 recipes

Wood sorrel sauce

Ingredients
  • 500 ml (2 cups) wood sorrel leaves
  • 30 ml (2 tbsp) butter
  • 7.5 ml (1½ tsp) lemon juice
  • 7.5 ml (1½ tsp) maple syrup or honey
Method
  1. Melt the butter in a saucepan, add the sorrel and stir for 1–2 minutes, until it wilts.
  2. Put it in a blender or food processor with the lemon juice and the syrup or honey and blend until well combined.
  3. Serve it straight away, or refrigerate it and warm it before use; the author finds it even better the next day.

The author serves it on fish, or as a dipping sauce for grilled prawns.

Wood sorrel 'lemonade'

Ingredients
  • About 500 ml (2 cups) wood sorrel leaves, stems or flowers, or a mix
  • about 250 ml (1 cup) water
  • maple syrup or another sweetener to taste
Method
  1. The amounts are rough: use as much sorrel as you like and enough water to cover it.
  2. Blend the sorrel and water until well combined, strain through a fine sieve and sweeten to taste.

Wood sorrel syrup

Ingredients
  • Equal parts wood sorrel
  • sugar and water

The author usually uses 250 ml (1 cup) of each.

Method
  1. Put everything in a saucepan and bring it to a simmer, stirring.
  2. Simmer for 15–30 minutes, strain through muslin (cheesecloth) and pour into sterilised bottles.
  3. Use it in tea, topped up with soda water, over ice cream or fresh strawberries, or in vinaigrettes and marinades.

Wood sorrel cooler

Ingredients
  • About 950 ml (1 US quart) water
  • 125 ml (½ cup) wood sorrel leaves, stems
  • flowers and seed pods
  • 15 ml (1 tbsp) agave nectar or honey
  • a dash of salt
Method
  1. Mix everything in a blender.
  2. If you can, leave it in the fridge overnight before drinking.

Wood sorrel tea

Method
  1. Fresh wood sorrel leaves and boiling water, one part leaves to three parts water.
  2. Put the leaves in a container, pour the boiling water over them and leave to steep for 5–15 minutes.
  3. Strain out the leaves and sweeten to taste if you like.
  4. Drink it hot, or pour it over ice for an iced tea.

Chilled lemony wood sorrel soup

Ingredients
  • 2 cloves garlic, minced
  • olive oil for cooking
  • 2 spring onions, chopped
  • 500 ml (2 cups) vegetable stock
  • 500 ml (2 cups) lightly packed wood sorrel leaves, with the flowers kept back for garnish
  • 3 medium mint leaves
  • 250 ml (1 cup) full-fat coconut milk
  • salt and pepper
Method
  1. Rinse the sorrel well and let it dry, then strip the leaves and flowers from the stems and throw away the bare stalks.
  2. Heat a little olive oil in a small saucepan and sauté the garlic until fragrant, then add the spring onions.
  3. Cover with the stock, bring to the boil and simmer for about 5 minutes, then add the sorrel for about 30 seconds.
  4. Take it off the heat, add the mint and blend smooth with a stick blender (or an ordinary blender).
  5. Stir in the coconut milk and season with salt and pepper.
  6. Refrigerate until well chilled, a couple of hours, and serve garnished with the sorrel flowers.

Spaghetti squash casserole with wood sorrel

Ingredients
  • About 450 g (1 lb) Italian or breakfast sausage
  • 1 medium spaghetti squash
  • several cups of wood sorrel, washed and drained, stems removed (the recipe gives no closer amount)
  • olive oil
  • 1 red onion, halved and sliced
  • 1 egg
  • 125 ml (½ cup) coconut milk or any milk
  • 1 garlic clove, minced
  • 15 ml (1 tbsp) garlic powder
  • 5 ml (1 tsp) tarragon
  • 5 ml (1 tsp) salt
  • 2.5 ml (½ tsp) black pepper
  • 1.25 ml (¼ tsp) ground sage
Method
  1. Heat the oven to 200 °C.
  2. Halve the squash, spoon out the seeds and stringy threads, set the halves cut side up on a baking tray, drizzle with olive oil, season with salt and pepper and bake for 20–25 minutes, until the flesh gives a little when pressed.
  3. Meanwhile cook the sausage and onion in a large pot over medium-high heat.
  4. When the sausage is cooked through, add the garlic and sauté for 2 minutes, then add the sorrel, milk and spices, cover and simmer until the sorrel wilts.
  5. Take the squash out of the oven and scrape out the flesh with a fork (careful, it will be hot).
  6. Combine the mixture with the egg, stirring thoroughly, spread it evenly in a baking dish (or the same pot, if it is ovenproof) and bake for 15–20 minutes, until the top is lightly browned.

Wood sorrel, kūmara and potato gratin

Ingredients
  • Two handfuls of wood sorrel, chopped
  • potato and kūmara, boiled first and sliced
  • cream
Method
  1. Layer the sorrel with the sliced potato and kūmara in a baking dish, pour cream over the top and bake at 180 °C until it starts to brown.

Potato salad with wood sorrel

Method
  1. Chop a big handful of wood sorrel leaves, stalks and flowers and mix them through a potato salad dressed with a cream dressing.

Wild weed spread

Makes about 600 g

The author makes it with chickweed, oxalis and onion weed together; the page does not say which oxalis (written for oxalis generally).

Ingredients
  • 500 ml (2 cups) raw cashews
  • 500 ml hot water
  • 80 ml (⅓ cup) extra-virgin olive oil
  • 1 garlic clove
  • the zest and juice of 1 lemon
  • 30 ml (2 tbsp) apple cider vinegar
  • 60 ml (¼ cup) nutritional yeast (or finely grated parmesan, or cream cheese)
  • 625 ml (2½ cups) wild greens, roughly chopped and tightly packed
  • 15 ml (1 tbsp) chopped thyme
  • dill
  • coriander or parsley
  • 5 ml (1 tsp) salt
  • 2.5 ml (½ tsp) pepper
Method
  1. Soak the cashews in the hot water for an hour, then drain them and throw the water away.
  2. Blend everything together until very smooth.
  3. The mixture is stiff, like cream cheese: a stick blender in a jug works best, and a jug blender may need a little water.
  4. Spread it on toast or crackers, or use it in sandwiches and burgers.

Spoon it into a jar or dish with a lid, pour a thin layer of olive oil over the top so it does not discolour, and keep it covered in the fridge; the author says it keeps for a couple of weeks.

Green goddess dip

Wild greens soup

Serves 4

The author's greens were puha, sow thistle, nasturtium, onion weed, wild fennel, oxalis, wild mustard and wild celery; the page does not say which oxalis (written for oxalis generally).

Ingredients
  • 1 diced onion
  • 1 potato, scrubbed and diced
  • 2 sliced garlic cloves
  • 500 ml (2 cups) roughly chopped wild greens
  • 45 ml (3 tbsp) olive oil
  • 1.3 litres water
  • 5 ml (1 tsp) salt
  • 30 ml (2 tbsp) nutritional yeast (optional)
  • 1.25 ml (¼ tsp) cracked pepper

  2. Fry the onion in the oil for a minute, then add the potato and garlic (the method also adds fennel bulb and celery stalks, which the ingredient list leaves out) and fry for another minute.
  3. Add the water, cover, bring to the boil and simmer until the potato is soft.
  4. Add the greens with the salt and yeast and cook for 3 minutes, until just tender — added at the end, they keep their colour.
  5. Blend until smooth (push it through a sieve if your blender isn't powerful), season with the pepper and more salt if needed, and serve drizzled with extra-virgin olive or hemp seed oil and scattered with edible weed flowers.
Stinging nettle Urtica dioica 15 recipes

Nettle soup

Serves 2

Method
  1. Cook about 225 g potatoes until soft.
  2. Wash and roughly chop 40 nettle tops.
  3. Heat a glug of vegetable oil in a pan and fry 4 crushed garlic cloves for 30 seconds.
  4. Add the nettles, 500 ml vegetable stock and the chopped potatoes, bring to the boil and simmer for about 10 minutes, then blend.

Nettle pesto

Makes 1 jar

Method
  1. Roast 50 g walnuts at 180 °C (fan) for 7–8 minutes, until lightly browned, and let them cool.
  2. Wearing gloves, pick the leaves off the nettles, discard the thick stalks and wash the leaves well.
  3. Blanch 50 g of leaves (weighed after picking) in boiling salted water for 20–30 seconds, until just wilted, lift them into iced water, then squeeze out as much water as you can — they no longer sting.
  4. Blend the walnuts and nettles with 20 g pine nuts, 2 minced garlic cloves, 30 g basil (stalks and all), the juice of 1 lemon, salt and black pepper, drizzling in 125–175 ml extra virgin olive oil until almost smooth.
  5. Keeps up to a week in an airtight jar in the fridge.

Nettle tea

Method
  1. Bring 1 litre of water to a simmer.
  2. Add a large handful of fresh nettle tops (or half a handful of dried) and half a lemon, sliced.
  3. Take it off the heat and let it brew for 5–10 minutes.
  4. Sweeten with a little honey if you like.

Nettle beer

Makes 6 × 750 ml bottles

Method
  1. Sterilise everything first — pots, demijohn, bottles and siphon tube.
  2. Wash and drain 1 kg nettle tops.
  3. Bring 4 litres of water to the boil, add the nettles, boil hard for 15 minutes, then strain into a clean pot.
  4. Stir in 750 g sugar, the juice of 1 lemon and 25 g cream of tartar until dissolved, and leave to cool to room temperature.
  5. Sprinkle a sachet of ale yeast over the surface, cover with muslin or a tea towel and leave overnight.
  6. Next day pour it into a demijohn with a bung and airlock and leave to ferment for up to 6 days.
  7. Siphon into brown bottles, cap them and chill in the fridge for a week before drinking.

Classic stinging nettle soup

Serves 6

Written for common stinging nettle (Urtica dioica and similar nettles) in North America.

Method
  1. Blanch about 225 g nettles in salted boiling water until just wilted, about 30 seconds, cool them in cold water, squeeze dry and finely chop a handful to finish the soup.
  2. Sweat 1 cup diced celery, 2 cups diced leeks, 2 diced shallots and 1 small diced onion in 3 tbsp butter over medium heat for 5 minutes.
  3. Add about 1 litre chicken stock and 2 cups diced potato and simmer until tender, about 15 minutes.
  4. Add the blanched nettles and blend, then stir in the chopped nettles and 120 ml cream and season with salt and white pepper.
  5. Serve with crème fraîche and chopped dill or mint.

Steamed nettles with ricotta

Serves 2

Written for common stinging nettle (Urtica dioica and similar nettles) in North America.

Method
  1. Wash about 225 g young nettle tops and steam them, covered, over boiling water for 2–3 minutes until tender.
  2. Meanwhile warm 3 large crushed garlic cloves in 3 tbsp olive oil over medium heat for at least 10 minutes until browned, then discard the garlic.
  3. Add the steamed nettles to the oil, season with salt, pepper and a pinch of chilli flakes, gently fold in ½ cup ricotta and warm through.
  4. Serve warm or at room temperature with olive oil and lemon wedges.

Nettle spanakopita

Serves 6

Written for stinging nettle in Britain; the recipe does not name the species.

Method
  1. Brown 25 g butter in a large frying pan, add 150 g washed, still-damp nettle leaves and cook for about 6 minutes until wilted; cool, squeeze out the liquid and chop roughly.
  2. Mix with 200 g crumbled feta, 50 g grated parmesan, the zest and juice of a lemon, two-thirds of a beaten egg, grated nutmeg and seasoning.
  3. Heat the oven to 200 °C (180 °C fan).
  4. Lay 3 filo sheets end to end, overlapping by about 5 cm, brush with some of 75 g melted butter, add 3 more sheets and more butter, then a seventh sheet in the middle.
  5. Spoon the filling in a line about 2 cm wide along one long edge, tuck in the ends, roll into a long sausage and coil it into a spiral about 20 cm across in a shallow round pan or on a buttered baking tray.
  6. Brush with the rest of the egg, scatter with 1 tbsp sesame seeds and bake for 40–45 minutes until golden.

Nettle and blue cheese rarebit

Serves 4

Written for stinging nettle in Britain; the recipe does not name the species.

Method
  1. Wilt 200 g nettle leaves in 1 tbsp hot olive oil for 1 minute, cool briefly and chop roughly.
  2. Mix with a 200 ml tub of crème fraîche, 1 tsp wholegrain mustard, half of 140 g crumbled blue cheese, and seasoning.
  3. Toast 4 slices of sourdough on both sides under the grill, spread with the nettle mix, top with the rest of the cheese and grill until golden and bubbling.

Nettle frittata

Serves 6

Written for common stinging nettle (Urtica dioica and similar nettles) in North America.

Method
  1. Steam about 450 g tender nettle tops for at least 5 minutes, until thoroughly wilted, in batches if your pot is small.
  2. Chop them finely and, while still warm, mix in 3 tbsp melted butter, 60 ml warmed cream, ¼ tsp salt, black pepper and a little grated nutmeg, then work in 4 large eggs — the mixture will look stiff.
  3. Heat the grill.
  4. Heat 1 tbsp oil in a 20 cm cast-iron pan until nearly smoking, pack the mixture in and press it down, cover, turn the heat to medium and cook for 5 minutes.
  5. Uncover, scatter over 3 tbsp grated parmesan and grill for 5–10 minutes, until the cheese melts and the centre puffs slightly.
  6. Rest for 10 minutes before cutting into wedges; good with a little tomato sauce.

Nettle and breadcrumb gnocchi

Serves 4 as a starter

Written for common stinging nettle (Urtica dioica and similar nettles) in North America.

Method
  1. Steam about 115 g washed nettles for 2–3 minutes, spread them on a tray to cool, then chop roughly and purée in a food processor with 1 egg, 2 egg yolks, ½ cup grated parmesan, a little nutmeg and a pinch of salt.
  2. Mix in ¾ cup (about 85 g) fine dry breadcrumbs, cover and rest for an hour; the dough should be firm, not sticky — knead in more crumbs if it is.
  3. With floured hands roll it into 1 cm logs, cut them on the diagonal and dust with flour (they can be frozen at this point).
  4. Boil in lightly salted water until they float, then a couple of minutes more, 3–5 minutes in all.
  5. Meanwhile brown 4 tbsp butter in a frying pan, add 1½ tbsp thinly sliced mint with salt and pepper for 20 seconds, then 60 ml dry white wine, and take it off the heat.
  6. Toss the gnocchi in the butter and serve on warm tomato sauce (about 1 cup) with grated parmesan.

Nettle crêpes with goat cheese

Makes 6

Written for common stinging nettle (Urtica dioica and similar nettles) in North America.

Method
  1. Batter: blanch or steam about 85 g young nettles for 60 seconds, squeeze dry and chop, then blend until very smooth with 250 ml milk, 1 egg, 2 tbsp butter, ¼ tsp salt and 1 cup sifted flour; rest for 30 minutes.
  2. Cook thin crêpes, about 60 ml of batter each, in a very hot, lightly oiled 20 cm pan, turning each for just a second once the first side is light golden.
  3. Filling: steam about 115 g young nettles for 2 minutes, cool, squeeze dry and chop medium-fine, then mix with 225 g soft goat cheese or ricotta, 55 g grated parmesan, half a beaten egg, nutmeg, salt and pepper.
  4. Spread a scant ¼ cup over each crêpe, fold in half and in half again, lay them on a lightly greased lined tray, sprinkle with parmesan, dot each with ½ tbsp butter and bake at 190 °C for 15 minutes, until hot through and slightly puffed.
  5. Serve with about 170 g more nettles, steamed for 5 minutes and blotted dry.

Dock, dandelion and nettle puddings

Makes about 35 small puddings or one large one

Written for broad-leaved dock and dandelion, with stinging nettle as a swap for them (pick only the top six or so leaves, before the nettle flowers) and dried nettle in the coating.

Method
  1. Boil 400 g pearl barley until tender, drain and cool.
  2. Hard-boil 4 eggs and chop finely.
  3. Finely chop 2 large leeks and soften them in a little oil.
  4. Blanch about half a supermarket bag of washed dock and dandelion leaves in boiling water for 20 seconds, then plunge into iced water (skip this and the puddings are more bitter and less green); squeeze out every drop of water and chop finely.
  5. Mix the chopped eggs, leeks, barley and greens with 3 raw eggs, and season very well.
  6. Wrap the mixture tightly in a tray covered with cling film, set in a larger tray of hot water, or seal it in a pudding bowl with baking paper, foil and string and stand it in a pan of water reaching at least halfway up.
  7. Steam for 45–60 minutes, in a 160 °C oven or on the hob, until it holds together; if it won't roll into balls, mix in 1–2 more raw eggs and steam longer.
  8. Turn out and serve as one pudding, or roll into small balls while warm and chill them (they freeze individually at this stage).
  9. Roll the chilled balls in flour, then in 1 beaten egg, then in 4 tbsp fine dry breadcrumbs (mixed, if you like, with 4 tbsp dried, ground nettle), and deep-fry for 1–2 minutes until browned; or simply flour and fry them.
  10. Serve warm, with garlic mayonnaise.

Super simple nettle soup

Method
  1. Pick a bag of nettle tops — the top 3 or 4 pairs of leaves, with a little of the upper stem — wearing gloves, and wash them well.
  2. Sweat 2 chopped onions (or leeks) in butter until soft but not browned.
  3. Add half the nettles and let them wilt; the cooking removes the sting.
  4. Add 2 or 3 potatoes cut into cubes under 1 cm and let them sweat a little too — they thicken the soup.
  5. Pour in a little boiling water or vegetable stock (you can add more later); if you used water, add stock powder or a cube.
  6. Dried seaweed or mushroom powder enriches it further.
  7. Simmer gently until the potatoes are soft, then add the rest of the nettles and blitz.
  8. Simmer for another minute or two, and adjust the seasoning and stock.
  9. Serve with chopped hard-boiled egg.

Nettle cordial

Method
  1. Make a syrup of equal parts water and white sugar (for example 50 ml water to 50 g sugar), heating gently and stirring until the sugar dissolves.
  2. Add a load of nettle tops — the top 3 or 4 pairs of leaves and their stem, picked with gloves — at least a cupful (about 250 ml) pressed down, or more.
  3. Dried nettles work too, with a slightly different flavour.
  4. Keep it on the heat until steaming hot, but don't let it boil.
  5. Leave to cool, then strain out the nettles (the author dries them in a dehydrator as nettle sweets).
  6. Stir in small amounts of citric acid or lemon juice until it tastes slightly sharp — or a dash of white wine or cider vinegar — or leave it as a syrup.
  7. It keeps for several weeks in the fridge.

Nettle gimlet

Method
  1. Mix nettle cordial with gin, up to equal parts, to taste, and stir over ice.
  2. Add a squeeze of lime juice or a pinch of citric acid if it is too sweet.
  3. Strain into a chilled coupe, or, less classically, top with a splash of soda or tonic; the author likes a little vermouth in his.

Tree mallow Malva arborea 2 recipes

Fried tree mallow seed heads

Written for tree mallow (Lavatera arborea), on an Irish foraging blog.

Method
  1. Pick young, green seed heads (their texture is better).
  2. Fry them for a few minutes, then season with salt and ground cayenne pepper.
  3. The three leaf-like parts around each head can be taken off before cooking or while eating.

The author also toasts them in a popcorn maker.

Tree mallow and lemon soup (molokhia style)

Written for tree mallow (Malva arborea), improvised by the author on molokhia, the Egyptian soup of jute mallow sometimes made with common mallow; the page gives no quantities.

Method
  1. Purée finely chopped raw tree mallow leaves in a blender with some water.
  2. Fry chopped onion in oil until soft.
  3. Crush garlic cloves, coriander seeds, salt and pepper in a mortar and add them with some vegetable stock and lemon juice, then add the puréed leaves.
  4. Simmer gently for a while, and purée again if it is not smooth enough.
  5. The lemon is meant to offset the mucilage, though the author found tree mallow not very slimy; one of the two tasters found it too lemony.
Turkey tail Trametes versicolor 1 recipe

Turkey tail mushroom broth

Makes 2.5 litres

Written for turkey tail with artist's bracket (Ganoderma applanatum) and shiitake.

Ingredients
  • 2 onions with their skins, roughly chopped
  • 1 leek
  • 1 bulb of garlic, halved across
  • 2 celery sticks (ends and tops too)
  • 2 carrots
  • 1 beetroot (optional)
  • an 8 cm piece of fresh ginger, sliced
  • an 8 cm piece of fresh turmeric, sliced (or 30 ml/2 tbsp powder)
  • 50 g dried or fresh mushrooms — turkey tail, artist's bracket pieces and shiitake
  • 8 g dried seaweed (kelp, kombu or wakame)
  • 2.5 ml (½ tsp) crushed black peppercorns
  • 1 large stick of rosemary or 6 thyme sprigs
  • 4 bay leaves
  • 4 litres water
  • 5–10 ml (1–2 tsp) salt
Method
  1. Put everything in a stockpot, bring to the boil uncovered and simmer gently for 1–1½ hours.
  2. Strain out the solids, keeping the mushrooms to slice and return to the liquid.
  3. Bring it back to the boil and bottle it into jars sterilised in a 150 °C oven; the lids pop as they seal.
  4. Store sealed jars in the pantry and opened ones in the fridge.
  5. Drink it as it is, or use it as a base for miso soup or with cooked noodles or rice.

Watercress Nasturtium officinale 5 recipes

Watercress pesto

Watercress seed mustard

Watercress flower salt

No method written yet.

Watercress and puha salad

Ingredients
Balsamic reduction (makes about 600 ml; you need 45 ml)
  • 1 litre balsamic vinegar
  • 150 g unrefined cane sugar
  • 1 rosemary stalk
  • half a diced red onion (optional)
Method
  1. Raw watercress: use only watercress that is bought or cultivated, or picked from water with no sheep or cattle grazing above it (see this entry's liver fluke warning).
  2. Stir until the sugar dissolves, bring to the boil, then simmer, stirring often, until reduced by half.
  3. Cool, remove the rosemary and bottle it.
  4. Maple walnuts: toast 125 ml (½ cup) walnuts on a lined tray in a 180 °C oven for 10 minutes, then stir through 5 ml (1 tsp) ground cinnamon, 30 ml (2 tbsp) maple syrup and 2.5 ml (½ tsp) salt and return to the oven for 2 minutes; cool.
  5. Salad (serves 4): scrunch half a finely sliced red onion with 45 ml (3 tbsp) balsamic reduction and 5 ml (1 tsp) salt in a large bowl and leave it while you prepare the rest, adding each to the bowl without mixing: 1 orange, peeled and segmented; 1 litre (4 cups) watercress, the stems finely chopped (they can be stringy) and the leaves roughly chopped; 250 ml (1 cup) young puha leaves, roughly chopped; 4 finely sliced radishes; and 250 ml (1 cup) ruruhau, mizuna, alfalfa or broccoli sprouts (optional).
  6. Drizzle with 30 ml (2 tbsp) extra-virgin olive oil, add the walnuts, toss and serve.

Watercress and parmesan tart

Method
  1. For a 30 cm tart tin.
  2. Heat the oven to 180 °C.
  3. For the pastry, grate 125 g very cold butter into 250 g sieved plain flour, 50 g finely grated parmesan, 1 tsp icing sugar, salt and plenty of black pepper.
  4. Rub together lightly, keeping it cool, then add just enough water to form a rough ball (less water, less shrinking).
  5. Chill for 30 minutes, roll out and line the tin, patching where needed.
  6. Prick the base, chill for 20 minutes, then blind-bake with baking paper and beans for 15 minutes; remove them and bake on until lightly toasted.
  7. For the filling, strip 500 g watercress leaves from the fibrous stalks (some stalk is fine — just a coarser tart), keeping some white flowers for garnish.
  8. Melt 20 g butter in a large heavy pan over a medium heat, add the watercress and the juice of 1 orange, and sweat gently for 10 minutes, stirring often.
  9. Blitz it with its juices, then beat it hard with 3 large eggs and 2 extra yolks, 300 ml double cream, all but a handful of 200 g grated parmesan, and plenty of salt and pepper — the more air, the more it rises.
  10. Pour into the case, sprinkle with the reserved parmesan and 25 g pine nuts or chopped hazelnuts, and bake for 20–25 minutes, until bronzed and only slightly wobbly in the middle.
  11. Cool a little and scatter the watercress flowers on top.
White clover Trifolium repens 5 recipes

White clover and strawberry cookies

Ingredients
  • 225 g margarine or butter
  • 150 g brown sugar
  • 120 ml apple sauce
  • 30 ml milk
  • 375 g flour
  • 1 tsp baking soda
  • a loosely packed 120 ml of chopped white clover flowers
  • 150 g diced strawberries
Method
  1. Heat the oven to 180°C.
  2. Cream the butter and sugar, beat in the apple sauce and milk, then work in the flour and baking soda.
  3. Fold the clover and strawberries through last.
  4. Bake 13–15 minutes.

White clover pudding

Ingredients
  • About 500 ml of white clover blossoms (snip the petals from the flower heads and throw out any browned ones)
  • 15 ml (1 tbsp, or one sheet) of unflavoured gelatine
  • 250 ml water
  • 125 ml freshly squeezed orange juice (about two large oranges)
  • 60 ml honey
  • 250 ml cream whipped stiff or 250 ml full-fat Greek yoghurt
  • a pinch of salt
Method
  1. Dissolve the gelatine in 60 ml of water.
  2. In a small saucepan, slowly bring the blossoms, water, orange juice, honey and salt to the boil.
  3. Take it off the heat, stir in the gelatine until it has completely dissolved, and leave it to stand for 10 minutes.
  4. Cover and refrigerate until it begins to set, then fold in the whipped cream or yoghurt, spoon it into a serving bowl and refrigerate until set.

White clover jelly

Ingredients
  • 500 ml–1 litre (2–4 cups) of fresh white clover blossoms, stems removed
  • 1 litre water
  • 60 ml bottled lemon juice (or 1 tsp citric acid)
  • one 50 g box of regular powdered pectin
  • 1.25 kg (5 cups) sugar
Method
  1. Pour the water, just boiled, over the blossoms, push them under and steep for 15–20 minutes.
  2. Strain, pressing the blossoms gently, and top the tea up with water to 1 litre if it is short.
  3. Stir in the lemon juice and bring it to the boil, then whisk in the pectin until it has dissolved and boil hard for a full minute.
  4. Only then add all the sugar at once — added earlier, the jelly will not set — stir until dissolved, return to a full rolling boil for exactly 1 minute, take it off the heat and skim.
  5. Ladle into prepared jars, leaving 5 mm headspace, and process in a boiling water bath for 10 minutes.
  6. Cool undisturbed for 12–24 hours before checking the seals; refrigerate any that did not seal, and refrigerate after opening.
  7. Do not leave out the lemon juice: it helps the set and makes the jelly acidic enough to can.

White clover flour

Method
  1. Pick white clover blossoms; leaves and stems can stay in, but they taste stronger, so separate them out for a milder flour.
  2. Lay the blossoms out to dry on a screen somewhere well ventilated and out of direct sun (sun dries them in a few hours, but can cost them nutrients).
  3. Grind them in batches in a small blade coffee grinder, or all at once in a food processor on high for about 5 minutes.
  4. It shrinks a lot: about 1 litre of blossoms with stems gave 500 ml of blossoms and about 125 ml of flour, so pick roughly 8 times the volume you want.
  5. It will not hold baked goods together on its own: use it for up to a quarter of the flour in a recipe.
  6. It tastes mildly sweet and green, like spring peas, and gives a spongy texture that suits biscuits and soft baking.

White clover berry punch (alcoholic)

Ingredients
  • About 225 g blackberries
  • 225 g blueberries
  • 250 ml fresh white clover flowers
  • 125 ml orange juice
  • 500 ml tequila
Method
  1. Lightly muddle the berries in a jug, add the clover flowers, pour in the tequila and orange juice and stir.
  2. Add plenty of ice, and water to taste.
White-leaved lawyer Rubus schmidelioides 3 recipes

Lawyer berry jelly

Makes 6 jars of 250 ml

Written for Australian native raspberry (Rubus probus), a different bramble; the author does not mention New Zealand lawyers.

Ingredients
  • 1 litre berry juice (fresh or thawed frozen berries put through a cold-press juicer)
  • 300 ml cold-pressed apple juice
  • the juice of 3 lemons
  • 1.3 kg white sugar
  • 10 whole cloves
  • the zest of an orange
Method
  1. Somewhere between a jam and a jelly: the juice is pressed rather than dripped through a bag, so it sets cloudy.
  2. Wash the jars and dry them, open end up, in a 120 °C oven for about 15 minutes, leaving them in the warm oven until needed; boil the lids for 5 minutes and let them air-dry.
  3. Put the berry, apple and lemon juices and the sugar in a jam pan, dissolve the sugar over low heat, then bring to the boil.
  4. Add the cloves and orange zest, skim off foam as it rises, and boil to setting point — about 30 minutes for the author, giving a soft set.
  5. Ladle the hot jelly into the hot jars and seal.

Lawyer berry vinegar

Written for Australian native raspberry (Rubus probus), a different bramble; the author does not mention New Zealand lawyers.

Ingredients
  • 400 g berries
  • 500 ml white wine vinegar
  • 100 g white sugar
Method
  1. Warm the vinegar with the sugar until it has dissolved.
  2. Put the berries in a large clean jar or a non-reactive bowl, pour the warm vinegar over them and leave overnight.
  3. Strain gently through a fine sieve or muslin and pour into a sterilised bottle or jar.

The author uses it to quick-pickle wafer-thin slices of raw beetroot (3 tablespoons of vinegar and ¼ teaspoon of sea salt for 2 small beetroot, soaked at least 15 minutes) served with goat's cheese.

Lady in Red cocktail

Written for Australian native raspberry (Rubus probus), a different bramble; the author does not mention New Zealand lawyers.

Method
  1. For one drink.
  2. Muddle a big handful of berries (fresh or frozen) in a cocktail shaker, add 60 ml tequila blanco, 30 ml Cointreau, 60 ml fresh lime juice and 60 ml sugar syrup, shake hard over ice and pour.
  3. Strain it if you don't want the seeds.
