Wild Whangaroa pesto

Makes about 350 g

The author's greens, picked from roadsides around Whangaroa Harbour, were nasturtium, kawakawa, fennel, samphire, fat hen, wild mustard, cleavers and clover. The author warns that New Zealand roadsides are still sprayed with glyphosate, so pick with care.

Ingredients
  • 1 garlic clove (or 4 peeled onion weed bulbs)
  • 60 ml (¼ cup) macadamia nuts and 60 ml (¼ cup) grated parmesan
  • dairy or plant-based — or 125 ml (½ cup) macadamias and no parmesan — 750 ml (3 cups) roughly chopped wild greens
  • the zest and juice of 1 lemon
  • 125 ml extra-virgin olive oil
  • salt and pepper
Method
  1. Blend in a food processor to the texture you like.
  2. Serve on crackers or toast, as a side, or stirred through soup, pasta or rice.

Wild greens soup

Serves 4

The author's greens were puha, sow thistle, nasturtium, onion weed, wild fennel, oxalis, wild mustard and wild celery.

Ingredients
  • 1 diced onion
  • 1 potato, scrubbed and diced
  • 2 sliced garlic cloves
  • 500 ml (2 cups) roughly chopped wild greens
  • 45 ml (3 tbsp) olive oil
  • 1.3 litres water
  • 5 ml (1 tsp) salt
  • 30 ml (2 tbsp) nutritional yeast (optional)
  • 1.25 ml (¼ tsp) cracked pepper
Method
  1. Cut the vegetables and greens to similar sizes.
  2. Fry the onion in the oil for a minute, then add the potato and garlic (the method also adds fennel bulb and celery stalks, which the ingredient list leaves out) and fry for another minute.
  3. Add the water, cover, bring to the boil and simmer until the potato is soft.
  4. Add the greens with the salt and yeast and cook for 3 minutes, until just tender — added at the end, they keep their colour.
  5. Blend until smooth (push it through a sieve if your blender isn't powerful), season with the pepper and more salt if needed, and serve drizzled with extra-virgin olive or hemp seed oil and scattered with edible weed flowers.
Wild garlic Allium vineale 5 recipes

Wild garlic hummus

Written for wild garlic (Allium vineale).

Method
  1. Drain and rinse a 425 g tin of chickpeas and put them in a food processor with about 20 large wild garlic stems, rinsed and with any dried stems removed (more or fewer to taste).
  2. Add 2 tbsp lemon juice, 1 tbsp tahini, 125 ml olive oil and 1 tbsp nutritional yeast, and process until completely smooth.
  3. Add salt, taste, and adjust any of the other ingredients.
  4. Top with a little more olive oil and serve straight away, or chill.

The author keeps the tahini light so it does not swamp the garlic, which is milder than a garlic bulb.

Broccoli with wild garlic and lemon

Written for wild garlic (Allium vineale), whose bulbs are each about the size of one clove of garlic.

Method
  1. Cut 1.4 kg of broccoli into florets and simmer, covered, in 80–125 ml of water until tender, 10–15 minutes.
  2. Meanwhile peel and finely chop 8 wild garlic bulbs, and mix them with 60 ml lemon juice (from 2–4 lemons), 125 ml olive oil, and salt and pepper to taste.
  3. Toss the cooked broccoli in the dressing.

Wild garlic pesto

Written for wild garlic (Allium vineale) in Gers, France.

Method
  1. Rinse a bunch of stems in water with a little vinegar, then in clean water.
  2. Blend with 100 g pine nuts, almonds or hazelnuts, the juice of a lemon, and salt and pepper, pouring in olive oil in a thin stream until it is a soft paste.
  3. Keep in a jar in the fridge.
  4. Good with fish, pasta or on toast.

Potato and wild garlic galettes

Serves 4

Written for wild garlic (Allium vineale).

Method
  1. Peel and finely grate 4 potatoes.
  2. Rinse a bunch of wild garlic stems in water with a little vinegar, then in clean water, and snip them like chives.
  3. Mix the potato, the wild garlic and 1 egg, and season.
  4. Heat a film of oil in a frying pan, drop in small spoonfuls and press them down lightly.
  5. Cook about 15 minutes, turning halfway, until golden and crisp, then drain on paper towel.
  6. A spoonful of flour makes them softer.

Wild garlic soup

From a French page on wild garlic (Allium vineale).

Method
  1. Wash and cut 400 g of wild garlic and cook it in 2 tbsp olive oil, then stir in 100 g flour to thicken.
  2. Add 2 litres of water with a stock cube dissolved in it, and salt.
  3. Boil for 15 minutes.
  4. Serve with cream, croutons or cheese.
Wild radish Raphanus raphanistrum 1 recipe

Roast wild radish leaves with yoghurt

Written for sea radish (Raphanus raphanistrum subsp. maritimus), the coastal form of wild radish; the author borrowed a classic Turkish way with charlock.

Method
  1. Roast the leaves with onion and garlic in plenty of olive oil, then mix with yoghurt — stop cooking at this point or it will split.

The author served it in toasted pitta with seared pheasant breast.

Wisteria Wisteria sinensis 1 recipe

Wisteria fizzy drink

Makes 5 litres

Ingredients
  • 2–2.5 litres (8–10 cups) wisteria flowers, stripped from the stalks
  • 750 ml (3 cups) golden sugar
  • 4 lemons
  • 2 sliced
  • the juice of the other 2
  • 5 litres water
Method
  1. Not yet checked against this entry: Wikipedia says every part of the plant contains a toxin, and the author uses the flowers only, saying the rest of the plant is toxic.
  2. The page does not say which wisteria; Chinese wisteria is the one recorded wild in New Zealand.
  3. Stir the sugar, water and lemon in a large jar until the sugar dissolves, add the flowers and push them under, leaving room in the jar because the mixture rises and falls as it ferments.
  4. Cover with muslin held by a rubber band, and date it.
  5. Stir hard every day for 3–4 days, until the flowers rise and bubbles appear when you stir.
  6. Strain through muslin into flip-top bottles, leave at room temperature for 1–2 days, checking the fizz, then keep it in the fridge and serve cold.
